# Unit 2

## Text A · Say Yes · Tobias Wolff

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

Our first impression of this text may be that this is a story about the relationship between husband and wife. If we look deeper, however, we find that it can also be taken as a discussion of subtle expressions of racist feelings that are found in many ordinary people.

Racism is one of the most irrational and dangerous sentiments in our world. History has recorded countless stories of human cruelty and suffering caused by racism. We can never forget how 6 million innocent people were slaughtered in German concentration camps simply because they were Jews; we cannot forget what the Japanese did to the Chinese during the Second World War because they were made to believe that they were the superior race; we cannot forget how hundreds of thousands of people were brutally murdered in a matter of a few months in Rwanda, a country of only 3 million people. Indeed, even as mankind enters the 21st century, racism is still far from being a thing of the past. If we look around we can find many countries and regions where people are being tormented by the worst kind of racism.

Racism has been called the national curse of the United States ever since the black slaves were first brought into this continent. We all know the sufferings of the blacks as described in Uncle Tom's Cabin; we also know about the Ku Klux Klan and the Jim Crow laws. But since the Civil Rights movement of the 1960s, a lot of changes have taken place, and the racial relationship has dramatically improved so much that the country even has a black president in the White House. However, cultural heritage tends to linger on. It is not uncommon for people to enter the 21st century but still think and act like they were still in the Middle Ages. So racism is still very much alive, even in the United States. The only thing is that it now may take more subtle forms. The present article deals precisely with this subtle manifestation of racial prejudice. The little argument between this white couple reveals a very disturbing truth, that is, racism is by no means only the problem of a few terrible monsters; in a certain sense, we are also often guilty of the crime, and unless all of us ordinary people open our eyes to this fact and determine to say no, it is bound to continue to plague the world.

This story can also be regarded as a discussion of husband-wife relationship. It's interesting that the debate takes place between a white couple. And the fact that they argue over the racist problem and not about problems that husband and wife usually quarrel about may reflect the general social background at the time when racism was a problem of public concern in the United States. What is the writer trying to tell us here? Maybe, he is telling us that women are usually more sensitive and observant, or more serious on matters of moral principles. Maybe he is telling us husband and wife can be married for many years without really knowing each other's innermost thoughts; they may remain strangers.

#### 2 Structure

I. The issue that started the family quarrel (paras. 1–5)

1. The couple were chatting while washing dishes when the husband said something about interracial marriages, which he thought on the whole were a bad idea.
2. The wife considered his point of view ridiculous, which made the husband feel hurt because his wife was clearly implying that he was a racist.

II. The arguments of the husband in his self-defence (paras. 6–18)

1. He likes blacks and their language and gets along well with them.
2. Different races have different cultures, which makes marriage difficult, but it has nothing to do with racism.
3. Statistics show many interracial marriages finally break up.

III. The husband's failure to please his wife and divert her attention from this unpleasant argument (paras. 19–24)

The wife cut her finger, the husband rushed to help stop her bleeding and hoped she would show appreciation and reward him by ending this unpleasant discussion.

IV. The answer (paras. 25–44)

1. The wife insisted on her husband's immediate answer as to whether he would marry her if she were black, single and they fell in love.
2. The husband was forced to give his honest answer that he wouldn't, which confirmed his wife's opinion that he was a racist, and made her furious.

V. The anger (paras. 45–48)

1. The husband thought that his wife was not behaving in a reasonable and logical way.
2. He felt deeply hurt at first by his wife's attitude.
3. In the end he was sorry for what had happened.

VI. The stranger (paras. 49–57)

1. The husband decided to forgive her and make up with her by his changing answer to her question, but this did not improve the situation.
2. His wife did not return to bed, making him feel she was a stranger once again like she had been the night they were married.

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. The moral of the story is everybody's guess. Let students decide what may be the central idea.

2. If students are in favor of taking the story as a revelation of subtle expressions of racial prejudice, they may find Text B has a very similar story. Encourage them to read that text.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text A

1. They are doing the dishes, his wife washing while he dried. (para. 1)

To do the dishes means to wash the dishes. Pay attention to these verb + noun collocations.

More examples from the text:
to call sb.'s name; to mop the floor; to dry the dishes; to wipe the table; to demonstrate the difference; to take a breath; to win the argument; to spray the silverware; to pinch one's brows; to bite one's lower lip

Students should be encouraged to list such collocations from each text.
Refresh students' memory of the use of adjectives as verbs
e.g. to empty one's pockets; to wet one's lips; to narrow the gap; to dirty one's hands; to clear the street; to clean the room; to long for the vacation; to busy oneself with sth.; to thin out the corn; to calm down; to cool down; to warm one's hands over the fire; to smooth things over; to dry one's tears

2. Unlike most men he knew, he really pitched in on the housework. (para. 1)

Do not mix up "unlike" and "dislike". The former is the opposite of the preposition "like" while the latter is the opposite of the verb or noun of "like".

More examples:
Unlike many young people of his background, he hates the attention he is given because of his father.
Unlike the United States, Japan largely relies on bank loans for its capital needs.
Unlike many other government officials, he is neither corrupt nor corruptible.

pitch in: to offer help and support
housework: domestic duties such as cleaning and cooking (not to be confused with "homework")

3. A few months earlier he'd overheard a friend of his wife's congratulating her on having such a considerate husband. (para. 1)

Review the verb pattern: verb + (pro)noun + doing sth.

More examples:
I overheard the young couple quarreling last night.
She overheard the men discussing their plan to rob a bank.

congratulate sb. on doing sth.: give sb. one's good wishes when sth. special or pleasant has happened to them
e.g. They all congratulated me on winning the first prize.
When I grew older, I realized that I really should have congratulated myself on having such a poor but loving father.

considerate: thoughtful, kind, careful not to hurt or cause inconvenience to others

4. ... somehow got onto the subject of whether white people should marry black people. (para. 2)

somehow: in some way (I don't remember how it happened)
e.g. That day somehow I didn't feel like eating.
We went to Wangfujing together the other day and somehow I couldn't find him anywhere.

get on(to): to begin to discuss
whether white people should marry black people: whether it is advisable or a good idea for white people to marry black people. "Should" here does not mean "must".

5. He said that all things considered, he thought it was a bad idea. (para. 2)

all things considered: when one considers every aspect of the problem or situation
e.g. All things considered, he is doing pretty well at school. (Under these condition she is doing quite well.)
All things considered, the reform is a success. (There are many problems of course.)

6. Sometimes his wife got this look where she pinched her brows together and bit her lower lip. (para. 4)

"Where" here is used as a relative adverb introducing an attributive clause which modifies the word "look".

Compare:
He went to a school, which was very far from where he lived.
He went to school where he learned many interesting things.
I saw a movie last night which was real junk.
I saw a movie last night where there was a lot of violence.

pinch her brows together: to contract or squeeze her eyebrows; to frown

7. ... and I've worked with blacks and we've always gotten along just fine.(para.6)

Notice how the man tried to show that he was not a racist. The man of course was by no means the worst kind of racist. In fact, he would be considered quite liberal. But it is precisely the fact that even decent people are deeply influenced that shows the seriousness of the matter.

gotten: past participle of "get" in American English

8. I don't need you coming along now and implying that I'm a racist. (para. 6)

Here, "you coming along..." is the complex object of the verb "need".

9. I just don't see what's wrong with a white person marrying a black person, that's all. (para. 7)

Here's another example of a complex object (a white person marrying...) to a preposition (with).

More examples:
What's wrong with peasants coming to big cities to look for jobs?
What's wrong with people demanding to know how their taxes are being spent?

10. They don't come from the same culture. (para. 8)

As racial discrimination based on color differences is now totally discredited, today many people try to justify their prejudices by cultural differences.

11. That's okay with me. I like hearing them talk. (para. 8)

That's okay with me: I have no objection to that.

12. Like you know me? (para. 11)

In informal English, "like" is often used as a conjunction (= as or as if).

More examples:
Like I said, I can't get there by 5 p.m.
Do you speak to children like you speak to adults?

13. Oh boy, he thought. (para. 14)

"Oh boy" is used often in American English when someone is slightly annoyed. The man was annoyed because his wife was determined to pursue the argument.

14. Don't take my word for it. Look at the statistics. (para. 14)

You don't have to accept what I say as true. Look at the statistics.

take sb.'s word for it: (spoken) used to say that sb. should accept what you say as true
e.g. Take my word for it. This boy is going to have a bright future.
You can take my word for it. I will never let you down.

15. She was piling dishes on the drain-board at a terrific rate. Many of them were still greasy... (para. 15)

at a terrific rate: at a great speed. "Rate" means "speed" as also in "growth rate".
greasy: covered with grease (animal fat softened by cooking or heating) as in
~ food, ~ dishes, ~ skin, ~ hair, ~ fingers

16. "Yes, different," he snapped, angry with her for resorting to this trick of repeating his words so that they sounded hypothetical. (para. 18)

snap: to say sth. quickly and angrily
resort to this trick: to adopt this method
"Trick" is often sth. you do to deceive or outwit.

17. ... and threw all the silverware back into the sink. (para. 18)

"Ware" means manufactured goods. It is often used to form compound words such as:
silverware, ironware, software, hardware, glassware, earthenware, ovenware

Compare: warehouse

18. She stared down at it, her lips pressed tight together, then plunged her hands under the surface. (para. 19)

She stared down at it, with her lips pressed tight...
"Pressed" here is past participle and "tight" modifies "lips".

plunge: to cause sth. to fall suddenly and with force
e.g. The fish plunged deep into the water.
This scandal plunged the government into a serious crisis.

19. He ran upstairs to the bathroom and rummaged in the medicine chest for alcohol, cotton, and a Band-Aid. (para. 20)

rummage: to search for sth. by moving things around in a careless way
It is usually used in the following patterns: to rummage in a place for sth.; to rummage through a place for sth.; to rummage around

20. ... (he) dabbed at her thumb with the cotton. (para. 20)

dab: to touch sth. lightly, usually several times
It is usually used in the following patterns: to dab at a certain place (e.g. a bleeding lip) with sth.; to dab sth. on/onto somewhere

21. He hoped that she appreciated how quickly he had come to her aid. (para. 21)

come to her aid: to come to help her
Also: to come to our rescue, to come to rescue us

22. He'd acted out of concern for her, he thought that it would be a nice gesture on her part not to start up that conversation again ... (para. 21)

The man had shown concern for his wife, and he hoped that his wife would show her concern in return by not continuing this unpleasant conversation.

Refresh students' memory of "out of concern".
out of: because of
e.g. I just followed the crowd out of curiosity.
The job was not very well paid. She took it just out of interest.
I really have no excuse. I did it out of carelessness and stupidity.

23. "So," she said, "you wouldn't have married me if I'd been black." (para. 25)

Help the students review this pattern by asking them to respond to questions like the following:
He knew there was a burglar in the house, but he was alone and unarmed. Now what would you have done if you had been in that situation?
He discovered a bag of gold. Do you know what he did with it? What would you have done if you had found so much money?
If you had been in Tangshan that night, what would you have done the moment you realized that it was an earthquake?

24. "For Christ's sake, Ann!" (para. 26)

"For Christ's sake" is often used to express annoyance, like "Oh boy". The man was really upset that his wife wouldn't stop.

25. The only black girl I ever knew was my partner in the debating club. (para. 28)

Students should remember that "debating" here is a gerund used as the attributive of "club".

More examples:
rinsing nozzle, reading materials; teaching methods; eating habits; running shoes; hiding place; sewing machine

Note: Sometimes such kind of noun phrases become compound words (with or without a hyphen): sitting-room, sleeping-pills, dining-car.
There is a subtle difference between these noun phrases and phrases modified by present participles. "A sleeping beauty" for example is a beauty that is sleeping. It is different from "sleeping-pill" because the pill is not sleeping.

26. "Then you probably would have been going out with a black guy." He picked up the rinsing nozzle and sprayed the silverware. (para. 30)

More examples for the pattern "would have been doing sth." in the subjunctive mood:
If it had not been for his help, my father would still have been begging in the street.
If he had not hit upon this idea, he would still have been living on government relief.

27. "Let's say I am black and unattached," she said ... (para. 31)

let's say: let us suppose
unattached: still single or unmarried; not engaged; without a regular companion

28. ... this is stupid. (para. 32)

"This" here refers to the whole argument.

29. There was no possible way of arguing with the fact that she would not be herself if she were black. (para. 32)

What follows the word "fact" is an appositive clause which explains what the "fact" is, rather than modifying it like in attributive clauses. Nouns that are often used to introduce such appositive clauses are: reason, fact, idea, view, decision, etc.

Ask students to complete the following sentences:
What do you think of the idea that________________?
How do you explain the fact that________________?
What is the reason (why)________________?
Do you accept the view that________________?

30. He had won the argument but he still felt cornered. (para. 34)

corner: to get a person into a position from which it is hard to escape. Here, it means "to put a person into a difficult situation".

31. "Let's not move too fast on this," he said. (para. 40)

Don't let us rush to a decision. I have to think carefully before I give you an answer.

32. "Since you put it that way—" (para. 42)

Since you make it a yes or no question—/ Since you ask your question so bluntly—

33. She said "Thank you," and walked from the kitchen into the living room. (para. 45)

Thank you: Thank you for telling me the truth. This polite expression actually shows her mounting anger.

34. ... but she didn't snap through the pages the way he would have done. (para. 45)

snap through the pages: to turn the pages suddenly, making short and sharp noises

Ask students to observe the following uses of the word "snap":
She snapped two twigs off a tree and used them as chopsticks.
She snapped her briefcase shut and left angrily.
I only told him that a certain Miss Li had called and he snapped at me for no reason.
He only stayed long enough to snap a few pictures.
At first I treated it as a joke, but then I suddenly snapped.

35. While he was at it, he decided, he might as well mop the floor. (para. 47)

Since he was doing the dishes, he might as well mop the floor.
be at it: to be in a continuous state or activity
e.g. be at war, be at work, be at peace

36. When he was done the kitchen looked new, the way it looked when they were first shown the house. (para. 47)

When he was done: When he had finished
e.g. When you are done with the book, pass it on to Chen.
In the relative clauses following such nouns as "way", "reason", "time", "day", the relative adverbs are usually omitted,
e.g. I don't like the way (how) he talks to people.
I don't know the reason (why) she turned down that offer.
Do you remember the time (when) we went to Huashan together?
The day (when) I went to school, I was both excited and scared.

37. The night was clear and he could see a few stars to the west, where the lights of the town didn't blur them out. (para. 48)

The night was clear: The night was starry, no clouds, no mist, no smoke.

Ask students to translate the following phrases with the word "clear":
clear water, clear air, clear meanings, clear situation, clear case, clear sky, clear picture, clear head, clear example, clear view, clear conscience

Compare:
The street is clean. (no garbage; not dirty)
The street is clear. (no traffic; not blocked)

38. In another thirty years or so they would both be dead. What would all that stuff matter then? (para. 48)

In another thirty years or so they would both be dead. So what's the sense of arguing about these problems?

The use of the word "another" here seems to suggest that they must have been married for about thirty years. Therefore in another thirty years they would both be in their late eighties, assuming they were married in their late twenties. This shows that theirs obviously was not one of those little harmless arguments between a young husband and wife. It concerned a matter of principle to Ann, who must have been a very serious idealistic person.

39. He thought of the years they had spent together, and how close they were, and how well they knew each other, and his throat tightened so that he could hardly breathe. (para. 48)

Call students' attention to the uses of the three "and"s again. (The first two mean "in addition" whereas the last one means "as a result".)

The man seemed to be happy with the marriage, and was ready to humor his wife.

40. I'll make it up to you. (para. 49)

make it up to sb.: to do sth. good for sb. because you feel responsible for sth. bad that happened between you
e.g. I can't take you to the park today, son. I'm sorry, but I will make it up to you next week when I am not so busy.

Compare this to "make it up with sb.", which is different in meaning:
I had a little quarrel with my sister last night, but I made it up with her soon. (or: ... we soon made it up)

41. ...he knew that he had to come up with the right answer. (para. 51)

...he knew that he had to give the right answer. It is clear that the man was merely trying to make peace with his wife. It does not mean that he has realized his mistake.

come up with: to think of (an idea, plan, suggestion or reply)

42. "We'll see," she said. (para. 52)

This expression is used in spoken English when you do not want to make a decision right now. Ann said this to show that she refused to be convinced this time.

43. Finally he heard the bathroom door open and close. (para. 53)

We don't know what Ann was doing. One thing was clear though. She found it difficult to share the same bed with someone who had such repulsive views.

44. "All right," he said. He lay there, but nothing happened. (para. 57)

When Ann told her husband to turn off the light, she made him feel that she had forgiven him and would soon join him. But "nothing happened". She didn't come to bed.

45. His heart pounded the way it had on their first night together, the way it still did when he woke at a noise in the darkness and waited to hear it again—the sound of someone moving through the house, a stranger. (para. 57)

Now we know that actually in all these years they were married, the man had also often felt that he did not really know his wife; she was still a "stranger" to him.

### III Translation of Text A

回答"是"
托拜厄斯·沃尔夫

夫妻俩在一起洗碗，妻子洗，丈夫擦干。与他认识的大多数男人不同，他确实在努力帮着做家务。几个月前，他偶然听到妻子的一个朋友恭喜她有这么一个体贴入微的丈夫。

他们谈论着各种各样的的话题，不知怎么地就聊到白人该不该和黑人结婚这件事上了。丈夫说，考虑到各种因素，他认为这是一个糟糕的想法。

"为什么？"妻子问道。

有时候，妻子会带着这种表情，紧皱眉头，咬着下嘴唇。当他看到她面带这种表情时，他知道他该赶紧闭嘴不说话，可他从未做到过。实际上，这反倒使他觉得更多。她现在已经露出了这种表情。

"为什么？"她又问道。她站在那里，手里拿着一只碗停在水面上没动。

"听着，"他说，"我和黑人一起上学，和他们一起工作过，我们一直相处得不错。我不需要你现在来暗示我是一个种族主义者。"

"我没暗示什么，"她说道，"我只是不明白白人和黑人结婚有什么不对，仅此而已。"

"他们来自不同的文化背景，黑人甚至有自己的语言。这倒没什么，我喜欢听他们说话。"

"但是你不会想要和一个黑人结婚，不是吗？"她问。

"婚姻是另外一回事。来自他们文化背景的人和来自我们文化背景的人永远不可能真正互相了解。"

"就像你了解我一样吗？"妻子问道。

## （续）

"对。就像你了解我那样。"

"但是，如果他们是相爱的呢？"她说道。

唉，上帝！他感到无奈。他说，"别只听听我，看看那些数据吧，大多数的婚姻都长不了。"

"数据？"她快速地把盘子堆放在晾板上，很多还沾着油渍，叉子尖之间还残存有食物的残渣。"好吧，"她说，"那么外国人呢？我想你对两个外国人之间的婚姻也是同样的看法吧。"

"对，"他说，"我的确这么认为的。你怎么能够了解一个有着完全不同背景的人呢？"

"不同背景，"妻子说道，"不像我们，来自相同的背景。"

"对，不同背景，"他高声说道。他对妻子很生气，因为她总是重复他的话，这把戏让他听起来很虚伪。"这些还没洗干净呢，"他说着又把所有的碗碟都扔回到洗碗池里。

她低下头看着，紧紧地抿着双唇，然后把双手伸进池子里。"阿呀！"她叫着向后退了一步。她托住右手腕，把手举了起来，右手的大拇指在滴血。

"待在那儿别动，"他说。他跑到楼上的卫生间，从药箱里翻出酒精、棉花和一片"邦迪"创可贴。当他下楼时，看见她靠着冰箱，闭着眼睛，手仍然举着。他拉过她的手，用棉球轻轻擦拭她的拇指，血早就止住了。他挤了一下拇指看看伤口到底有多深。

"伤口很浅，"他说，"明天你甚至都看不出伤口在哪儿了。"他希望她会感激他这么快就帮她处理好了伤口。他是出自对她的关心才这么做的，希望她不要再谈论刚才的话题，对她来说那也是个示好的姿态，因为你已经厌倦了这种争论。

"我会把剩下的洗完，收拾利索，"他说道，"你去休息吧。"

"没事，"她说，"我来擦干。"

他又开始洗起来。

"这么说，假如我是黑人，你是不会娶我的了。"

"上帝啊，你还有完没完，安妮！"

"这是你说的，不是吗？"

"不是，我没有那样说。这个问题荒谬得很，如果你是黑人，我们也许永远不会相遇。你会有你的朋友圈子，而我会我的朋友圈子。我认识的唯一一个黑人女孩是我在辩论俱乐部里的搭档。"

"但假如我们相遇了，而且我是黑人呢？"

"要是那样的话，你很可能正跟一个黑人小伙子在一起呢。"他拿起喷头，冲洗着碗碟。

"我们假设一下，我是黑人，又是单身，"她说道，"我们遇见了，而且相爱了。"

他瞥了她一眼。她正注视着他，两眼闪闪发亮。"你听我说，"他用理智的语气说道，"这很荒谬，如果你是黑人，你就不会是你了。"当他说到这儿时，他觉得自己完全正确。这是无可争辩的事实：假如她是黑人，那么她就不是她自己了。所以他又说了一遍："如果你是黑人，你就不会是你了。"

"我知道，"她说，"但我们来假设一下。"

他深吸了一口气。虽然他已经在赢了这场争论，但他仍感觉被逼得无法脱身。"还假设什么呢？"他问道。

"假设我是黑人，但我还是我，而且我们相爱了。你会娶我吗？"

他想了想。

"嗯？"她问道，眼睛更亮了，"你会娶我吗？"

"我正在想呢，"他回答道。

"我看得出来，你是不会娶我的。"

"我们别这么快就下结论，"他说，"有许多情况需要考虑。我们不能做让自己后悔一辈子的事。"

"不要再考虑了。会还是不会？"

"既然你这么说——"

"会还是不会？"

"我的上帝，安妮。好吧。我不会娶你。"

她说了句"谢谢"，然后从厨房走进了客厅。过了一会儿，他听到她翻杂志的声音。他知道她正在生气，根本无法看进去，但她没有像他那样把书弄得哗啦作响。她慢慢地翻看着，仿佛是在研究其中的每个字。她在表示对他的冷漠，他知道这达到了她想要的效果，这伤了他的心。

他别无选择，只好对她表现出满不在乎的样子。他一声不响地洗好剩下的碗碟，然后把它们擦干放好，接着他还擦净了台面和灶台，还把刚才被滴下来的血弄脏的那块地擦干净了。

这时他心里想，擦洗到了这里，干脆把地板也给拖了算了。他做完这些事情后，厨房看上去焕然一新，就像他们第一次来看房时那样。

他拿起垃圾桶走到屋外。夜色清明，他看见西边的天空有几颗星星在闪烁，镇上的灯光并没有掩住它们的光芒。在埃尔米诺大街上，车辆不多，但也始终不断，像一条静静流淌的河流。他感到惭愧，他和妻子争吵。再过那么30年，他和妻子都会老死，而到那时，今晚争吵的事情又有什么意义呢？他想起了与她共同度过的岁月，他们是那么的亲密，那么的知心。他喉咙哽咽了，几乎喘不上气来。热血涌上胸口。他在那里停留了片刻，那些美好的情感让他非常享受。然后他从地上捡起垃圾桶，从后门回家了。

他回到屋里，屋内一片漆黑。妻子在卫生间里，他站在门外，叫她的名字。"安妮，非常抱歉，"他说，"我会弥补我的过错，我保证。"

"怎么弥补？"她说道。

他没想到她会这么说。但是从她的话音中他听到了一种声音，那种直接确定的语气是他从前从未听过的，他知道他必须做出正确的回答。他倚在门上低声对她说："我会娶你。"

"再说吧，"她回答，"你去睡吧，我一会儿就出来。"

他脱了衣服，钻进被子里。终于，他听到卫生间的门开了，接着又关上。"关灯，"声音从门厅过道传来。

"什么？"

"把灯关了。"

他伸手拉了一下床头灯的链绳，屋内黑了下来。"好了，"他说。他躺在床上，但什么都没有发生。

"好了，"他又说了一遍。接着他听到穿过房间的脚步声。他坐了起来，但是什么都看不见。房间里一片寂静。他的心怦怦直跳，那种心跳的感觉就如同他们度过的第一个夜晚一样，又如同他曾经在黑夜中被声呐惊醒，等着再次听到它一样——有人走过房间的声音，一个陌生人。

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

1. critical: critic, critically, criticize, criticism
2. consider: considerate, inconsiderate, considerable, considerably, consideration, considering

2.

1. Verb phrases
argue with sb./sth., blur out, come to sb.'s aid, come up with sth., congratulate sb. on sth., dab at, finish up, glance over at sb./sth., get along, get onto the subject, get into a fight, lean against some place, make it up to sb., pile dishes on, pitch in, put sth. away, reach over, snap through the pages, stare down at sb./sth.
2. Prepositional phrases
on one's part, out of (concern/pity/sympathy), be angry with sb., be okay with sb., be at it, be tired of sth., be wrong with sth.
3. Other phrases
take sb.'s word for it (=take sb. at his/her word), all things considered, for Christ's sake

3.

do/wash/dry the dishes, mop the floor, pinch one's brows together, press one's lips, repeat one's words, squeeze sb.'s hand, take a breath, take a reasonable tone, win the argument

4.

1. ... all things considered, he thought it was a bad idea. (para. 2)
absolute structure
2. I don't need you coming along now and implying that ... (para. 6)
gerund with its logical subject used as the object of a verb or preposition
3. I just don't see what's wrong with a white person marrying a black person ... (para. 7)
gerund as the post modifier
4. ... she didn't snap through the pages the way he would have done. (para. 45)
the way + relative clause

#### Reading Comprehension

1.

| Part | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Part I (paras. 1–5) | The issue that started the family quarrel<br>1. The couple were chatting while washing dishes when the husband said something about interracial marriages, which he thought on the whole were a bad idea.<br>2. The wife considered his point of view ridiculous, which made the husband feel hurt because his wife was clearly implying that he was a(n) racist. |
| Part II (paras. 6–18) | The arguments of the husband in his self-defence<br>1. He likes blacks and their language and gets along well with them.<br>2. Different races have different cultures, which makes marriage difficult, but it has nothing to do with racism.<br>3. Statistics show many interracial marriages finally break up. |
| Part III (paras. 19–24) | The husband's failure to please his wife and divert her attention from this unpleasant argument<br>The wife cut her finger, the husband rushed to help stop her bleeding and hoped she would show appreciation and reward him by ending this unpleasant discussion. |
| Part IV (paras. 25–44) | The answer<br>1. The wife insisted on her husband's immediate answer as to whether he would marry her if she were black, single and they fell in love.<br>2. The husband was forced to give his honest answer that he wouldn't, which confirmed his wife's opinion that he was a racist, and made her furious. |
| Part V (paras. 45–48) | The anger<br>1. The husband thought that his wife was not behaving in a reasonable and logical way.<br>2. He felt deeply hurt at first by his wife's attitude.<br>3. In the end he was sorry for what had happened. |
| Part VI (paras. 49–57) | The stranger<br>1. The husband decided to forgive her and make up with her by his changing answer to her question, but this did not improve the situation.<br>2. His wife did not return to bed, making him feel she was a stranger once again like she had been the night they were married. |

2.

1. Oh goodness, he thought. (He knew that he had touched upon a subject that would start off one of those unpleasant arguments with his wife.) He said, "You don't have to believe my theory. Just look at the statistics (of how many of those marriages end in failure)."
2. "Let's suppose I were black and not married or engaged, still single," she said ...
3. "Well? Go on and answer my question," she said more eagerly and excitedly ... "Will you marry me? You won't, I know."
4. Since he was wiping the counters and mopping the blood stain on the floor, he thought there was no reason why he shouldn't mop the whole kitchen floor at the same time. (It was clear that he was trying to make his wife drop the argument by pretending to be fully occupied by the cleaning and mopping.)

#### Language Exercises

1.

1.
1) Suffix "-ware," meaning "objects made of a particular material"
2) Suffix "-ical," meaning "of, characteristic of, or belonging to"

2.
1) unjust
2) injustice
3) unequal
4) inequality
5) unable
6) inability
7) invisible
8) uncountable
9) unavoidable
10) unbelievable
11) indefinite
12) insignificant
13) inseparable
14) unbalanced
15) uncomfortable
16) uncommon
17) unconditional
18) inedible
19) unconscious
20) unaware
21) unavailable
22) inefficient
23) unfair
24) unfamiliar
25) unfriendly
26) indirect
27) ungrateful
28) inconsiderate
29) unimportant
30) incompetent
31) unlimited
32) incomplete
33) incapable
34) unnecessary
35) unreasonable
36) inappropriate
37) unpleasant
38) unofficial
39) unpopular
40) inadequate

3.
1) dislike/hate (unlike is wrong)
2) unlike (dislike is wrong)
3) different
4) sincere
5) shallow
6) untrue/false
7) unsteady/unstable/shaky
8) heavy/weighty/dark
9) darken/dim
10) tighten
11) arrogantly
12) physical/material
13) valueless (invaluable is wrong)
14) awake

4.
1) wash the dishes
2) pinch one's brows together
3) press one's lips tight
4) squeeze fruits
5) rinse clothes
6) spray cool water
7) take a reasonable tone
8) win the argument
9) demonstrate indifference
10) hurt sb.'s feelings
11) wipe the counter
12) mop the floor

2.
1. into; for; from
2. at; in; of
3. up; out; of
4. up; on; of/about
5. about; out; with
6. onto; of; as/like; with; to
7. up; with; at; of; on
8. up
9. down
10. out; in

3.
1. It's okay with me if you want to keep it secret. But I think you'd better tell her father the truth. He will find out soon anyway.
2. You can take my word for it that they are very much attached to each other. They will never break up.
3. In this famous battle, Zhou Yu and Zhuge Liang came up with the same idea—to destroy the enemy warships by setting them on fire.
4. I decided to tell him about my trouble. He had always been loyal to his friends. I knew that he would be glad to come to my aid/rescue.
5. If things go on like this, we are sure to face a thirsty future. Therefore we must do everything we can to save our water resources for our children's sake.
6. My grandson bought me an air conditioner. I know that he acts out of concern for my health, and I appreciate it.
7. All things considered, I think we still have a lot of room for improvement.
8. I have often heard him complain about our dining-hall food. Now he is at it again. There is always room for improvement of course in our school management, but we, on our part, shouldn't be too hard to please.
9. When the early settlers first came to America, life was extremely hard. And everybody, men and women, old and young, rich and poor, knew that they had to pitch in in order to survive.
10. He knew that he had hurt his brother's feelings. He wished to make it up to him.

4.
1. adverbial modifier (状语)
2. predicative (表语)
3. object (宾语) of the verb like
4. object of the preposition in (介词宾语)
5. subject (主语)

5.
1. Bill's friends were impressed by the way he faced his incurable disease.
2. The way the man used a screwdriver showed that he couldn't be a carpenter.
3. Watch your teacher's lips carefully and say the word the way she does.
4. People are anxiously watching to see the way things will work out in their country.
5. The way the dispute between labor and management was settled surprised the public.
6. From the way he walked, I could tell that something was wrong with his leg.
Or: The way he walked showed that something was wrong with his leg.
7. The way they talked to her aroused her suspicion/made her suspect that they were keeping something from her.

6.
1. The old man was dozing off in an armchair with a magazine on his lap.
2. Under the bridge, the police found a man lying dead, with a knife in his chest.
3. He walked out of his boss's office with his head held high.
4. In September, the school came to life again, with children playing and running around the playground.
5. With their children grown up and gone, the old couple sometimes feel lonely.
6. With all the packing finished, the family could now sit down and have some tea.
7. With a big bag on her shoulder, the old woman moved slowly in the snow.

7.
1. That was the way people lived their lives in my day.
2. Mrs. Jones loves her students the way she loves her own children.
3. The farmer's wife doesn't like the way some young people treat their parents.
4. With Big Yao on the team, I'm sure they'll beat their rivals.
5. The man was sitting in his armchair, with his face buried in his hands.
6. The way people helped him during his journey when he had no money convinced him that in this society, people can still depend on strangers.
7. The way these young people organized campus activities showed their leadership qualities.

8.
1. You know what? All things considered, it's not a bad idea to be a teacher. As a matter of fact, I think it is an excellent idea.
2. You said you do not want any money. You may not want money, but you do need money. I don't see what's wrong with students earning some money during their spare time.
3. Somehow this tune sounds very familiar, but I can't recall what it is. In any case, it is a Russian folk song.
4. It's getting dark. The next town is still two hours' drive away. We might as well camp in the forest, pitch a tent, build a fire, and have a good sleep before we continue our journey tomorrow.
5. Take my word for it, time will heal all wounds and cure all pains.
6. People often resort to body language when words fail.
7. He who only occasionally offers a helping hand on housework does not deserve to be called a considerate husband. A considerate husband should pitch in habitually on the housework with his wife.
8. He knew that he had hurt his girlfriend's feelings and was now looking for a way to make up to her.
9. Some marriages break up because of personality clashes and some because of differences on matters of principle.
10. We'll do all we can to help but you on your part must also make a great effort.

9.
(1) with (2) for (3) other (4) regarding/on (5) discuss (6) interracial
(7) cause (8) apart (9) handle (10) both (11) help (12) communication

10.
1. Neither the man nor his wife in the story seems to know how their differences should be dealt with.
2. The man is well pleased with himself, believing that his wife ought to consider herself as lucky to have such a considerate husband as he is.
3. He is proud of being such a terrific husband, always ready to pitch in on the/with housework.
4. The wife can disagree with her husband (when he says) that interracial marriage is not a good idea, but she should try to reason with him patiently. At any rate/in any case, she should not take him for a hopeless racist immediately.
5. When he came back home, he found his wife cooking dishes, and they smelt wonderful.
6. When my son got married, I gave him my parental advice. I told him that a successful marriage does not depend on winning every argument. That is not what marriage is about.
7. Everybody was shocked when they heard the terrible news from one of the speakers at our meeting.
8. Things related to culture such as ideas, beliefs, and customs, often have a tendency to last a long time, long after society has changed. This is known as/called cultural lag.
9. The man in the story is not completely free from racism. His body has moved into today, but his head may still partly remain in the past century. This can happen to all of us.
10. It is very impolite if you don't bother to send a quick thank-you note when you receive a gift from your friend.

---

# Unit 3

## Text A · The Rite of Spring · Arthur Miller

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

This essay deals with gardening again, a rather familiar subject, and not usually regarded as an issue of particularly great importance either. But written by Arthur Miller, one of the most famous contemporary American playwrights, it has unusual poetic charm and very different perspectives. We have therefore decided to include this piece here although the language is a bit difficult and perhaps should be used later if the text is not too short for that purpose.

Gardening is generally regarded as a hobby, a pastime. And many little books as well as essays must have been written to tell people how to grow tomatoes, beans, cucumbers, etc., in their private garden. But gardening, like many other human activities, often has symbolic meanings for different people. In our culture, it is often compared to the rearing of young children, hence, teachers are often called "gardeners". In "Mandela's Gardening", gardening is associated with Mandela's revolutionary work and struggle. He talks about how gardening offers him simple but enduring satisfaction in prison. He tells us how the sense of being the owner of the small patch of earth gives him a small taste of freedom. He says that he sees the garden as a metaphor for certain aspects of his life because like gardeners, revolutionary leaders also have to take responsibility for what they cultivate; they must mind their work, try to drive back enemies, save what can be saved, and eliminate what cannot succeed. But Arthur Miller is not a revolutionary leader. He is a writer, a man who is particularly gifted in describing the beauty of human activities and experiences.

In this essay, the author not only tells us how he acquires unique pleasure from this activity. He also enjoys the unusual challenge of "conflict", the satisfaction of being able to improve and "do it just right this time", he talks about the parental instinct at work in gardening and the joy of seeing the healthy growth of plants and the success in their protection; he mentions also the moral significance, how gardening keeps reminding people of the delicate and easily ruined Earth and how the healthy growth, the orderliness and thrusting life in the garden reflect "similar movements in the gardener's own spirit".

#### 2 Structure

As most essays, this one can be divided into three parts: the introduction, the body, and the conclusion. The first two paragraphs form the first part, in which the writer points out why he starts a garden. He does so, not for love of vegetable but as a way of embracing spring—a season of revival. In the body paragraphs (paras. 3–10), he lists various reasons for gardening. The concluding paragraphs (paras. 11–12) state the thesis of the essay: gardening, which begins in spring—a season of hope, keeps people hopeful for the future, especially in the coldest days of December.

I (paras. 1–2)

Gardening is regarded as the rite of spring, a way of celebrating the coming of spring.

II Reasons for keeping a garden (paras. 3–10)

1. In gardening, one gets pleasure as well as conflict, as one has to make choices.
2. Gardening reminds us of how delicate, bountiful, and easily ruined our planet is.
3. A true gardener should have a sense of responsibility.
   1) Gardening is neurotic in that it requires care and love.
   2) Gardening is moral in that it requires patience and persistence.
   3) Gardening involves learning through trial and error/learning through mistakes, like in the case of the tomatoes.
4. Gardening is character building.
   1) The growth of plants is a way of the movements in one's spirit/the spiritual growth of man.
   2) Gardening requires striving as in other human activities.

III (paras. 11–12)

Gardening enables people to revive hope/stay hopeful for the future.

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. As we suggest above, it may be advisable for students to recall the text "Mandela's Garden" and make comparisons between the two authors.

2. Ask students why the author gave this title. Make sure they do not misinterpret when the author says that he would like gardening better if the plants could grow hot dogs. Ask students what they think is Arthur Miller's real attitude toward gardening? Does he love gardening or not?

3. Ask students what Arthur Miller regards as the attractions of gardening.

4. See if students can think of any other possible reasons for taking up this hobby.

5. Arthur Miller is a very colorful literary figure. Make students find more information about this character, and in this way help them learn to use the Internet for information.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text A

1. I have never understood why we keep a garden and why over 36 years ago when I bought my first house in the country, I started digging up a patch for vegetables before doing anything else. (para. 1)

From these lines we can infer that the author is not a farmer. He only "keeps" a garden as a hobby, and he has kept it for over 36 years.

The word "country" here means "countryside". There is a joke about a customer who goes to the supermarket to buy some eggs, and when he is told that "These eggs are fresh from the country", he asks, "Which country?"

"A patch" is a small piece of land usually used for growing vegetables. Compare with "a plot of land" below which also means a small piece of land, but can be used for building or other purposes.

2. When you think how easy and cheap, relatively, it is to buy a bunch of carrots or beets, why raise them? (para. 1)

relatively: in a relative sense (相对来说)
Note: In Chinese, we say "相对来说比较容易", whereas in English we say "relatively easy", NOT "relatively easier".

a bunch of: a group of
e.g. a bunch of people, a bunch of flowers, a bunch of keys, a bunch of bananas/grapes/ cherries

why raise them?: This is an elliptical sentence. The complete sentence should be "Why do we raise them?"
More examples:
Why go there today?
Why sleep on the floor?
Why tell me now?

3. And root crops especially are hard to tell apart, when store-bought, from our own. (para. 1)

root crops: 块根作物
Note how the word "crop" is translated into Chinese
e.g. The crops are coming up beautifully this year. 今年庄稼长势很好。
What kind of crops are grown here? 这里种什么作物/庄稼？
We had a very good crop of strawberries this year. 我们今年草莓收成很好。

tell sb./sth. apart: to recognize/tell the difference between two people or two things that are similar; distinguish
e.g. I can't tell Japanese apart from Chinese in appearance.
Some crops and weeds are hard to tell apart when young.

4. There is a human instinct at work here, a kind of back-breaking make-believe that has no reality. (para. 1)

The author thinks that it is perhaps partly human nature why we like gardening. It is human instinct to be active and pretend that what you have done is better than it really is.

back-breaking: physically hard and tiring
make-believe: the activity of pretending that sth. is real or that a situation is better than it really is
at work: (here) having a particular effect or influence
e.g. We must understand that there is still a strong anti-reform force at work.
With cold-war mentality still at work, both sides may misjudge the present situation.

5. Besides, I don't particularly like eating vegetables. I'd much rather eat something juicy and fat. (para. 1)

I'd much rather eat...: I'd much prefer eating...

6. As it is, though, I cannot deny that when April comes I find myself going out to lean on the fence and look at that miserable plot of land, resolving with all my rational powers not to plant it again. (para. 2)

as it is, though: although, as it turns out/in reality (虽然实际上)
e.g. I had hoped to complete my essay today, as it is, though, I'm afraid I won't be able to finish it until the end of the week.
We know how important it is to change our economic policy. As it is, though, we still put too much emphasis on GDP.

This is a very complicated sentence. The basic structure is "As it is, I cannot deny..." What follows is a clause introduced by "that" used as the object of the verb "deny". This object clause consists of "I find myself going out", the main clause and its adverbial clause of time "when April comes". "To lean on the fence and look at that miserable plot of land" serves as the adverbial of purpose of the verb "going out". "Resolving... not to plant it again" is a present participle phrase used as adverbial of manner.

There are other examples in the text which have complicated structure. They deserve our close attention.
Notice how verbs are used in different verb patterns:
to come; to deny that (clause);to find oneself doing sth. to lean on; to look at, to resolve (not) to do sth. to plant the land, to start digging to like eating; to understand why...

7. But inevitably a morning arrives when, just as I am awakening, a scent wafts through the window, something like earth-as-air, a scent that seems to come up from the very center of this planet. (para. 2)

waft through: (of smell or noise) to float through the air in a gentle way
scent: a particular, esp. a pleasant smell. It can also refer to the smell an animal or person has
e.g. The dogs picked up the scent again.

Synonyms of "smell": scent, perfume; odor; flavor; aroma; stink; stench

8. And the sun means business, suddenly, and has a different, deeper yellow in its beams on the carpet. The birds begin screaming hysterically, thinking what I am thinking—the worms are deliciously worming their way through the melting soil. (para. 2)

mean business: to be very serious about sth. you have to do
e.g. This time, we mean business when we say that we will improve our banking system.
—Is she serious when she says she'll quit her present job?
—Yes, I think she means business.

Synonyms of "beam": light, ray
Synonyms of "carpet": rug (床前或壁炉前的小地毯); tapestry (挂毯)

worm one's way through: to move like a worm through
The verb "worm" here is a verb which means to move like a worm. There are many verbs like this in English.
e.g. Food prices rocketed to a new height and many people complained.
A small path snaked through the trees and up the hill.
Suppression is not a clever way to deal with people's complaints. It always boomerangs.
The train thundered past at that moment.
The boy tried to thread through the big crowd.
The shoe flew towards him. Luckily he ducked his head in time.
She noticed that a car had been tailing her.

9. It is not only pleasure sending me back to stare at that plot of soil, it is really conflict (para. 3)

This can be taken as an elliptical sentence. The complete sentence would be: It is not only pleasure that sends me back to stare at that plot of soil, it is really conflict.

10. The last few years we put 36-inch-wide black plastic between the rows, and it worked perfectly, keeping the soil moist in dry times and weed-free. (para. 3)

Note how these compound adjectives are formed:
36-inch-wide; 20-year-old (man); two-meter-deep (water), 500-page-thick (book); 7,000-foot-high (mountain), 800-mile-long (river), 100-story-high (building); thirty-meter-tall (tree)

Notice the different meanings of the word "row" in the following sentences:
He sat on the second row. (排、列)
We can't row the little boat across the swift river. (划船)
He said that he had a terrible row with his wife yesterday. (吵架)

keeping the soil moist... and weed-free: making sure that the soil remains moist... and free from weed

Pay attention to this pattern: keep sb./sth. + adjective. "Moist" means "damp". "Weed-free" means free from or without any weed.
More examples of the use of "-free":
He bought some gifts for his daughter at the airport duty-free shops. Our food used to be pollution-free.
They claim their city to be housefly-free

11. But black plastic looks so industrial, so unromantic, that I have gradually moved over to hay mulch. (para. 4)

But black plastic looks so unnatural (because it is made in factories) and ugly that I have gradually changed/shifted to hay mulch.
Note the differences of the following words:
weed: a plant you don't want
hay: long grass, cut and dried for feeding animals
reed: a tall, thin plant that grows near water, often used for making things

12. Keeping a garden makes you aware of how delicate, bountiful, and easily ruined the surface of this little planet is. (para. 5)

If you keep a garden, it will help you realize how generous the land of the Earth is to us and how easily damaged it is.

13. In that 50-by-70-foot patch there must be a dozen different types of soil. Tomato won't grow in one part but loves another, and the same goes for the other crops. (para. 5)

50-by-70-foot: 50-foot-wide and 70-foot-long
the same goes for sb./sth.: the same applies to/is true for sb./sth.
e.g. Students will not work hard unless they have a sense of achievement. This goes for the teachers, too.
If you want others to take you as a friend, you must show them your respect and understanding. This goes for international relations as well.

14. I suppose if you loaded the soil with chemical fertilizer these differences would be less noticeable, but I use it sparingly and only in rows right where seeds are planted rather than broadcast over the whole area. (para. 5)

I suppose if you fill the soil with chemical fertilizer, these differences will not be so obvious, but I use it very carefully and only in where the seeds are instead of spreading it over the whole place.
Notice the word "broadcast" here means differently from that in "broadcasting news".

15. I'm not sure why I do this beyond the saving in fertilizer and my unwillingness to aid the weeds. (para. 5)

beyond: except (used in negative sentences)
e.g. I don't know anything about this beyond what you told me.
He had no other income beyond his retired pension.

Note: The word "beyond" is very useful. We suggest you look it up in the dictionary.

16. The attractions of gardening, ... are neurotic and moral. Whenever life seems pointless and difficult to grasp, you can always... (para. 6)

neurotic and moral: spiritual and moral
Notice that the word "neurotic" is usually used in a negative sense. The author, for humorous effect, keeps using such words and expressions to hide his intense interest and full appreciation of the significance of gardening. But these ironical expressions cannot fool experienced readers.

pointless: meaningless

17. Also, your paternal or maternal instincts come into play because helpless living things are depending on you, require training and encouragement and protection from enemies. (para. 6)

Psychologists say that we all have paternal or maternal instincts—the natural desire to take care of our young children.
Note: The author is personifying the plants.

come into play: to start to have an effect
e.g. We hope that when the new policy comes into play, the job situation will improve.
Many scientists believe that the increasing natural disasters are signs that the global warming has come into play.

18. In some cases, as with beans and cucumbers, your children—as it were, begin to turn up on you in massive numbers, growing more and more each morning and threatening to follow you into the house to strangle you in their vines. (para. 6)

as it were: as you might say (可以说). It is used for making a description sound less definite or less exact.
e.g. For years the blind man only had this dog to keep him company. It was, as it were, his little brother.
She felt that she had just had a terrible dream as it were, and was now reawakening.

The author here is describing how the beans and cucumbers can grow in massive numbers and come to him suddenly as if to strangle him in their vines. The word "strangle" here doesn't really mean to choke him to death. He is saying these numerous beans and cucumbers are like naughty and playful children, struggling to embrace him at the same time, making him almost hard to breathe.

19. Gardening is a moral occupation as well, because you always start in spring resolved to keep it looking neat this year, just like the pictures in the catalogues. (para. 7)

keep it looking neat: Here's another verb pattern in which the word "keep" can be used. Other verbs that can be used in this pattern are: find, see, hear, feel, watch, notice, imagine, send, etc.

neat: carefully arranged and looking nice
catalogue: a book with pictures and information about things you can buy (商品目录)

20. But by July, you once again face the chaos of unthinned carrots, lettuce and beets. (para. 7)

chaos: a situation in which everything is confused and in a mess
e.g. This was the most terrible natural disaster in recorded history, and weeks after it happened, the country was still in chaos.
The burst of the real estate bubble will surely cause an economic chaos.

unthinned carrots: carrots allowed to grow in such a way that no necessary space is left in between

21. This is when my wife becomes—openly now—mistress of the garden. (para. 7)

mistress of the garden: the one in the family who has control over the garden

22. A consumer of vast quantities of vegetables, she does the thinning and hand-cultivating of the tiny plants. Squatting, she patiently moves down each row selecting which plants shall live and which she will cast aside. (para. 7)

a consumer of vast quantities of vegetables: a person who eats lots of vegetables
vast quantities of: a vast quantity of/a large amount of/great amounts of
cast aside: to get rid of/throw away

23. She looks about skeptically. Her favorite task is binding the tomato plants to stakes. (para. 8)

skeptical: ("sceptical" in British English) doubtful; disbelieving; uncertain; unconvinced; incredulous; inclined to question
"Stake" here means a wooden or metal post with a pointed end used for supporting or marking sth. (桩；柱).
Notice that the word "stake" can have many different meanings.
e.g. The majority stake is still in the hands of the government. (股权)
We cannot let it pass because the future of our country is at stake. (处于危险当中) You can't afford to lose because the stakes are too high. (赌注)

24. She is an outspoken, truthful woman, or she was until she learned better. (para. 8)

outspoken and truthful: 心直口快的；实话实说的
until she learned better: until she realized it was unwise and changed her ways

25. ... then she gives me my good-bye kiss and says casually, "Tomatoes in damp soil tend more to get fungi," and walks away to her plane. (para. 8)

"Fungi" is the plural form of "fungus".

26. But by October nothing in the garden matters, so sure am I that I will never plant it again. (para. 8)

matter: to be important
e.g. Your health matters a great deal to me.
Does it really matter whether we get high scores for all our courses?

so sure am I that...: Note that this sentence is in inverted order.
plant it: to plant the garden. Note that the word "plant" means to use the land for growing things.

27. There are few sights quite as beautiful as a vegetable garden glistening in the sun, all dewy and glittering with a dozen shades of green at seven in the morning. (para. 9)

Notice the subtle differences of: shine, glisten, glitter, glare, gleam, glow, glister, beam, shimmer, twinkle, flicker, flash, sparkle
shades: (here) slightly different forms or types or degrees of (color, meaning, opinion, etc.)

28. In some pocket of the mind there may even be a tendency to change this vision into a personal reassurance that all this healthy growth, this orderliness and thrusting life must somehow reflect movements in one's own spirit. (para. 9)

pocket: a small area different from the areas around it
e.g. There are still some pockets of the city where are extremely poor.
The country is back to normal except for a few small pockets of resistance against the government forces.

change this vision into a personal reassurance that...: When he sees this beautiful view, he feels certain that...
orderliness: this well-organized and arranged garden
thrusting life: the fact that the garden is full of life and everything is growing so vigorously

29. As it is, April is for getting irritated all over again at this pointless, time-consuming hobby. I do not understand people who claim to "love" gardening. (para. 10)

be irritated at: to be annoyed at and impatient about
all over again: a second time from the beginning
pointless, claim: Once again, do not let the author's use of these words mislead us to believe that he does not like gardening.

30. A garden is an extension of oneself—or selves—and so it has to be an arena where striving does not cease, but continues by other means. (para. 10)

an extension of oneself: that part added to one's body
it has to be an arena where striving does not cease: it has to be a stage or field (舞台) where your effort to achieve sth. never stops

## （续）

Note: The verb "cease" is pronounced /si:s/, NOT /si:z/. It can be used both as vt. or vi. When used transitively, it can be followed both by an infinitive (cease to do sth.) or gerund (cease doing sth.) as object.

other means: other ways or methods. Notice that the spelling does not change when used in the singular form. We say "a means of", instead of "a mean of".

31. But you will feel better for not standing on your dignity. (para. 10)

stand on dignity: to make it clear to sb. that he must treat you with dignity (保持尊严；摆架子)

32. And that's what gardening is all about—character building. (para. 10)

character building: developing one's good personal moral qualities

33. But is it conceivable that the father of us should have been a weaver, shoemaker, or anything but a gardener? Of course not. (para. 11)

is it conceivable that...: is it thinkable/possible that...
Note: The opposite of "conceivable" is "inconceivable", whereas the opposite of "thinkable" is "unthinkable".

34. Only the gardener is capable of endlessly reviving so much hope that this year, regardless of drought, flood, typhoon, or his own stupidity, this year he is going to do it right! (para. 11)

regardless of: in spite of; without being affected or influenced by
e.g. All public works continued to function regardless of the terrible earthquake and tsunami.
All people above 18 have the right to vote regardless of sex, race, education, religion or financial status.

### III Translation of Text A

春之祭
亚瑟·米勒

说真的，我从来都不明白，我们到底为什么要有一个菜园子，为什么36年前，当我第一次在乡下买了房子以后，我会别的事情都不做，首先就挖一块菜地。想想现在买一堆胡萝卜或者甜菜头，相对来说，那么容易，而且又那么便宜，为什么还要自己去种呢？尤其是那些块根植物，自己种的和店里买的，根本就很难分辨。这里肯定有人的本性在起作用。人就喜欢脱离现实，毫无意义地瞎折腾。再说，我又并非特别喜欢吃蔬菜。我宁可吃些油汪汪、香喷喷、一咬一口肉汁的东西，比如说热狗。

要说，如果能在窗外种热狗的话，那倒真的有了—种可以毫不犹豫为自己辩护的理由。可是，在现在这种情况下，我无法否认，每当4月来临，我就会发现自己走出家门，倚着园子外的篱笆，望着那块倒霉的地，十分理智地下定决心再也不去种它了。然而，总有那么一天，当我早晨醒来的时候，一股香味似乎从窗外飘进来，就好像来自地球中心的泥土的清香。这时，太阳似乎也突然认真起来，它投射到地毯上的光似乎比往常要更加深些。那些鸟开始歇斯底里地鸣叫，心里和我一样，想着那些美味可口的虫子正从融化的土中慢慢爬出来。

让我回去看那块地的并不只是快乐，而是矛盾和斗争。每年碰到的问题是一样的：今年我们该使用什么方法？前几年，我们在一行行的蔬菜之间放上了36英寸宽的黑色塑料薄膜，效果极好。干旱的时候能够保湿，而且保证没有杂草。

但是黑塑料薄膜一看就是来自工厂的东西，一点浪漫的情调都没有。所以我就逐步改用干草覆盖料。我们收到了不少干草，草腐烂了，的确能改善土壤的结构。再说，看起来也很舒服，而且又不花一分钱。

家里有个菜园子能使你意识到我们这个小星球的表面有多娇嫩，多丰饶，多容易被破坏。在我们那块50英尺宽、70英尺长的土地上，肯定有十几种不同的土壤。西红柿在某个地方长不好，但是在另外一个地方却长得很好。其他庄稼也一样。我想，要是你在地里撒满化肥，这种差别就不会那么明显。但是，我用化肥非常节省。我只放一点在播了种子的地方，而不是漫撒在整片地上。我不知道我为什么这么做，我只是想省点化肥，也不想给杂草帮便宜。

管理菜园对人的吸引力，我认为，起码对我来说，是由于神经或道德方面的原因。每当生活似乎变得无聊，掌握不住的时候，你总可以走出房门到园子里去做点什么。此外，你当父亲或母亲的本能也会发挥作用，因为那些有生命的东西全都靠着你，你要去训练，去鼓励，去保护，让它们不受敌人的侵犯。有时候，比如说你的扁豆或黄瓜你的话，那些你可以说是你的孩子们，会突然开始向你发动大进攻，每天早晨长得越来越多，好像要追着你进房间将你绞死在它们的瓜藤条之中。

管理菜园也是一种与道德品质有关的活动，因为你总是在春天开始的时候下定决心今年要它弄得就和商品广告上画的那样好。但是到了7月，你又一次会面对因为没及时采摘而密密麻麻、乱成一堆的胡萝卜、莴笋和甜菜头。这个时候，也就是我妻子公开成为菜园的女主人的时候。她吃蔬菜的胃口奇大。她会来间苗，培育那些小植物。她会耐心地蹲着，一行行走下去，决定哪些应该保留，哪些必须扔掉。

在这个时候，我妻子86岁的老妈第一次来看看我们的菜园了。她是一个植物学家。她以怀疑的眼光四下望望。她最喜欢的工作是给西红柿搭架子。她是一个有话说就说、直言不讳的人。我妈原来是这样，直到后来学乖了。她说没有说"你们把西红柿种到园子里潮湿的地方了。"她一直要等到10月份，也就是她每年照例要回到欧洲她自己家里的时候，临和我吻别时，才随便说一句，"在潮湿的地方种的西红柿容易长霉。"说完就向飞机走去。不过，到10月份的时候，园子里什么东西都无所谓了，我已经肯定我再也不会再种什么了。

我想，我之所以搞一个菜园，是因为我必须如此。不然我要每天几次走过什么都不种的围着篱笆的空园子，这是无法想象的。再说，种菜还有一些好处。这肯定是什么我每年又会这件事情的原因。世上很少有在清晨7点钟阳光下闪闪发亮的菜园子那么美妙的景色，一切都闪耀着晶莹的露水，深浅不同的层层绿色。实在比一行行的热狗要好看多了。在人的脑子的某个角落，甚至于可能有一种把这种美妙的景象转化成个人安慰的倾向，让他觉得这些植物的茁壮成长、整齐有序、和努力向前推进的生命一定在某种意义上反映了一个人精神上的活动。如果没有菜园子让我们去耕种，我都不知该对什么样的事情倾注我们这些多出来的业余爱好去烦恼。我不理解那些扬言"热爱"园艺的人。一个菜园子是一个人或者很多人的延伸。所以，它应该是一个活动舞台，在那里努力和奋斗不会停止，而会只以其他办法继续下去。比方说，你有时必须面对这样的情形：你必须承认你的莴苣种得不够深，或者水浇得不够。这时你不能在幻想它明天还会长出来，你必须把这块地再刨一遍。但是你会感觉好多了，因为你没有为了自尊而不及时认错。而这正是从事园艺的意义所在：它能锻炼人的性格。这就是为什么亚当当园丁的原因。（而我们都知道他最后的下场。）

但是，除了当园丁，我们能想象我们的祖先是—个织布工、鞋匠，或者任何其他什么吗？当然不能。只有园丁才能够永不停顿地恢复巨大的希望，相信不管干旱、水灾、台风或者他自己的愚蠢，今年他一定会做好。让我们还是相信上帝为他创造出的唯一能够自欺欺人的所选择的合适的职业吧。

我想，为了表示我的诚实，我应该坦白承认，以上这段话是我在12月份最冷的一天写下的一。

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

noun + verb + ing: back-breaking (para. 1), time-consuming (para. 10)
noun + adjective: weed-free (para. 3)
Compound words expressing measurement: 36-inch-wide (para. 3), 50-by-70-foot (para. 5)

2.

1. Verb phrases
cast aside, come into play, depend on, lean on, load a place with sth., stand on one's dignity, tell sb. apart, from go, for go, turn up on sb., turn one's mind toward sth., look about, worm one's way through somewhere, change sth. into, be aware of, be broadcast over
2. Prepositional phrases
at work, in massive numbers, in some cases, regardless of, without hesitation, for sb.'s sake, by other means
3. Other phrases
a bunch of, all over again, anything but, as it is, as it were, shades of (a color), vast quantities of, rather than, a plot of soil, a patch of land, "d much rather"

3.

build one's character, consume time, cultivate plants, improve the soil keep a garden, make a visit/trip, mean business, revive hope, ruin the planet, strangle a person, improve the soil's composition, face chaos

4.

The use of irony runs throughout the text. The author starts by saying that he doesn't understand why he has taken up gardening, especially when he doesn't like eating vegetables:
Besides, I don't particularly like eating vegetables. I'd much rather eat something juicy and fat. Like hot dogs. (para. 1)
But immediately in the next paragraph he recalls the pleasure of feeling the vibrations of life in early spring, inviting him to garden, which is certainly much more appealing than rows of hot dogs.
Another example can be found in paragraph 6, when the author talks about the thriving beans and cucumbers:
... your children—as it were, begin to turn up on you in massive numbers, growing more and more each morning and threatening to follow you into the house to strangle you in their vines.
Here negative words like "turn upon," "threatening," and "strangle" are used, but actually they are used in an exaggerated way to describe the pleasure and satisfaction of a gardener seeing the vegetables growing so well, just like caring parents seeing their children growing up and about to hug them.
We should also note the seeming paradox between the title, "The Rite of Spring," and the last paragraph. It seems as if the author is daydreaming in the depth of winter, but he is actually looking forward to the coming spring when he can once again start gardening, a glimpse of hope in the cold winter.

5.

1. When you think how easy and cheap, relatively, it is to buy a bunch of carrots or beets, why raise them? (para. 1)
Noun clause introduced by wh-words(more examples to be found in Exercise 6)
Elliptical sentence
The full question is "why should/do we raise them?".
2. And root crops especially are hard to tell apart, when store-bought, from our own. (para. 1)
Elliptical relative clause
The full clause is "when they are store-bought."
3. It is not only pleasure sending me back to stare at that plot of soil ... (para. 3)
Gerund is used as subject of the sentence; it also has an object and adverbial modifier of its own. (more examples to be found in Exercise 6)
4. A consumer of vast quantities of vegetables, she does the thinning and hand-cultivating of the tiny plants. (para. 7)
Appositive

Gardening reminds us of how delicate, bountiful, and easily ruined our planet is.<br>3. A true gardener should have a sense of responsibility.<br>1) Gardening is neurotic in that it requires care and love.<br>2) Gardening is moral in that it requires patience and persistence.<br>3) Gardening involves learning through trial and error/learning through mistakes, like in the case of the tomatoes.<br>4. Gardening is character building.<br>1) The growth of plants is a way of the movements in one's spirit/the spiritual growth of man.<br>2) Gardening requires striving as in other human activities. |

2.

1. It is perhaps human nature that makes us like gardening, a human instinct to believe that we must be active and work extremely hard for no reason.
2. Also, while you garden, you begin to feel and act like a father or a mother, seeing your plants as children who depend on you and whom you need to take care of and protect from enemies and dangers.
3. Deep in my mind, I am even inclined to believe that such a beautiful view of the garden can somehow make me feel sure that this is what my personal life should be like, growing healthily, well-organized and full of vitality in spirit.
4. A garden is like an inseparable part added to your life ... where you must continue to work hard and succeed, although in other ways.
