# Unit 7

## Text A · Wisdom of Bear Wood · Michael Welzenbach

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

The theme of this story is obviously suggested at the very end when the author describes the legacy of the boy's encounter with the old woman as "a wisdom tutored by nature itself, about the seen and unseen, about things that change and things that are changeless, and about the fact that no matter how seemingly different two souls may be, they possess the potential for that most precious, rare thing—an enduring and rewarding friendship".

The story is not very subtle, nor is it particularly dramatic, but it has delightful descriptions of beautiful landscape, and description of a friendship between two souls.

What is particularly interesting about this friendship is the fact that it occurs between two people who are "seemingly different" in every way: One is an American boy of twelve, and the other is an English woman old enough to be the boy's grandmother. They are total strangers, but their chance encounter almost immediately leads to a beautiful friendship. This is because they need each other. First of all, they are both lonely. The boy is lonely because he is alone in a foreign country with his parents; the woman is lonely because she has lost her dear husband and besides she is not in good health. Therefore, they find consolation in each other's company. But they have another important bond—their common interest in nature. For the boy the Wood is his private paradise, and for the woman it is part of her husband's legacy. The boy is full of curiosity about the mysteries of nature, and the woman might be said to hold the key to the treasures of human knowledge, being married to a professor of biology. One might also mention the shortbread the woman keeps supplying for the boy, which he loves. But the real reason for their friendship is the old woman's selfless love and care for the boy. It is often said that true love is in the giving and not in the taking. The woman takes delight in giving the boy good food to eat, and good books to read. And they are precisely what the boy wants at his age. Does the woman ask for anything in return? No. However, through giving, she cannot help receiving. Although totally unaware, the boy has given the woman great consolation too.

The boy will never forget this friendship either. In this world, true friendship is both rare and precious. And the boy is very lucky to have found such a wonderful friend in his childhood. What he learned about nature with the help and encouragement of this woman in Bear Wood may very well have determined his life: the things he has observed and the things he can't see; the things that change as well as the things that do not change. They all combine to form the wisdom of Bear Wood that the boy has received as a precious gift.

#### 2 Structure

The text can be divided into three parts. The introduction (paras. 1–4) describes the American boy's first six months in England. The body paragraphs (paras. 5–35) relate how the boy met Mrs. Robertson-Glasgow and how they remained friends till her death. In the last two paragraphs (paras. 36–37), the boy reflects years later on the legacy of that friendship.

I. In his first six months in England, the 12-year-old American boy enjoyed the woods and farms there, but avoided making friends for fear that he would have to say good-bye too soon. (paras. 1–4)

II. The boy made the best friend in the world. (paras. 5–35)

1. How the boy and an old woman, Mrs. Robertson-Glasgow, met and became friends. (paras. 5–23)
2. How their friendship grew, and benefited both of them. (paras. 24–27)
3. How the boy took the old woman's death. (paras. 28–35)

III. Friendship is possible between very different people. (paras. 36–37)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. As there does not seem to be a particularly profound moral message, the teacher should probably put more emphasis on the language rather than the content.
2. There are quite a few places in the story where the language is particularly descriptive. Draw students' attention to the figures of speech used by the author and help them to appreciate the beauty of the language.
3. Find out whether anybody in class has had similar experience of meeting a much older person who has influenced his/her life. Encourage them to tell their personal stories.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. My father's government job demanded that he go overseas every few years, so I was used to wrenching myself away from friends. (para. 1)
demanded that he go: (NOT "went" or "goes") subjunctive mood
every few years: used for showing how often sth. happens, esp. when there is a regular time between them (每隔几年)
wrench oneself away from: to twist and pull oneself away (or free) from

2. Loving nature, however, I was most delighted by the endless patchwork of farms and woodland that surrounded our house. (para. 2)
loving nature, however: however, as/because I loved nature
a patchwork of farms (fields): farms that look like small pieces of cloth of different colors when seen from far above
woodland: a piece of land covered with trees

3. In the deep woods that verged against our back fence, a network of paths led almost everywhere, and pheasants rocketed off into the dense laurels ahead as you walked. (para. 2)
to verge against: to be close to, to be at the edge or border of (This is a rare expression.)
Notice how the preposition "against" is used to mean "next to", "touching or hitting the surface of".
e.g. A heavy rain was pattering against the windows.
He stood his stick against the wall.
The policeman was leaning against the door.
a network of: a system of lines, wires, roads, etc. that cross each other and are connected to each other (一种网络)
pheasants rocketed off: pheasants took off like rockets
Compare:
Factories mushroomed. (They grew like mushroom.)
He tried to worm into that organization. (He tried to get into that organization like a worm, i.e., slowly and with difficulty, and often by using clever methods.)
It could snowball into a serious conflict. (It could gradually develop into a serious conflict.)
He was wolfing it down as if he had not eaten for days. (He was eating it very quickly like a wolf.)

4. I spent most of my time roaming the woods and fields alone, playing Robin Hood, ...(para. 3)
to roam the woods (fields, streets, hills, etc.): idiomatic use of "roam" as a transitive word. Normally it is used as an intransitive verb as in "roam about" or "roam around". "To roam" means to walk or travel and usu. without any clear purpose.

5. Keeping to myself was my way of not forming attachments that I would only have to abandon the next time we moved. (para. 3)
I did not try to make many friends because in that way I did not have to give up my friendship the next time I had to move.
to keep to oneself: to live a very quiet and private life and not do many things involving other people

6. ... through no design of my own. (para. 3)
... not that I intended to do it/not that I planned it that way/just by chance.

7. ... to roam about his immense property. (para. 4)
property: land, buildings, or both together (房地产，庄园)

8. I started hiking there every weekend, up a long, sloping hill to an almost impenetrable stand of trees called Bear Wood. (para. 4)
an almost impenetrable stand of trees: trees growing so thick that they are impossible to go through or enter into
stand: a group of trees growing in a given area
impenetrable: derived from "penetrate"

9. Slipping through a barbed-wire fence, I'd leave the bright sun and the twitter and rustle of insects and animals outside and creep into another world—a vaulted cathedral, with tree trunks for pillars and years' accumulation of long brown needles for a softly carpeted floor. (para. 4)
the twitter and rustle: onomatopoeia (象声词)
More examples:
the twitter of birds; the rustle of leaves; the hiss of the snake; the gurgle of the water; the cracking of the fire; the banging of the door; the fluttering of the flag; the rumbling of the waves; the tick-tock of the clock
with tree trunks for pillars: with tree trunks serving or functioning as pillars Draw students' attention to the word "for" here.
More examples:
With his torn shirt for paper and his bleeding finger for a pen, he wrote his last letter.
She just had a banana for breakfast.
With the green banana for glue, he fixed the radiator.

10. My own breathing rang in my ears, and the slightest stirring of any woodland creature echoed through this private paradise. (para. 4)
I could even hear my own breathing, and even the lightest movement of any bird or animal in the wood could be heard throughout this paradise.

11. I proceeded quietly, careful not to alarm a bird that might loudly warn other creatures to hide. (para. 5)
I moved on quietly...
creature: anything that is living (but not a plant), usu. with a particular quality as in the following:
all living creatures; a charming creature; a terrible creature; a strange creature

12. Perhaps this is why the frail old lady I nearly ran into was as startled as I was. (para. 6)
frail: thin and weak, esp. because you have become old

13. Then, recovering quickly, she gave a welcoming smile that instantly put me at ease. (para. 6)
to put sb. at ease: to make sb. feel relaxed
More examples of "at ease":
He always feels ill at ease in front of strangers.
The professor must be quite popular. The students all look at ease in his presence.
Also: to make sb. at ease; to set sb. at ease

14. A pair of powerful-looking binoculars dangled from her neck. (para. 6)
powerful: Here it means "高倍的 (望远镜)".
Notice the difference between binoculars and telescope or looking-glass. Binoculars have two glasses (双筒望远镜), and the word is made by putting together "bi-" which means "two" and "ocular" which means sth. related to eyes.

15. "Yes, they're wary," she said. "But then, gamekeepers have been shooting them ever since they got here. They're introduced, you know, not native." (para. 11)
wary: careful, cautious, watchful, suspicious, alert
gamekeeper: sb. whose job is to look after birds and animals that are kept to be hunted on private land. Notice the use of the word "game" to refer to the animals, birds, and fish that are hunted for food or as a sport.
to be introduced: to be brought into this place from somewhere else for the first time

16. Anybody who knew this sort of stuff was definitely cool even if she was trespassing in my special place. (para. 12)
cool: This informal word is hard to define nowadays. It is loosely used to mean attractive, fashionable, relaxed, etc., in a way people, esp. young people admire.

17. Would you care to join me? (para. 13)
This is considered polite but formal, and is used to ask if sb. wants to do sth..

18. I had been warned against going off with strangers... (para. 14)
Review the usual patterns in which the word "warn" is used: to warn that, to warn sb. not to do sth.; to warn sb. against sth., etc.

19. ... extending her fine hand. (para. 15)
... offering to shake hands with the boy.
"Fine" here means small and attractively shaped, looking rather delicate. Remember the woman is old and weak.

20. ... to walk the fields. (para. 17)
Notice that the word "walk" is usu. used as an intransitive verb except in some idiomatic expressions. "To walk the fields" is one of them, which means to go over or along on foot.
Other common expressions in which "walk" is used as a transitive verb:
When people walk their dog, I wonder whether they ever realize that the dog would be much happier completely free in nature than just being allowed a little walk every day.
There are no more buses at this hour. Let's walk it.
It's dark outside. I'll walk you home.

21. Soon I saw a small brick cottage that glowed pinkly in the westering sun. (para. 18)
Soon I saw a small brick cottage shining with a pink color in the sun that was moving toward the west.

22. ... glass-fronted cases containing figures of ivory and carved stone... (para. 18)
Check students to make sure that they know the meanings of "case" and "figure" in this context.

23. I had made the best friend in the world. (para. 23)
Notice that in the usual phrase "to make friends with", "friend" is always in the plural e.g.
I don't think it's difficult to make friends with people who do not belong to your age group.
Parents must try to make friends with their own children. They can't take their love and respect for granted.
It is wrong to say "I want to make friends (or make a friend) with him". We normally say "I want him to be my friend" or "I want to have him as my friend".

24. ... and my well of knowledge about natural history began to brim over. At school, I earned the nickname "Prof" ... (para. 24)
Notice the figurative use of the word "well" and the exaggeration of having knowledge "brimming over".
to brim over: to have so much of sth. that it begins to flow over the edge of the container. It is used in the patterns:
Something is brimming over.
Somebody is brimming over with something. (love, confidence, knowledge, etc.)
Two other expressions are similar in meaning:
to overflow with
to burst with

25. Familiarity sometimes makes people physically invisible, for you find yourself talking to the heart—to the essence, as it were, rather than to the face. (para. 26)
When people get to know each other really well, sometimes they don't notice physical changes. The boy did not see that his friend, the old lady, was getting weaker and weaker because all the time he was talking to her heart, rather than to her face.
essence: the most important quality of sth., the thing that makes sth. what it is
Notice that "to talk to sb.'s essence" is not a common expression, but the author can't think of a better word, that's why he says "as it were", which is used to describe sth. in a way that is not quite accurate.

26. I eyed it as I went to the refrigerator. (para. 28)
to eye sth./sb.: to observe or watch narrowly, often in suspicion, jealousy, or in longing
e.g. The policeman eyed me in suspicion. I think he thought I was drunk.
He eyed me up and down, not knowing whether he should believe my story.
as: during the time when, while (一面……一面……)

27. My mother was regarding me with a strange gentleness. (para. 29)
My mother was looking at me with a strange gentleness because she wanted to break the sad news gently so that I would not take it too hard.

28. ... and I could tell she was having difficulty. (para. 30)
... and I could see that she was having difficulty finding a suitable way to break the news.

29. ... thanks to her help. (para. 35)
thanks to: because of
Notice that this is sometimes used ironically:
Now thanks to the rain, we'll have to put off our match.
Thanks to his brilliant leadership, our company is now bankrupt.
She failed again, all thanks to you!

30. It is a wisdom tutored by nature itself, about the seen and the unseen, about things that change and things that are changeless, and about the fact that no matter how seemingly different two souls may be, they possess the potential for that most precious, rare thing—an enduring and rewarding friendship. (para. 37)
I learn a lot of knowledge, taught by nature itself, about the things I can see—the birds, insects, trees, and flowers, and the things I cannot see—ideas, scientific laws and principles. I also learn a lot about the things that change, including life itself, as well as the things that are changeless like friendship, love, and many basic values.

### III Translation of Text A

熊树林的智慧

迈克尔·威尔巴·赫

我12岁时，全家搬到英国居住。这已经是我这短短12年中第四次大搬家了。父亲在政府部门供职，他的工作要求他每隔几年就出国，所以我已习惯了一次次不得不和朋友们离别的滋味。

我们在伯克郡租了一套建于18世纪的农舍。附近有古老的城堡和教堂。但是因为我热爱自然，所以我特别为我家周围无边无际的农田和林地所陶醉。我家背后篱笆外有一片大树林。树林深处的小路四通八达。当你向林中走去时，不时有雉鸡突然跃起，钻进密密的月桂树丛里。

大多时候，我独自在树林和田地里漫步——就像当年的罗宾汉，做孩子的白日梦，或是收集各种甲虫，观察各种鸟类。这里真是一个男孩的乐园——虽然我是孤独的。我从来独往，不和别人建立特别亲密的关系，因为这样就可以避免再次搬家时不得不又要和朋友离别之苦。但是有一天，我却意外地与一个人成为好朋友。

我们那时在英国已经住了6个多月了，老农场主克劳福德这才允许我在他巨大的庄园里漫步。于是我开始每个周末徒步去那儿，沿着一条长长的斜坡，登上小山里一片密不透风的树林，名叫熊树林。那是我的秘密堡垒，我觉得对我来说那是一个几乎神圣的地方。只要从那条铁丝网的篱笆溜进去，我便可以将骄阳、昆虫和鸟儿的鸣叫声都隔绝在外面，进入另外一个世界——一个有树干做支柱、多年棕色针铺成柔软地毯的拱顶大教堂。四周安静得我可以听见自己的呼吸声。树林里发生的任何轻微响动都会在我这个天堂里回荡。

一个春天的下午，我在树林里的某个地方漫游，因为一周前，我在那里似乎看到过一个池塘。于是，我向那个位置悄悄地前进，生怕惊动哪只小鸟高声尖叫，吓得林中小动物都躲起来。

也许正是我的蹑手蹑脚，当我差点撞上迎面而来的一位瘦弱老太太的时候，我们俩都吓了一大跳。她一下屏住了呼吸，本能地用手碰了一下喉咙。但很快她就恢复了正常，朝我微笑了一下表示见到我很高兴，这让我立刻放松下来。她脖子上挂着一副看起来倍数很高的双筒望远镜。“你好，年轻人，”她说道。“你是美国人还是加拿大人？”

美国人，我赶紧解释道。我住在山的那一边，我刚才是想看看这里是不是有个池塘。是农场主克劳福德先生让我进来的。不过我刚好要回家，所以再见吧。

正当我转身要走的时候，这位老太太笑了笑。她问我，“你今天看到林子那边的小猫头鹰了吗？”她向林子的尽头指了指。

她怎么知道那些猫头鹰？我觉得十分惊奇。

“没有，”我回答道。“我以前看到过，但是从来没有近距离观察。它们总是先看到我就飞走了。”

老太太笑了，“是的，它们很警惕，”她说道。“不过它们来到这里后，守林人就一直在捕杀它们。你知道它们是从国外传进来的，不是本地的物种。”

“不是本地的鸟？”我惊奇地问道。凡是知道这种事情的人都了不起，虽然她私自闯入我的秘密领地。

“哦，不是！”她笑道，“我家里有很多关于鸟类的书，解释各种关于鸟的问题。事实上，”她突然说道，“我刚好正想回家喝茶，吃果酱馅饼。你愿意和我一起来吗？”

我常常被警告不要说跟陌生人走。但是不知为什么，我感觉这位老太太是不会有意意的。“好的，”我说道。

“我是罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太。”她自我介绍道，伸出了她纤细的手。

“我叫迈克尔，”我说道，笨拙地握住了她的手。

我们便向她家走去。在路上，她告诉我她大约在十年前，她和当大学教授的丈夫两人是如何一起搬到伯克郡住的。“他去年去世了，”她说道，脸上露出思念之情。“所以我现在一个人住，有的是时间在田野里散步。”

很快我便看见一栋夕阳西下中粉红色的小砖房。罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太打开房门，邀请我入内。我静静凝视着眼前的书架和装着象牙和石头雕刻的玻璃门柜子、装满各种化石标本的贮藏柜和一只盘盘用大头针钉着的蝴蝶标本，我简直惊呆了。尤其是那许多鸟类标本，最为奇妙了，其中有一只包括用玻璃作为眼睛的雕塑。

“哇！”我只能以此来表达我的惊讶。

“你妈妈让你几点回家？”她一边打开水龙头准备烧茶水一边问道。

“她说，”我撒了个谎。然后我又瞟了一眼钟，又说道，“也许五点以前吧。”这样来说，我就有几乎一小时的时间。当然，要把屋里每样东西都问明白，一小时是远远不够的。但是我在喝茶、吃馅饼的时候从罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太那里学到了不少知识。

一小时的时间过得太快了。事实上，罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太几乎不得不把我“推”出小屋。但她让我带了两大本书回家，一本是满是美丽插图的有关鸟类的书，另一本是关于蝴蝶和其他昆虫的。我答应她下一个周末归还它们——如果她不介意我再次造访的话。她微笑着说她会等着我再去。

就这样，我交到了世界上最好的朋友。

当我还书的时候，她又借了我更多的书。不久，我几乎每个周末都要去她那儿。我对于大自然方面知识的了解也就这样大大增长起来。在学校里，我得到了个“教授”的绰号，也赢得了同学们的许多尊敬。甚至有一次，学校里一个喜欢欺负别人的小恶棍特地带了一只他拉到或者很可能他要打死的死鸟，来让我鉴定。

那年夏天，我和我的朋友度过了很多非常愉快的日子。我发现她能做世界上最好吃的烤饼。我们会一边在熊树林里探险，一边快乐地吃着烤饼、讨论她借给我的书中的内容。下午我们会回到小屋，她就会跟我讲她丈夫的故事——他是多么好的一个人！有几次，她说着说着似乎就要流泪了，便迅速走出房间，说是去沏茶。但她总会带着微笑回来。

时间一天天过去了，可是我竟然没有注意到她已经越来越虚弱，笑得也越来越少了。当人与人之间变得熟悉时，身体上的变化往往被忽视了，因为人们开始用心灵对话——用本质的东西在交流，而不是停留在表面。我虽然能猜到她十分孤独，但我不知道她还身患重病。

假期结束回到学校后，我的身体长得很快。我踢球提高了，也结识了一位好朋友。但是我仍然在周末拜访罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太，她也依然用烤饼招待我。一天早上，当我下楼去厨房的时候，我发现桌上有一个十分眼熟的饼干罐。

我一面打量它，一面走到冰箱边。

这时，我母亲用一种特别温和的眼神看着我。“儿子，”她悲痛地说道。听着她悲伤的语调，我立刻明白了一切。

她把手放在饼干罐上。“这是克劳福德太太早上带来的。”她停了一会儿，我能够看出她说话有些困难。“这是罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太留给你的。”

我凝视着窗外，泪水刺痛了我的眼睛。

“我很难过，迈克尔，罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太昨天去世了。”她继续说道。“她已经很老了，而且病得很重，她是到了离开我们的时候了。”

母亲用手搂着我的肩膀。“你让她很开心，因为她很寂寞。”她说道。“你能成为她的好朋友，也很幸运。”

我静静地拿着饼干罐进了自己的房间，将它放在床上。然后，我快速地跑下楼去，冲出大门，向树林跑去。

我在树林里走了很久，直到我停止流泪，视线恢复清晰。那是一个春日，从我在这里遇到罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太算起差不多刚好过了一年。我环顾四周，发现我在罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太的帮助下，我掌握的有关大自然——关于鸟类、昆虫、植物和树木的知识增加了很多。然后我想起来卧室里有世界上最好吃的松饼。我应该回去品尝它，就如周末在罗伯逊-格拉斯基太太家里一样。

随着时间流逝，那个饼干罐被我装满了各种干树叶、化石标本、彩色的石子和其他零碎物。我今天仍然保存着它。

但我很久以前在熊树林里的这次巧遇给我留下的远不止这些。这是一种大自然熏陶下获得的智慧——它让我们了解到那些可见或是不可见的事物，以及不断变化或永远不变的现象。它也告诉我们，无论两个生命表面看来有多么不同，

## （续）

他们仍然可能拥有一种珍贵的礼物——一份持久、并且有益的友谊。

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

stand (impenetrable stand of trees, para. 4)
stuff (stuffed birds, para. 18)
run (ran the water for tea, para. 20)
well (my well of knowledge, para. 24)

2.

trays of pinned butterflies (para. 18)
I eyed it as I went to the refrigerator. (para. 28)

3.

1. Verb phrases

burst through (the door), dangle from, go off with sb., go overseas, invite sb. in, keep to oneself, look forward to, move to (a place), pass away, put one's arm about sb.'s shoulder, put sb. at ease, rest one's hand on sth., ring in one's ears, roam about, rocket off, run into sb., set off, slip through, verge against (some place), warn sb. against (doing) sth., wrench oneself away from somewhere, fill up with sth., be inclined to do sth., be used to (doing) sth., be introduced (into a place)

2. Prepositional phrases

back at school, in a rush, in time, on weekends, through no design of one's own, with tree trunks for pillars, at ease, between sth. and sth. else

3. Other phrases

as it were, every few years, odds and ends, a network of, rather than, thanks to

4.

abandon friends, accumulate wealth, alarm a bird, catch one's breath, collect bugs, earn a nickname, extend one's hand, form attachments, hide the truth, make a friend, make tea, munch food, play Robin Hood, rent a farmhouse, sting one's eyes, surround the house, walk the fields, roam the woods, give permission, warn people, glimpse a pond, startle sb., stuff a bag with, regard sb., explore a place, eye sb., possess the potential

5.

1. My father's government job demanded that he go overseas every few years ... (para. 1)
demanded that he go: (NOT "went" or "goes") subjunctive mood
2. Loving nature, however, I was most delighted by ... farms and woodland ... (para. 2)
loving nature, however: however, as/because I loved nature
present participle used as adverbial of reason
Cf. I gazed about in silent admiration at the bookshelves, glass-fronted cases containing figures of ivory and carved stone ... (para. 18)
present participle used as attributive modifiers
3. We had been in England for about six months when old farmer Crawford gave me permission to ... (para. 4)
"when" here indicates "just after which," suggesting that the permission was given after we had been in England for about six months.
4. ... a vaulted cathedral, with tree trunks for pillars and ... brown needles for a softly carpeted floor. (para. 4)
for: serving as, here used to suggest that the tree trunks are like pillars and the needles are like a floor
5. It is a wisdom tutored by nature itself, about the seen and the unseen ... (para. 37)
the + adjective = plural noun
"The" is often used before an adjective to form a plural noun when you are referring to people of a particular type or a particular country. More examples: the rich, the poor, the unemployed, the oppressed, the British, the Japanese.

6.

1. Use of metaphor

... the endless patchwork of farms and woodland ... (para. 2)
... a vaulted cathedral, with tree trunks for pillars and ... brown needles for a softly carpeted floor. (para. 4)

2. Use of onomatopoeia (象声词)

... I'd leave the bright sun and the twitter and rustle of insects and animals outside ... (para. 4)

3. Vivid descriptions that create a picture with words

In this text we can find a number of beautiful descriptive sentences that create a picture in the readers' minds by appealing to their senses of sight, taste, smell, touch, and sound, as well as internal emotion and feelings. For example:
Soon I saw a small brick cottage that glowed pinkly in the westering sun. (para. 18)
In para. 4, the description of Bear Wood creates a mental picture of a secluded, private paradise by appealing to the reader's senses of sight ("impenetrable stand of trees," "vaulted cathedral," "with tree trunks for pillars"), touch ("Slipping through a barbed-wire fence," "softly carpeted floor"), and sound ("my own breathing rang in my ear").

4. I have gained a lot of knowledge, taught by nature itself, about the things I can see—the birds, insects, trees, and flowers, and the things I cannot see—ideas, scientific laws, and principles. I have also learned a lot about the things that change, including life itself, as well as the things that are changeless like friendship, love, and many basic values. I have also learned that no matter how different two people seem to be, it is possible for them to form a lasting and valuable friendship, to be cherished for their life time.

# Unit 8

## Text A · The Man in Asbestos · Stephen Leacock

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

To better understand this story, it is important to remember that it was written in 1911 by a Canadian professor of political economy at McGill University, who is actually better known for his humorous and satirical writings. Stephen Leacock, who might be called a dystopian, had serious doubts about his opposites—the utopians, who tended to paint rosy pictures of the world's future and believed that human wisdom would give us happy solutions to all our problems. Leacock did not share the beautiful dreams of the utopians. He did not believe machines, industrialization, and scientific and technological developments can automatically lead us to a paradise on Earth. Like George Orwell's 1984 and Animal Farm, The Man in Asbestos paints humanity's future in darkly pessimistic colors.

In this "New Society" presented by Leacock, there is no hunger, no disease, no death, no crimes, no gender inequality, no natural disasters. But life is dull, empty, meaningless, and people are miserable.

Naturally, Leacock's views meet with very strong objections. Social reformers and left-wing people are angry that such incredible human achievements are shown as negative and bleak. They say that Leacock seemed to be implying that such momentous problems as hunger, war, disease, natural disasters and inequality are what gives life interest, fullness and purpose; in other words, these are the things that make us human. If that were the case, it would mean that they should not be eliminated. Such reasoning can only be the justification of the status quo and a plea for the end of human progress. It can only appeal to people in positions of privilege and personal comfort.

But perhaps we should not judge Leacock's views too hastily. This story does raise a thought-provoking question. In our battle against human scourges, we sometimes forget that a world of complete bliss is impossible. Human beings are always going to have problems. When old problems are resolved, new problems are bound to occur. And to attempt to solve those new problems, human beings have to again make painstaking efforts. Difficulties, hardships, risks, suffering, pain, anxiety, failure and sacrifice will always be part of the human experience. Indeed, perfection is not only impossible, it is also undesirable, because "perfection" means the end of change, and the end of change means the end of progress. And that would be the worst problem for human beings.

Scholars have expressed the concern that science has been busy finding answers, but it has often neglected to ask the right questions. No doubt, the "new society" presented by Leacock is considered a paradise by the Man in Asbestos. The problem is: People do not have the same definition of the word "paradise".

History has told us how important it is to guard against utopians. It is because the beautiful promises of utopians tend to disarm us easily and lure us to seek our own destruction. The history of human civilization has given us many unforgettable examples which show that "The Road to Hell Is Often Paved with Good Intentions." Tremendous human sufferings in history have been caused by those who often sincerely try to make things better. Freedom, equality, progress, longevity, science, machines...—these are all good things, but we do not always know what they really mean. And we often forget that they can all be double-edged swords.

Therefore, do not let us dismiss the warnings of people like Leacock lightly. After all, we have nothing to lose if the warnings of Leacock or Orwell turn out to be unwarranted false alarms whereas they would feel happy if their warnings have not fallen on deaf ears.

Note:
A dystopia is a fictional society, usually described as existing in a future time, when the conditions of life are extremely bad due to deprivation, oppression, or terror. Science fiction writers like to use this idea. Social critics, especially postmodern social critics, also use the term "dystopian" to condemn trends in post-industrial society they see as negative. In most dystopian fiction, a corrupt government creates or sustains the poor quality of life, often conditioning the masses to believe the society is proper and just, even perfect. Most dystopian fiction takes place in the future but often purposely incorporates contemporary social trends taken to extremes. Dystopias are frequently written as warnings, or as satires, showing current trends leading to a nightmarish conclusion.

#### 2 Structure

The first six paragraphs (1–6) form the introduction of the essay, which explains why the narrator eagerly plunged into a distant future to witness its marvels. The body paragraphs (7–84) narrate what he discovered in this future world and how his feelings about it changed from astonishment and thrill to doubt, and finally to disgust and rejection. In the brief conclusion (paras. 85–87), the narrator found himself back in the old world.

I. The narrator plunged into a distant future to witness its marvels. (paras. 1–6)

II. What the narrator witnessed there helped him make up his mind to return to the old world. (paras. 7–84)

1. He was astonished at a few of the marvels. (paras. 7–28)
2. He felt thrilled at the Conquest of Nature. (paras. 29–59)
3. He began to doubt whether the Conquest was a blessing. Finally, he became angry at all the marvels and rejected the world of marvels. (paras. 60–84)

III. The narrator woke up to find himself in his hotel room. (paras. 85–87)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. This text is the abridged version of the 4,124-word original. It has left out lots of interesting details because the original is much too long for our purpose. And yet we hate to drop this piece. We are quite aware that it is an insult to the author to tamper with his literary creation. We can only hope that he will forgive us. Here we print the original for the benefit of teachers. It is up to them whether it should be given to students as hand-out material.
2. Let students discuss this allegory of the future. Encourage them to give examples of how utopian ideas led to human disasters.
3. Encourage students to criticize Stephen Leacock. Does the elimination of hunger necessarily mean to use the pill and forgo all the pleasures of eating? Does the elimination of gender inequality require the elimination of sex, love, all gender differences? Do we have to kill all weather changes in order to conquer natural disasters?
4. Can we give a clear-cut moral judgment of utopia and dystopia, and say one is completely good and the other is completely wrong?

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. To begin with, let me admit that I did it partly from jealousy. (para. 1)
To begin with: First of all, First
I did it: It refers to the practice of dropping into a sleep of four or five hundred years.
from jealousy: out of jealousy. Compare all these prepositions or prepositional phrases that may be used to express a reason:
because of, due to, for the sake of, owing to, out of, from, with (His face was blue with cold.), for (She cried for joy.)

2. It seemed unfair that other writers should be able at will to drop into a sleep of four or five hundred years, and to plunge headfirst into a distant future and be a witness of its marvels. (para. 2)
Questions: Where did you get the clue to the author's identity? And where did you first notice that it was science fiction?
to plunge headfirst into: to go right into... without hesitation
be a witness of its marvels: and see the wonderful changes that would have taken place during this long time

3. ... the very existence of the outer world was hushed. (para. 6)
... the world around us had become silent and still.

4. ... the long passage of the centuries. (para. 6)
passage: Here it denotes the action of the verb "to pass" as also in phrases like: the passage of the time; the passage of the law. Note how it is used in different contexts in the following sentences:
This is the most dramatic passage in his famous essay.
There is a secret passage underneath leading to all the strategic points.
In these sentences, the word "passage" becomes something concrete, a countable noun, and the meaning is also changed.
More examples:
Due to poor management (经营管理) the bank was rated C last year.
The company management (管理人员) were totally corrupt.
Nobody ever suspected his belonging to that secret gang.
Her personal belongings remained untouched.
The publication of the book was regarded as a great cultural event.
You won't be able to get full professorship without publications.
Government control may not be desirable. But government regulation is absolutely necessary.
According to the new government regulations, foreign investors can own more than 50% of the stocks of a company.

5. ... I woke up, and looked about me. (para. 7)
... I woke up, and looked around me.

6. Beside me sat a man ... but with no particular surprise or interest. (para. 10)
Questions: What kind of a man did the author see when he reopened his eyes centuries later? What did the descriptions of the man show?
Why was the man's face hairless? Why is it that the author seemed unable to determine his age? How would you explain the man's total lack of interest in everything?

7. Don't you keep track of them any more? (para. 15)
keep track of: to keep informed of sb./sth.
Questions: The man in asbestos obviously did not bother to keep time. Why? How would you explain the different attitudes towards time?

8. "Stop," I said, my brain reeling. "Tell me one thing at a time." (para. 19)
my brain reeling: My head seemed to be moving round and round in a whirling motion. I felt dizzy, bewildered, completely puzzled.
one thing at a time: one by one

9. "You are in a museum. The figures in the cases are specimens like yourself. But here," he said, "I'll... come out on Broadway." (para. 25)
the figures in the cases: the figures in the boxes. These human beings were obviously treated as museum pieces, or specimens.
But here: But listen; But look
Broadway: See Notes to the Text. Like Wangfujing in Beijing, this name usually evokes the image of a great business hub.

10. ... I stood riveted in astonishment. (para. 26)
... I stood still as if fastened to the ground with a rivet (铆钉).

11. In place of the roaring thoroughfare that I had known, this silent, moss-grown desolation... There were no wires overhead—no sound of life or movement except, here and there, there passed slowly to and fro... the same look of infinite age upon them. (para. 27)
in place of: instead of
roaring thoroughfare: very lively or thriving main street
moss-grown desolation: a wasteland covered with moss
no wires: no electric wires
to and fro: back and forth

12. ... the era of the conquest... (para. 28)
It refers to man's conquest of nature. Up until very recently, people still believed that by relying on science and technology, we would be able to make nature serve our needs; we would conquer nature as its master.

13. ... humanity was destined to move forward. (para. 28)
Based on Darwinian theories of evolution, through struggle for survival and natural selection, people believe that humanity is sure/certain/bound to move forward, since the inevitable law is the survival of the fittest, progress is not only desirable, it is inevitable.
destined: (of a person's future) regarded as developing as though according to a pre-existing plan
Compare: destiny, destination

14. ... rendered me almost speechless. (para. 28)
... made me almost speechless.

15. "Oh, done away with long ago," he said. (para. 31)
done away with: eliminated; wiped out; got rid of

16. But how do you get about? (para. 32)
But how do you move around?

17. I tried to pull my senses together. (para. 38)
I tried to calm down and think.

18. ... I must go at it systematically. (para. 38)
... I must do/work at/deal with it in an orderly way.

19. ... that momentous things have happened since my time. (para. 39)
momentous: important, significant, decisive, earthshaking, epoch-making, far-reaching

20. ... It died out of itself. (para. 40)
Make sure that the students know where to pause.
died out: ceased existing completely
of itself: by itself

21. I nodded assent. (para. 41)
I nodded in agreement.

22. You were all caught in the cogs of your own machines. (para. 42)
You could not escape the consequences of your industrialization. / You became the slave of your own machines.

23. ... just as soon as mankind... (para. 46)
Words like "mankind", "chairman" are considered sexist. The preferred words today are "humanity" and "chairperson".

24. Agriculture went overboard. (para. 46)
Agriculture died out.
to go overboard: to fall over the side of the boat into the water

25. Eating and all that goes with it... (para. 46)
Eating and everything related to it...
Questions: How was the Conquest of Nature realized? Do you like Chemical Food and Asbestos Clothes?

26. ... the revolt of women and the fall of Fashion. (para. 48)
Here "the revolt of women" is referring to women's liberation movement.
Question: What was the author's general attitude toward fashion?

27. ... my old-time harangues,... and I stopped. (para. 48)
harangue: speech of strong feeling, usu. delivered before a gathering
sheer: pure; nothing but
decorative dress: dress not to keep one warm but to make one look beautiful, fashionable clothes.
Question: Why did the author suddenly check himself?

28. ... we killed the changes of climate. (para. 50)
Question: What had the men in asbestos done to the climate?

29. ... the texture of life. (para. 60)
... the kind of life or the quality of life.
texture: It originally refers to the arrangement of the threads in a textile fabric. Here it means the distinctive or identifying character.

30. Well, we hunted them down... (para. 67)
We searched until we found them (one by one)...
down: adv. to its source
More examples:
They decided to track the rumor down.
He was young and strong. It took only 10 minutes for him to run the drug dealer down.

31. I've heard about your arrangements with the women... (para. 80)
He was of course referring to marriage. But because he either did not know about love and marriage or did not care, he did not know how to describe this relationship.

32. Yes, but give a rest to the rest of the corridor! (para. 85)
This is a play on the word "rest". "The rest of the corridor" obviously refers to the other people who live on that floor of the hotel.

### III Translation of Text A

穿石棉衣服的人

斯蒂芬·利科克

首先，我要承认，我这么做，部分原因是出于嫉妒。

我觉得，别的作家都能够随心所欲地睡上四五百年，然后直接进入遥远的未来，体验那时各种神奇的事物，而我不能。这太不公平了。

我也要这么做。

我为这次长眠作了充分的准备。

在某种意义上，这干脆就是直截了当的自杀。但是我就这么做了。

我感觉到自己在慢慢失去知觉。我进入了深不可测的梦乡。周围万籁俱寂。我迷迷糊糊地感到日子一天天过去，然后一年一年，最后是一个世纪。

然后，我就突然醒来了。我往四下里望。

我这是在哪里啊？

我发现自己好像在一个博物馆的房子里，坐在一张宽敞的长榻上。

在我身边，还坐着一个人，他脸光滑无色，看不出多大年纪。身上穿的衣服好像是纸糊的，给烧成灰以后仍然还保持着原来的形状。此人正在默默地望着我，既没有显得惊奇，也没有表示丝毫兴趣。

“快！”我迫不及待地问道。“我现在是在什么地方？你是谁？”

“你怎么用这样激动的怪腔怪调，”这人显得有些不太高兴。

“请告诉我，”我又说，“今年是不是公元3000年？”

“我一无所知，”他回答道。

“难道你们不再记载时间了吗？”我吃惊地倒抽了一口气。

“我们曾经记载过，”那人说道。“但后来就没有了。因为，”他接着说，“等我们消灭了死亡以后——”

“消灭死亡！”我叫道，兴奋得直坐了起来。

“我刚才说到——在我们消灭死亡和食物以及——”

“等等！”我说，这时我的脑子都晕了。“请您一件一件地讲。”

他突然哼了一声，说道，“我明白了，您肯定是睡了很长时间了。好吧，您有问题就问吧。”

奇怪的是，我突然想问的第一个问题是——

“您身上穿的衣服是什么做的？”

“石棉，”那人回答说。

“谢谢，”我回答道。“现在请告诉我，我现在在哪里？”

“您现在在一个博物馆里。那些橱窗里的人都是像您一样的展品。看来，”他说，“如果你想了解对我来说显然是一个全新时代的情况，您就要从这展台走下来，出去到百老汇大街看看。”

我就走下了展台。可是我一走到街上，我就惊呆地站在那里，动弹不得了。百老汇！这怎么可能？这变化也太让人吃惊了！我记得以前这是一条车水马龙的通衢大道，可现在成了沉寂无声、长满青苔、一片荒凉的地方。那些大楼都倒塌成为堆堆瓦砾。整个地方鸦雀无声。没有一辆车在动。头上的电线也都不见了——没有任何生命的迹象和会动的东西。只有三两两来来回回慢吞吞走动、穿着和我认识的那个人一样的石棉衣服的一些人，一个个没有毛发，脸上都同样显出一种经历过无穷岁月的样子。

我的天哪！难道这就是我一直朝思暮想、期盼能见到的人类征服一切以后的新时代？我一直以为，人类总是会不断向前进步。可是现在眼前这幅荒凉的图景使我简直哑口无言了。

我喘着气问了一个问题。

“请问那些电车和汽车到到哪里去了？”

“早就打发了，”他说。

“那你们怎么走动呢？”

“我们不走动，”他回答道。“我们为什么要走动？在这里和在其他什么地方没有什么区别。”

这时，我脑中一下涌起了千百个问题。我先问了个最简单的。

“那你们怎么上下班工作呢？”

“工作！”他说。“工作已经不存在了。”

我张大了嘴朝他看了一会儿。

我努力想使自己头脑冷静下来。我现在意识到，如果要想弄清楚这个做梦也没有想到过的新的未来，我就要系统地来了解。

“我懂了，”我停了停，说，“自从我那个年代到现在，已经发生了巨大的变化。我希望您能一点一点地给我解释。首先，您说工作已经不存在了，是什么意思？”

“很简单，”我新交的朋友回答说，“它自己就消失了。是机器把它给消灭了。假如我没有记错的话，就在您那个时代就有一些机器了，是吧？”

我点了点头。

“但是你们发现那些机器对你们没什么好处。机器越好，你们干活就越辛苦。你们生产的财富越多，你们要的东西也就越多。你们都受困于这些机器，动弹不得。”

“您说得一点也不错，”我说。

“后来，就到了征服自然的伟大时代，人和机器取得了最终的胜利。”

“这么说，他们真的把自然征服了？”我马上问他，当年的希望，重新使我血脉贲张起来。

“征服了，”他说，“把它彻底打败了！之后，很多东西就一一出现了。一百年之内，一切都解决了。实际上，当人类一把他们的精力从扩大欲望转向减少需要，一切就立刻变得十分容易了。先是化学食品。我的天，这简直简单得不能再简单了。这一来，农业就没有了，吃饭，以及与此有关的家务劳动，一切的一切，都没有了。现在，每个人只要一年服一粒浓缩丸，就全妥了。”

我不得不打断他：“您的意思是你们没有胃了？”

“当然有，”他说，“但我们的胃作用不同。且听我往下说。我刚才先说化学食品，这就减少了大约三分之二的工作。这以后就是石棉衣服了。就一年中的时间，人类就做了足够的这种衣服，永远穿不完了。当然这都和妇女们以及她们不再追求时髦和婚姻相关，不然，这是永远不可能的。”

“现在人们不赶时髦了，”我问道，“不讲那种愚蠢的铺张浪费的——？”

我当时正想再一遍反对那种纯粹为了追求时髦的老话，但我突然见到那些正在走动的穿着石棉衣服的人，我就忍住没有讲。

“都没有了，”那个穿石棉衣的人说。“这以后，我们把气候变化也给它消灭了。我相信你们那个时代，你们并不知道你们有多少工作劳累是和你们所谓的气候变化有关的。它意味着需要各种各样的特别的衣服和能挡风霜雨雪的房屋建筑。那都是一大堆令人头昏目眩的工作。你们那时候日子肯定是够可怕的——不是风就是雨，空中的乌云乱飞，海洋给风搅得白浪滔天，地上一切都让冰雪盖得严严实实。”

“有时候还是很美的。这么说你们消灭天气变化了？”我说。

“是的，还有燃料、房屋以及所有与之相关的劳动都没了。”

他停了一会儿。

“这么说来，”我说，“征服自然的意思就是现在已经没事可做了？”

“一点不错，”他说，“无事可做了。”

“人人都有饭吃？”

“根本吃不完，”他回答说。

“房子、衣服？”

“要多少有多少。”

这时我才第一次明白工作在以前生活中的意义，明白我们那时生活的质量和内涵有多少是和我们生活的热切追求的努力完全不可分的。

过了一会，我的眼睛往上一看，发现一个建筑顶上有个东西在摆动，好像是留下来的电话线头。

“那些通信系统现在怎样了呢？”我就问道。

“哦，”那个穿石棉衣的人说，“也全都去掉了。你看，工作停顿了，商业没有了，食品不需要了，天气变化也消失了。这一来，就没有需要交流的东西了。那如果还有人要四处走动，就成傻瓜了。再说，这还有危险！”

“原来！”我说，“你们还有危险？”

“对，有啊，”他说。

“这意思是不是你们还要死去？”我问道。

“不，”那个石棉人说，“已经有几百年没人死了。我们把死亡给消除了。疾病和死亡其实就是细菌惹的祸。我们把细菌一个一个找了出来，统统消灭了。”

“那您的意思是，”我马上吃惊地问道，“现在你们能永远活下去？”

“不错，”他说，“当然，除非我们受伤。所以我们采取措施，把一切事故都消灭了。我们为此禁止一切电车、车辆、飞机等交通工具的使用。”

我们默默地坐着。我望着四周随时可倒塌的建筑，头上永远不变的天空，和那阴沉沉空荡的街道。这么说，原来这就是人类征服自然的结果，消灭工作，结束饥饿和寒冷，停止一切艰苦的奋斗，消除一切变化和死亡——不，这就是幸福的千年盛世。可是，不知为什么，这一切总有点问题。我想了想，然后连着向那石棉人问了几问题，几乎都没有停下来想想他的回答。

“现在还有战争吗？”

“几世纪以前就消除了。”

“你们还有报纸吗？”

“报纸！我们要它们干什么？报上有什么可登的呢？无非是发生的一些事情，什么战争啦，事故啦，工作啦，死亡啦。那些东西没有了，报纸也没有了。你听说，”那石棉人接着说，“您好像还是根本没懂我们的新生活。你们那个时候，开始一段时间，人们做什么？”

“开始十五年左右，”我说，“我们一般用来上学受教育。”

“不错，”他回答道，“现在请你注意我们是如何对此加以改进的。我们现在的教育是用外科手术来完成的。奇怪的。你们那时候，竟无人认识到，教育只需要一个外科手术，把头脑盖打开，植入一片事先准备好的脑子就行了。事实上我们最近发现很多事情都没有必要用到头脑，用消化器官就够了。”

他停顿了一会儿，然后接着说道。

下面我们接着说，那受完教育以后的时间又忙什么呢？

“忙什么？”我说，“一个人当然得工作，然后，说实话，很多时间和感情是投到异性身上的，就是谈恋爱，找一个能和你共同生活的女人。”

“哦，”那石棉人说，“我听说过你们和女人做出的这种安排，但我从来没有明白是什么意思。”

这时，我才突然想到，街上的那些人，看起来都是一个模样。

“告诉我，”我说，“现在是不是没有女人了？她们是不是也给你们清除掉了？”

“不，不，”那个石棉人回答道，“她们还在这里。你看见的这些人中，有些就是女的。只是，您知道，一切都已经改变了。这一切都和她们当年的女权运动有关，都是因为她们都强烈要求和男人一样。”

“原来，这就是你们理想的天堂，”我愤怒地对他说。“这个毫无趣味、没有生气的东西，生活上丢掉了工作和与之有关的负担，同时也也就没有了所有的快乐和甜蜜。原来的奋斗，现在变成停滞；以前的危险和死亡，现在换来了单调的所谓安全和可怕的永不停息的衰竭！把你们破坏的东西还给我吧，”我叫喊道，“那就把过去充满危险和压力的生活，包括那艰苦的劳动，难受的变化，和随之而来的各种痛苦。我不要什么平静，”我大声叫喊道——

“行，但是你也让走道里其他人安静一会吧！”一个愤怒的声音大声叫道。

这时我突然醒了。

我发现自己回到了旅馆的房间里。

## （续）

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

"Over" in "overboard" means going across from one side to another.
"Over" in "overhead" means above.

2.

1. Verb phrases

die out, do away with sth., go at sb., beat sth. out, cut out, hunt sth./sb. down, become of, turn on sb., improve on sth., look about, get about, get off; get down, get back and forth, rest on, go overboard, keep track of, plunge (headfirst) into, pull oneself together, launch into (a harangue, etc.), surge into minds, be bound up, be destined to, be made of, be eager to, be caught in the cogs, be connected with

2. Prepositional phrases

at will, in one's time, due to, in place of, in the effort of, next to, of itself, (one thing) at a time, bit by bit, for ever and ever

3. Other phrases

to begin with, to continue, to tell the truth, a matter of, a wilderness of, here and there, to and fro, back and forth

3.

1. alliteration: the repetition of the same sound at the beginning of words
More examples:
a fierce pang of pain ("The Nightingale and the Rose")
frothing and fragrant ("Christmas Day in the Morning")
bread and butter, friend and foe; cool as cucumber; cut the coat according to the cloth
2. play on the word rest, which is repeated.
More examples:
Nothing is so much to be feared as fear. —Henry David Thoreau
There are well-dressed foolish ideas just as there are well-dressed fools. —Nicolas Chamfort
Not ignorance, but ignorance of ignorance, is the death of knowledge. —Alfred North Whitehead
"Time flies like an arrow; fruit flies like a banana." —Groucho Marx
"Marriage is a fine institution (制度、习俗), but I'm not ready for an institution (我还没准备受任何制度的约束)." —Mae West

4.

1. To begin with, let me admit that I did it partly from jealousy. (para. 1)
To begin with: an infinitive phrase used adverbially before a statement, meaning at first or in the first place.
2. I was saying—(para. 18)
This is used to repeat what one has just said.
3. I said, my brain reeling. (para. 19)
Absolute construction as adverbial
More examples:
● He said yes, his eyes fixed on the floor. (past participle)
● He said yes, his eyes shining with excitement. (present participle)
● He said yes, his cap in his hand. (prepositional phrase)
● He said yes, his face red with shyness. (adjective)
4. I looked at him a moment open-mouthed. (para. 37)
Adjective as subject complement.
5. He nodded assent. (para. 41)
He nodded (this head in) assent. Note that assent here is not the object of the action verb "nod."
6. The better your machines, the harder you worked. (para. 42)
Comparative correlative clause: the more, the more ... (越……越……)
7. That, of course, could never have been if it hadn't been connected with ... (para. 48)
The past subjunctive mood indicates a contrary-to-fact past situation. In the if-clause, the imagined action is in the past perfect tense.
8. What on earth would we want them for? (para. 74)
On earth: used for emphasis in questions beginning with words such as what, how, where, and why.
9. How used your people to spend all the early part of their lives? (para. 74)
What used to occupy your time and effort after your education?
Used to: indicates a situation that existed in the past but does not exist now

#### Reading Comprehension

1.

(1) sleep
(2) plunge
(3) witness
(4) recognition
(5) nature
(6) eliminated/killed/done away with
(7) thrilled
(8) confused
(9) angry
(10) go back/return
(11) dream
(12) happened

2.

1. The busy exciting street I knew has now changed into this empty, lonely, lifeless place covered with moss.
2. You became part of the machines. You came to be controlled by the machines. (This is related to the idea of alienation caused by machines.)
3. I was on the point of starting my favorite critical speech about women's vanity for being fashionable ...
4. ... and how much of the way or pattern of our life has been closely connected with, or even determined by, our crazy pursuit of fashion.

#### Language Exercises

1.

1.
1) annoying/annoyed
2) astonished/astonishing
3) desolate
4) destined
5) stagnant
6) secure
7) simple/simplistic
8) commercial
9) devoted
10) educational
11) decorative
12) marvelous
13) jealous
14) systematic
15) favorable
16) fashionable
17) momentary/momentous
18) evident
19) infinite
20) dreadful

2.

1) 在海外
2) 追上
3) 克服
4) 忽略; 俯视
5) 偷听到
6) 反应过度
7) 估计过高
8) 超杀（指能够消灭比全球人类还要多的人）
9) 睡过头
10) 过度工作
11) 人口太多
12) 要价太高
13) 负担太重
14) 超时的

3.

1) 输入/投入和输出/产出
2) 一个社会的弃儿
3) 战争的爆发
4) 一场倾盆大雨
5) 一次严重的经济衰退
6) 下载失败
7) 选举的结果
8) 工人的平均收入
9) 对这一政策的大声抗议
10) 减少你摄入的食盐量
11) 一种新的人生观
12) 现金或技术人员的流出/流入

Note: 1–5题中的over-是“在……之上”和“从这头到那头”的意思；6–14题中的over-都是“过分的”“过多的”“过高的”之类的意思。

4.

Synonyms

1) obviously/clearly/doubtlessly/undoubtedly
2) mankind
3) ruin/destruction
4) continue/endure/live/exist/stay
5) rebellion
6) think over/consider/reflect on
7) take up/fill up
8) stopping/ceasing/stop/end
9) change
10) wipe out/kill/get rid of/do away with/knock out
11) era/age/time/period
12) infinite

Antonyms

1) finite
2) increasing
3) insane
4) economical/reasonable
5) ordinary
6) forbid
7) dissent

3.

1. You asked me what became of the missing king in the end. I haven't the faintest idea. History didn't say.
2. My brain was reeling when the voice on the phone told me that my bank savings were in danger of being stolen.
3. When he came to life, the first question that sprang to his lips was where the child was.

6. It rendered me speechless when he announced that we had lost all our investment.

8. We used to believe arrogantly that humanity was destined to conquer nature. It was just a matter of time.
9. Our countryside has changed. In place of huts and straw cottages, we now have modern buildings of glass and steel.

4. in; about/around; by; of

1. We've learned from the epidemic that everyone's fate is bound up with the fate of the world and that we should all pitch in to battle against such disasters.
2. —Sorry I cut you off just now. You were saying...
—I was saying that it is not easy to break down the resistance of those conservatives. But we must go at them step by step.
3. It's widely held that continued dependence on coal and oil is the major cause of global warming and more extreme weather. Recent years therefor have seen a shift to green energy in many countries.

# Unit 9

## Text A · Pompeii · Robert Silverberg

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

Vesuvius is probably the most famous volcano in the world and the most observed by scientists. A bibliography compiled in 1918 listed more than 2,000 books and articles on Vesuvius, and many more have been written since then. Geological evidence indicates that the volcano has been active for ten thousand years. Before its eruption in A.D. 79, however, it had been dormant for several centuries, and the early Greeks and Romans did not think of it as a dangerous volcano. In Roman times the area was a popular seaside resort where many people had summer homes. In the 1st century B.C., the geographer Strabo reported terrace farming on the slopes of the mountain but described the summit as barren with ash. Spartacus, the leader of a slave revolt during the same period, took refuge in the crater of Vesuvius when he and his followers were being pursued by the Romans.

Pompeii was rediscovered in 1748 but systematic excavation did not begin until 1860. About 2,000 skeletons were found in hollows left in the ash by the bodies of the victims. The archaeologists poured plaster of Paris into these hollows and so made plaster casts of men, women, children and domestic animals, looking exactly as they had been at the moment of death. Many of them had their hands or clothes in their mouths, indicating that they were trying to protect themselves from the poisonous gas. Several were found clutching bags of coins and other valuables.

We know that natural disasters have often been reduced to mere statistics. But over the years people have also shown intense interest in them. For one thing, natural disasters cause a lot of human tragedy, and human tragedy has always been a deep concern of human beings. Second, during a natural disaster, human beings usually reveal their true colors. We see ourselves at our best as well as at our worst. Third, the careful study of natural disasters may reveal scientific laws which may eventually help man discover ways to deal with natural disasters better. Fourth, natural disasters are often linked with human errors, and therefore can lead to our better understanding of our mistakes and shortcomings. Fifth, the memory of natural disasters can always remind us that natural power is still much greater than human power, and it is stupid arrogance on our part to think that we can conquer nature. Man should forever be humble and learn to respect nature's ways, and learn whatever other useful lessons from them.

What is unique about this particular natural disaster is the fact that the eruption destroyed a Roman city more than 2,000 years ago, but now the buried city is returned to us almost intact. From the ash and rubble of the city we now see what this Roman city was like with every detail. Among the 2,000 dead bodies we see men and women; old and young; rich and poor. We see gladiators, tourists, merchants, bakers. We see all kinds of buildings, including a brothel, and a bakery where there were still 81 loaves of bread left at the time of eruption. And in the buildings, we see furniture used at the time, domestic animals, and frescoes. No wonder Pompeii has become one of the most popular historical museums in the world. The present text is condensed from a much longer article that appeared in National Geographic many years ago. But even this much shortened version serves as a vivid introduction to this great archeological discovery.

#### 2 Structure

The selection can be divided into two parts. The first 7 paragraphs provide a brief account of Pompeii before and after the eruption of A.D. 79. The rest (paras. 8–20) accounts what happened on the day of the eruption.

I. A brief account of Pompeii before and after August 24, A.D. 79 (paras. 1–7)

1. Pompeii is a dead city. (paras. 1–2)
2. It used to be a prosperous city until a volcanic eruption buried it deep in A.D. 79. (paras. 3–4)
3. It was discovered again 1700 years later and has now been restored to what it was like the day of the eruption. (paras. 5–7)

II. A detailed description of what happened on the day of the eruption (paras. 8–20)

1. In the morning, disaster was on its way. (paras. 8–9)
2. The eruption began at one o'clock with a loud explosion. (paras. 10–13)
3. In the afternoon, buildings began to collapse and houses caught fire. (paras. 14–15)
4. In the evening an earthquake struck, killing hundreds of people. (para. 16)
5. Poisonous gas killed more people. (paras. 17–18)
6. By midday on August 25, Pompeii became a city of the dead and was buried deep. (paras. 19–20)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. Let students get hold of a map of Europe and locate the geographical names that appear in the text such as Italy, the Mediterranean Sea, Rome, the Bay of Naples, Naples, Vesuvius, and Pompeii.
2. Encourage students to explore more information online about the volcanic eruption that destroyed Pompeii in A.D. 79 and have them report their findings to the class.
3. There is a powerful movie called The Last Days of Pompeii, which described what the Romans were like and how they met their death, based on the unearthed artifacts. Try to get hold of the movie and let students watch it after class.
4. Encourage students to imitate the text and give a dramatic account of some of the most recent natural disasters that have taken place in China, and ask them what they regard as the most unforgettable aspects of those tragic events.
5. Draw students' attention to how the author recreates the horror of the eruption by using action verbs, many of which contain consonant clusters with specific sound effects such as: clatter, topple, puzzle, stumble, huddle. In addition, the sentences he uses are usually short or broken up by commas—a device that creates tension and drama. The text also contains many synonyms for describing activities. Take for example the act of running. This is expressed in a wide range of words such as dashing, rushing, plunging, fleeing, heading toward, moving slowly, making one's way toward, etc.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. A.D. 79 (para. 2)

in full: anno Domini, Latin for "in the year of our Lord". The Christian myth has it that A.D. 1 was the year Jesus Christ was born (He was probably born years earlier). Time before A.D. is called B.C., i.e., Before Christ.

2. ... Pompeii was a prosperous city of 25,000 people. (para. 3)

a city of 25,000 people: 一座人口为两万五千的城市
cf. a city with a population of 23,000

Do not mix up the two. It is redundant to say "a city with a population of 23,000 people", for the word "population" means "the number of people living in a particular area, country, etc."

3. Nearby was the Bay of Naples, an arm of the blue Mediterranean. (para. 3)

This is an inverted sentence. The subject is "the Bay of Naples". Inversion occurs because the subject with an appositive "an arm of the blue Mediterranean" makes it too long. It is moved to the end of the sentence to avoid top heaviness (the situation in which the subject of a sentence is too long while the predicate too short).
an arm of sth.: a long and narrow area of land or water that is joined to a broader area

4. Rich men came down from wealthy Rome to build seaside villas. (para. 3)

down: Here it means moving from/to a big city.
It can also mean "in or toward south".
e.g. They drove all the way down from Wuhan to Guangzhou.

5. Rising behind the city was the 4,000-foot Mount Vesuvius, a grass-covered slope where the shepherds of Pompeii took their goats to graze. (para. 3)

This is also an inverted sentence. The subject is "the 4,000-foot Mount Vesuvius". It is placed after the predicate because it has a long attributive qualifier "a grass-covered slope...".
Note: 4,000-foot Vesuvius (not 4,000-feet)
Similar phrases: five-year plan, three-head monsters, a 6-month contract

6. ... a terrible rain of fire and ash. (para. 4)

... the fire and ash fell like rain.
a rain of sth.: a large number of things that fall from the sky at the same time
e.g. a rain of bullets, a rain of soot, a rain of petals, a rain of arrows
Similarly:
a shower of: a lot of small light things falling or moving through the air together
e.g. a shower of leaves, a shower of sparks, a shower of meteors, a shower of stones, a shower of blows, a shower of insults, a shower of phrases
a storm of: a sudden loud amount of noise made by a group of people in reaction to sth.
e.g. a storm of abuse/criticism/protest, a storm of applause/cheers

7. The tragedy struck... (para. 4)

"Strike" is a verb often used with disasters
e.g. A typhoon/plague strikes.
Some other uses of "strike":
He struck his head against the roof. (=hit)
He struck his brother on the head. (=hit with hand/weapon)
The clock struck four. (=make a sound four times)
She struck up an acquaintance with a musician on the trip. (=start talking)
How did the film strike you? (=What do you think of the film?)
The lightning struck terror into their hearts. (=make people very frightened)

8. Mount Vesuvius, which had slept quietly for centuries, erupted with savage violence. (para. 4)

had slept quietly: had been inactive/dormant
This is an informal way of describing an inactive (though not dead) volcano. As the book Lost Cities and Civilizations, from which the text is taken, is intended for a general audience, the writer doesn't use many technical terms. The technical term for an inactive volcano is "dormant", which means "not active or growing but having the ability to be active at a later time".
savage violence: Both of these words are very strong and normally they would not be used together. We must be careful when we qualify a very strong word in translation from Chinese as often the adjective or adverb actually weakens the English, e.g. in general, no adverb should be used with "ruin", "condemn", "denounce", etc.

9. For three days the sun did not break through the clouds of volcanic ash that filled the sky. (para. 4)

the clouds of volcanic ash: the large amount of volcanic ash that was floating in the air and looked like cloud
Similarly:
cloud of dust; cloud of smoke; cloud of steam
(large amount/number of sth.) fill a place: If a thing or group fills sth., there is no space left.
e.g. Crowds of his fans filled the streets.
Baby clothes, milk and diapers filled the suitcase.
Figuratively:
The smell of freshly roasted duck filled the kitchen.
At weekends, happy sounds filled the house.
It was midnight, and moonlight filled the room.

10. Beneath the protecting shroud of ash, the city lay intact. (para. 5)

The city remained as it had been before the eruption. It had been protected by the thick layer of ash that buried the city deep. (This paragraph describes what Pompeii was like when it was rediscovered. Therefore the past tense is used.)
shroud of ash: a metaphor in which the ash is compared to a shroud (=a cloth that is used to wrap a dead body)
intact: complete and not damaged or changed
e.g. The Johnsons suffered a good deal of misfortune in the past year. Yet, the family remained intact.

11. In the wine shops, the wine jars were in place, and on one counter could be seen a stain where a customer had thrown down his wine glass and fled. (para. 5)

In the wine shops, the wine containers were in their right place, and people could see a (wine) stain a customer had left on one counter when he hastily put his glass down and fled.
in place: in its usual or correct position
e.g. The books were all in place on the shelves.
Let's put the chairs in place before we go.
Opposite: out of place
Note: In the second coordinate clause the subject-verb order is inverted. The subject "a stain" is qualified by a relative clause introduced by the relative adverb. As it is too long, it is placed after the predicate "could be seen".

12. To go to Pompeii today is to take a trip backward in time. (para. 6)

When you go to Pompeii today, it is like going back 1,700 years in history because you would find the city as it actually was the day of the eruption.

13. The old city comes to life all around you. (para. 6)

The city is no longer a dead city. You can imagine what was going on around you. (The next sentence describes the sounds of the city.)
come to life: (of a place) to become filled with the energy and excitement of active people
e.g. The house comes to life whenever their grandchildren come to visit it.
The park comes to life when its cheery blossoms bring thousands of tourists.

14. Ships from every nation are in port... (para. 6)

be in port: to have stopped at sea ports to load and unload cargo
Related expressions: to come into port; to enter port; to leave port

15. Such was Pompeii on its last day. (para. 7)

That was what Pompeii was like the day it was destroyed in the volcanic eruption. This is an inverted sentence, in which the subject "Pompeii" is placed after the verb "was". The predicative "such" is put at the beginning of the sentence because the word (pronoun) refers to what is said in the previous sentence.
Here inversion is used as transitional device. The sentence summarizes what has been said and naturally leads to the next paragraph.

16. A good imagination is all you need to restore it to activity. (para. 7)

If you have a good imagination you will be able to imagine what was going on—the sights, sounds, smells and activities of a busy, prosperous Roman town.
restore sth. to sth.: to make sth. return to its previous state or condition
e.g. A week at the beach will restore you to health.
The government is trying hard to restore the country's economy to full strength.

17. ... Pompeii's 25,000 people awakened to another hot day in that hot summer. (para. 8)

... Pompeii's 25,000 people woke up to find that they were going to have another hot day.
The preposition "to" here indicates a particular result.
awaken: It is generally used in the passive voice and is used figuratively. Here "awoke" or "woke up" would do just as well.

18. In the forum the town's important men had gathered after breakfast to read the political signs that had been posted during the night. (para. 8)

forum: the marketplace or public place of an ancient Roman city forming the center of judicial and public business
Cultural note: The forum was the centre of the city's religious, economic, and municipal life; it was a large area with a two-story entrance, around which were temples of ancient Greek gods, a large food market, the imposing headquarters of the woollen industry, the meeting place of the city council and the offices of the officials of the city.
In modern English a forum is a public meeting or lecture involving audience discussion.
sign: a posted command, warning, or direction

19. The mountain blew up, raining death on thousands. (para. 9)

To "blow up" and to "explode" are often confused, even by native speakers.
blow up: to destroy by explosion, e.g. to blow up a bridge/a building/a ship
explode: vi. to break into pieces violently, e.g. A mine bomb exploded.
explode: vt. to make sth. break into pieces, e.g. The country claimed to have exploded a nuclear device.
raining death: bringing/causing death. "Rain" is a transitive verb used figuratively here.
Similarly: rain blows/honors/gifts/scorn/curses, etc. on sb.

20. Like the trunk of a tree, it rose in the air, branching out as it climbed. (para. 12)

Like a tree trunk dividing into many branches while the tree is growing, the black cloud of volcanic ash from the crater expanded in different direction as it rose.

21. The sound of the explosion died away, but it still reverberated in everyone's ears. (para. 13)

Even when the loud sound stopped, people did not recover from the shock.
die away: (of sound/wind/light) to become weaker gradually until you cannot hear, or feel, or see it
e.g. They didn't go back to bed until the footsteps died away.
The firing finally began to die away in the late afternoon.

22. ... the Temple of Jupiter, the Temple of Apollo, the Temple of Isis. (para. 14)

Jupiter: the chief ancient Roman and Italian god; the equivalent of the ancient Greek god Zeus (宙斯)
Apollo: the Greek god of light, youth, beauty, and prophecy. He was later worshiped by the Romans.
Isis: one of the most important goddesses of ancient Egypt whose worship spread to Greece and Rome

23. An hour went by and darkness still shrouded everything. (para. 14)

An hour passed, things did not improve and darkness covered everything.
shroud: to cover or hide sth.

24. All was confusion. (para. 14)

There was total chaos everywhere. / The whole city had been thrown into chaos.
confusion: a state or situation, often with a lot of activity and noise, in which many things are happening in a way that is not controlled or orderly
e.g. In the confusion after a power cut, three children were injured.

25. The people of Pompeii now knew that doom was at hand. (para. 14)

The people of Pompeii now knew that they would not survive the eruption.
doom was at hand: death was near

26. ... only the quick-witted managed to escape. (para. 15)

the quick-witted: those who think fast and intelligently
Opposite: slow-witted, dull-witted

27. ... he led his little band out into the nightmare of the streets. (para. 15)

... he led his family out into the streets, which were extremely chaotic.
nightmare: a very difficult, unpleasant or frightening situation
e.g. Our trip abroad was a nightmare.

28. And a new trouble was in store for Pompeii. (para. 16)

in store: in a state of being ready or prepared to happen
More examples:
The immigrants did not know what was in store for them in the New World.
A great future is in store for our people.

29. Roofs went crashing in ruin... (para. 16)

Synonyms for fall in the text: crash, topple, collapse

30. Poisonous gas came drifting from the crater... (para. 17)

poisonous gas: "Poisonous" is the correct adjective for this gas that came from the volcano. "Poison gas" is the name given to poisonous gas which is specially manufactured to kill one's enemies.

31. Rushing throngs, blinded by the darkness and the smoke, rushed up one street and down the next, trampling the fallen in a crazy fruitless dash toward safety. (para. 17)

People panicked. They rushed into the streets to escape the falling buildings. It was dark and the air was full of smoke so they could not see where they were going. They rushed up one street and down the next in a hopeless attempt to reach safety. In their blind rush they ran over the bodies of people who had fallen down.

32. ... and Pompeians died by the hundreds. (para. 18)

by: often indicating a unit of time or other measurement
e.g. to rent a house by the year
to hire a boat by the hour
to sell eggs by the dozen
to sell cloth by the meter
to be paid by the hour
to be sold by the ounce
to go in by twos and threes

33. All over Pompeii, people lay down on beds of pumice stones, overwhelmed by the gas... (para. 18)

Everywhere people lay on flat piles of pumice stones which covered the ground.
a bed of sth.: a flat pile or layer of sth.
e.g. He stretched out on a bed of flowers on the hillside.
Mother and child lay down to sleep on a bed of new hay

34. Not once had Vesuvius stopped hurling... (para. 19)

Inverted order for emphasis and dramatic effect

35. ... Pompeii was a city of the dead. (para. 20)

... all the people who stayed on in Pompeii had died. / ... no one was still alive in Pompeii.

## （续）

### III Translation of the Text

庞贝
罗伯特·西尔弗伯格

距那不勒斯不远，一座奇特的城市在意大利炙热的太阳下沉睡，这就是庞贝古城，世界上没有别的城市和它雷同。除了蟋蟀、甲壳虫、蜥蜴，这里没有其他生物。不过每年还是有成千上万的人从遥远的国度到此参观。

庞贝是座死城，确切地说，自从公元79年夏天以来，将近2000年里无人在此居住。

在那年之前，庞贝是一座有两万五千人口的繁荣城市。附近是那不勒斯湾——地中海的一个狭长海湾。有钱人从富裕的罗马到这里来建海边别墅。农田环绕庞贝城，在城市的后面耸立着四千英尺高的维苏威山。它的一面山坡覆盖着青草，庞贝的牧羊人把羊群赶到这里放牧。那时庞贝是座繁忙的城市，也是座欢乐的城市。

但它突然在烈火和灰尘中消亡了。这场灾难发生在公元79年8月24日。维苏威火山在沉睡了几个世纪以后以巨大的威力爆发。成吨的炙热火山灰落入庞贝，将它掩埋。连续三天，阳光没有穿透天空中火山灰形成的云雾。火山爆发之后，庞贝被深埋地下，一座城市就这样消失了。

十几个世纪过去了……庞贝被遗忘了。后来，1700年以后，它重新被人发现。在厚厚的火山灰的保护下，这座城市完好无损。一切都如维苏威火山爆发的那天一样：面包房的烤炉里仍然有一个个长面包；酒店里，酒坛子还在原处，在一张柜台上还可以看到一位顾客逃跑时扔下酒杯而留下的污迹。

今天到庞贝去就是回到往昔的时光。庞贝古城在你身边复活了。你几乎可以听到马蹄在狭窄街道上的得得声、孩子们的叫声和店主们的笑声。蓝蓝的天空中没有云朵，夏日的高高的太阳挂在天上。山坡覆盖着绿茵茵的维苏威山在古城的背后直耸云霄，离城墙三千英尺之外，阳光照在庞贝湾的水上，闪闪发光。来自不同国家的船只停泊在港口，街上能听到各种陌生的语言。

这就是庞贝末日时的景象，也是火山灰清除后今天的景象。只要有丰富的想象力，你就能使这座古城一下子活起来。

公元79年8月24日清晨，庞贝的25,000人睡醒了。迎来了那个炎热夏季的又一个烈日。那天夜里在竞技场有一场角斗，全城的人都期盼着这场角斗士之间血腥的表演。孩子们奔向学校，手拿石板，身后跟着各自的狗。在市中心广场，市里的男人们要在早餐后聚在一起阅读夜里张贴出的通告；在广场的另一个地方，羊毛商在谈生意；钱庄老板在检查账本；从东方来的晚起的游客睡醒了，打着哈欠嚷着要吃早饭。

平静的上午时光慢慢流逝，庞贝城没有任何异样的情形发生。然而灾难已悄悄地逼近。就在覆盖着藤蔓植物的维苏威山坡的下面，一股巨大的力量就要爆发。下午一点钟的时候，危险的时刻终于到来，维苏威火山爆发了，给成千上万人带来死亡。在距火山口数公里的庞贝城里，人们听见了巨大的爆炸声。

“那是什么？”从城的一端到另一端，人们叫喊着，他们睁大眼睛，你看我，我看你，迷惑不解，焦虑不安。难道众神在天上开战了？

“快看！”有人喊道。“看维苏威山！”

成千双眼睛向上看去，成千只胳膊向上指着。一股黑云从炸开的山顶升起，越升越高，在天空中一边攀升，一边像树干上的树枝一样，向四周分成几股扩散。几分钟过去了，爆炸声渐渐消失，但这声音仍然在每个人的耳中回响。维苏威山上空的烟雾继续上升，像漆黑的夜晚，越升越高。此时庞贝城上空开始下起一场奇特的雨——石雨。这是浮石，主要由气泡构成。石头很轻，但却像是一场倾盆大雨。浮石几乎没有造成破坏。

“出了什么事？”庞贝人你问我，我问你。他们冲进庙宇——朱庇特神庙、阿波罗神庙、伊希斯生育女神庙。祭司们尽力让市民平静下来。天空漆黑，一小时过去了，黑暗仍然笼罩着一切。到处都是哭喊声。此刻庞贝人知道他们的末日就要来临了。当火烫的火山灰像密集的雨点一样开始降落，他们的恐惧开始加剧。聚集在木质屋顶的火山灰的巨大热量让一些房屋着了火，还有些建筑物在大量浮石的重压下纷纷坍塌。

在最初的几个小时里，只有一些特别机智的人逃了出来。一位有钱的羊毛商，把他的家人聚在一起，将首饰和钱塞入口袋，点着火把带领他那一小队人走进混乱肮脏的街道。几百个庞贝人在最初几小时的黑暗时光里逃出去了。他们在黑暗中跌跌撞撞，艰难地走向城门，出了城门后直奔港口，登上了船，逃离了危险，这才活下来将城市毁灭的故事告诉他人。其他人选择留在城里，聚集在庙里，公共澡堂里，或者自己家的地窖里。他们仍然希望噩梦会结束。

已经是晚上了，新的灾难在等待着庞贝。大地震了。屋顶顷刻倒塌，将希望躲过火山爆发的几百人埋在废墟下面。广场上高大的柱子倒了。整个城市就像在一只巨手的手中颤动。

此时已有三英尺厚的浮石覆盖着大地：火山灰在空中飘荡；人们尚能呼吸，可有毒的气体从火山口喷出。房屋都在坍塌；到处都听得见伤者和奄奄一息的人的哭喊声。在黑暗和烟雾中什么都看不见的人群，为了寻找安全地带，徒劳地到处乱窜，踏着摔倒的人的身体，从一条街窜到另一条街。大人闯进死胡同，发现被困在纷纷倒塌的建筑物之中。他们呆在那里，吓得无法再跑，只能等待死亡降临。

夜越来越深了，毒气越来越浓。人们可以躲避浮石，但是躲不了毒气。成百成百的庞贝人死于毒气。一氧化碳气体使人体不能吸收氧气。中毒者越来越疲倦，直到昏迷，再也恢复不了知觉。在庞贝城的各个角落，人们被一氧化碳气体击倒，躺在大为平坦的浮石堆上，静静地死去。

在整个漫长的夜里，庞贝人在街上漫无目的地游荡，或是蹲在已经毁坏的房屋里，或是聚集在庙里祈祷。到次日清晨，几乎没有人还活着。维苏威山始终一刻不停地向天空喷发浮石和火山灰，街道很快被填满了。八月二十五日正午，正好在火山第一次爆发24小时之后，发生了第二次爆发。火山灰形成的烟云再次从维苏威山顶升起，风把火山灰吹到了遥远的罗马，但是大部分还是落到了庞贝。

浮石和火山灰构成的致命的“阵雨”持续到第二天。至于爆发是再持续一天，还是一年，对庞贝城已无关紧要。因为到八月二十五日正午时，庞贝已是一座死城。

1. ... not since the summer of the year A.D. 79, to be exact. (para. 2)
(infinitive used as adverbial)

(infinitive phrase used as subject and predicative 不定式作主语和表语)

(verb pattern: to be + prepositional phrases)
5. Roofs went crashing in ruin, burying hundreds who had hoped to survive the eruption. (para. 16)
(present participle phrases used as adverbial modifiers 现在分词作状语)
6. Rushing throngs, blinded by the darkness and the smoke, rushed up one street and down the next, trampling the fallen in a crazy fruitless dash toward safety. (para. 17)
(present participle phrases used as attributive and adverbial modifiers 现在分词作定语或伴随状语)
7. Victims of carbon monoxide poisoning get sleepier and sleepier until they lose consciousness, never to regain it. (para. 18)
(infinitive phrases used as adverbial of result 不定式作状语表示结果)
They boarded boats and got away, living to tell the tale of their city's destruction. (para. 15)
