# Unit 10

## Text A · Two Friends · Guy de Maupassant

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

The Franco-Prussian War undoubtedly had a great influence on Maupassant. He was 20 when the then French emperor Louis Napoleon declared war on Prussia which was viewed as a threat to France's traditional domination of Europe. But the aggressor's war effort was ridiculously ill-prepared. Within two months, the French army was badly beaten by the Prussian army in Sedan, and the French emperor was forced to surrender unconditionally with his remaining 83,000 soldiers. When news reached Paris, people filled the streets, shouting, "Down with the empire, long live the republic!" A new government was formed and Paris was prepared for a siege. It held out for 130 days while the citizens ate sparrows, rats, cats, anything edible they could lay their hands on. On January 28, 1871, Paris surrendered. A peace treaty was signed with extremely harsh terms for France. It had to pay a war indemnity of 5 billion francs, and surrender Alsace and Lorraine. The German armies paraded through Paris in triumph. On January 18, William I was proclaimed German Emperor in the palace of Versailles. The German state was now a reality, and the French domination of Europe had come to an end.

Maupassant volunteered to fight the Prussians as a soldier for a short time. After he was demobilized he went back to study law in Paris where he practiced writing under the guidance of Flaubert, together with some other young writers. At one of those regular meetings, Flaubert suggested that each of the six young people present submitted a story to be published in a collection. Maupassant's story "Tallow Ball" was universally regarded as the best of the six pieces. It made him famous overnight and was followed by about 300 other stories in the following years, most of which were also inspired by or based on his experience of the war, including the present text.

#### 2 Structure

I. Siege (para. 1)

Paris was in a miserable situation under the Prussian siege. People all suffered from hunger.

II. Reunion of Good Old Friends (paras. 2–31)

1. Morissot and Sauvage, who had not seen each other for months, came across each other. The excited friends began to reminisce their regular fishing expeditions before the war. (paras. 2–20)

2. Energized by the alcohol and the fresh breeze, they were both too excited by the prospect of fishing. They managed to secure the proper password from the French colonel to pass the frontline. (paras. 21–32)

III. Martyrs and Friends Forever (paras. 33–102)

1. As the two friends left Paris, they paused as they looked out at the deserted country and gained the perception that the Prussians were dangerous who had spent months massacring and starving the people of France. (paras. 33–45)

2. The two friends had been so intent upon their fishing that, for a while, the war had faded from their minds. Then they were vigorously reminded of their situation by the rumbling of cannons, which gave rise to their discussion on war and politics while fishing. (paras. 46–64)

3. At that very moment, the two fishermen were seized and brought to the Prussian commander who accused them of being spies and threatened to kill them immediately unless they gave him their password to cross the French frontline. (paras. 65–73)

4. The two friends did not give the Prussian officer their password, and consequently they were shot dead. They remained loyal friends till the last moment. (paras. 74–102)

#### 3 The vivid and effective description of the characters and scenes

Maupassant chose his words and expressions very carefully, and as a result created unforgettable characters in the story: the two fishermen as well as the Prussian officer, and some very dramatic scenes including the terribly miserable situation of Paris, the war atrocities committed by the Prussians, the unforgettable fishing experience of the two fishermen and their brutal execution by the Prussian officer.

#### 4 Teaching Tips

**The theme of the story**

Our first impression naturally is that the theme of the story is friendship as the title has made it clear. We are familiar with this subject and have read or heard many stories about true friends. But there is something unusual about these two friends: First, one reason why they become close friends is that they both are enthusiastic anglers—unusually enthusiastic, and what makes fishing such a great hobby for them must also reflect their love for freedom and the natural beauty; second, they enjoy each other's company so much that they would spend every Sunday fishing together; third, they have no difficulty understanding each other and appreciating each other; fourth, they have the same interest in many problems and always enjoy sharing their views even if they do not always agree; but what makes their friendship really unique is the fact that they are always faithful to each other—Even when their lives are at stake, they would rather die together than let each other down. We know the saying that a friend in need is a friend indeed. But in this case, friendship has nothing to do with either side's need. In this story, we might wonder why these two people should decide to go fishing again under those circumstances. They were not crazy, nor were they hopelessly drunk. They were terribly hungry and would love to have some fish to eat, but they knew the danger, they knew they were risking their lives if they went fishing in their favorite place that day. We would not be able to understand why they should decide to go fishing that day if we do not fully understand how they loved fishing, loved peace and quiet, loved their friendship, loved their freedom and their memory of those beautiful days together.

The other theme of the story is the war. Maupassant once said "War! When I think of this word, I feel bewildered, as though they were speaking to me of sorcery, of the Inquisition, of a distant, finished, abominable, monstrous, unnatural thing." In this story he once again expressed his views about war through the two fishermen who regarded all those people who indulged in killing each other as being hopelessly stupid. Maupassant lived in an age when wars between nations and civil wars were frequent, and most of them could not be classified as just. There were winners and losers, but ordinary people usually were the victims. The two friends in this story were decent and honest people. They were not patriots fighting Prussians. But they were still brutally killed just before the war ended because they were foreigners to the Prussians and dared to go fishing in their occupied areas.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text A

1. Paris was under siege, in the grip of famine, at its last gasp. (para. 1)

This is a parallel structure of prepositional phrases.

Parallelism: Words, phrases, and clauses that are similar in structure and perform the same function in a sentence are said to be parallel. Parallelism makes the reader aware of the similarity in ideas. If the ideas are not similar, the parallel structure may seem awkward.

Other cases of parallelism from the text (see the underlined parts):

a) They had never so much as set eyes on them, but for four months now they had been aware of their presence on the outskirts of Paris, occupying part of France, looting, committing atrocities, reducing people to starvation ... (para. 38)

b) And they scrambled down the slope of one of the vineyards, bent double, crawling on their hands and knees, taking advantage of the cover afforded by the vines, keeping their eyes wide open and their ears on the alert. (para. 43)

c) They were so absorbed that they no longer heard, or thought, or paid the least attention to the outside world. (para. 50)

d) And as they talked, Mount Valérien went thundering on, demolishing French homes with its cannonades, pounding lives to dust, crushing human beings to pulp, putting an end to so many dreams, to so many long-awaited joys, tearing into the hearts of all those wives and daughters and mothers with pain and suffering that would never be eased. (para. 64)

e) In a matter of seconds they were seized, tied up, hustled along, thrown into a boat and carried across to the island. (para. 66)

f) Morissot swayed, spun round, then collapsed on top of his friend, with his face staring up at the sky and the blood welling from where his coat had been burst open across his chest. (para. 92)

2. There were few sparrows on the rooftops now, and even the sewers were losing some of their inhabitants. (para. 1)

"Their inhabitants" here refers to rats, implying that the hungry Parisians were even eating sparrows and rats if they could find them.

3. Every Sunday before the war it was Morissot's custom to set off at dawn with his bamboo rod in his hand and a tin box slung over his back. (para. 3)

slung: past and past participle of sling

sling: vt. to throw or put sth. somewhere with a careless movement and some force

e.g. Peter slung his suitcase onto the bed.

Don't just sling your bag on the floor!

4. And it was here that he met a tubby, jolly little man by the name of Sauvage. (para. 4)

Emphatic pattern: it + be + emphasized parts + that/who-clause

More examples from the text:

It was Monsieur Sauvage who caught the first fish. (para. 48)

It was then that Morissot happened to glance down at the net of fish which was lying in the grass ... (para. 85)

5. They often spent half the day sitting side by side, rod in hand, with their feet dangling over the water. (para. 4)

dangle: to hang or swing loosely, or to make sth. do this

e.g. A light bulb dangled from a wire in the ceiling.

Loose electric wires were dangling from the wall.

6. On spring mornings when the sun sent floating over the river that light mist which moves along with the current... (para. 5)

Pay attention to the shift of word order. The normal word order should be "... the sun sent that light mist floating over the river."

"Floating over the river" is now put before "that light mist" in order to avoid the misunderstanding that the attributive clause "which moves along..." modifies "the river" instead of "the mist".

7. In the autumn towards the close of day, when the sky was blood-red and the water reflected strange shapes of scarlet clouds which reddened the whole river, and the glowing sun set the distant horizon ablaze, making the two friends look as though they were on fire... (para. 10)

In the above sentence, readers can perceive the fall's dominant color in different shades, which, like spring, form the beautiful backgrounds of their fishing expeditions.

8. As soon as the two friends had recognized each other, they shook hands warmly, feeling quite emotional over the fact that they had come across each other in such different circumstances. (para. 13)

"The fact that..." in the above sentence is an appositive clause.

Compare:

... feeling quite emotional over the fact that they had come across each other in such different circumstances. (appositive clause)

This is a fact that we take as common sense. (attributive clause)

Another case of appositive clause in the text:

... scared by the feeling that somebody had been walking just behind them. (para. 65)

9. There's something worth remembering! (para. 19)

That is something worth remembering for us. "There" here is "there+is/are" structure, the same as "here" in the following sentence: Here is the Bus No 9.

10. A gentle breeze caressed their faces. (para. 22)

Breeze here is compared to a person who caressed the two friends' faces. We call this figurat use "personification".

11. I'm sure we'd have no trouble in getting through. (para. 28)

get through: to cross the frontline and get to their destination

12. "Right!" he said. "I'm your man!" (para. 30)

"All right!" he said. "I'm the one you need. I'll go with you."

13. On the opposite bank they could see the village of Argenteuil, which looked deserted and dead. (para. 34)

"Deserted and dead" is an alliteration. (Alliteration: the use of the same letter or sound at the beginning of words that are close together, as in "sing a song of sixpence.")

14. The hills dominated the horizon, and the great plain which stretches as far as Nanterre was completely empty, with nothing to be seen but its leafless cherry trees and gray earth. (para. 34)

dominate: to be the largest, highest or most obvious thing in a place

e.g. The cathedral dominates the city.

The fortress on top of the hill still dominates Barcelona harbor.

15. And they scrambled down the slope of one of the vineyards, bent double, crawling on their hands and knees, taking advantage of the cover afforded by the vines... (para. 43)

bent double: standing with their upper body curved forward and down toward the ground

e.g. The old man was bent double under his load.

He was bent double with laughter/pain.

taking advantage of the cover afforded by the vines: using the vines to prevent them from being seen by the enemy...

afford: (fml) to provide sb. with sth.

e.g. The tree affords some shelter from the sun.

Being a college professor affords you the opportunity simply to write and do research.

The legislation aims to afford protection to employees.

The program affords young people the chance to gain work experience.

16. The little building which once housed the restaurant was closed and shuttered and looked as though it had been abandoned for years. (para. 47)

house: if a building, place, or container houses sth., it keeps there.

e.g. The hotel once housed a casino and several restaurants.

The refugees have been fed, clothed and housed by welfare organizations around the world.

The collection of Greek statues is currently housed in the British Museum.

17. They carefully placed each fish into a fine-meshed net suspended in the water at their feet. (para. 49)

suspend: (often passive fml) to hang sth. from sth. else

The following are samples of such passive usage:

be suspended from sth.: A lamp was suspended from the ceiling.

be suspended by/on sth.: Her body was found suspended by a tree branch.

be suspended by/on sth. from sth.: A boat was suspended on wires from the rafters.

fine-meshed: fine means "made of very thin or narrow wires or threads" here.

18. And as they did so they were overcome by the kind of joy you only experience when you resume something you really love after being deprived of it for a long time. (para. 49)

"Do so" here refers to placing fish they caught into a fine-meshed net.

resume: to start doing sth. again after stopping or being interrupted

e.g. He will resume training as soon as his health condition is better.

deprive of: to prevent somebody from having or doing sth., especially sth. important

e.g. Why should you deprive yourself of such simple pleasures?

They were imprisoned and deprived of their basic rights.

19. On the mountainside was a white plume of smoke, showing where the gunpowder had just bellowed out. (para. 53)

plume: a cloud of sth. that rises and curves upwards in the air

e.g. A great plume of dust and ash rose from the volcano.

A black plume of smoke rose above the city.

bellow: to shout in a loud deep voice, especially when angry

20. ... every now and then the mountain spat out its deadly breath, exhaled its clouds of milky vapor... (para. 55)

"The mountain spat out its deadly breath" is a personification, comparing the mountain to a person who can breathe.

spit out: to force sth. out of your mouth

deadly breath: the smoke of gunpowder produced by firing of weapons

milky vapor: the white plume of smoke of weapons

21. "There they go again!" said Monsieur Sauvage with a shrug of his shoulders. (para. 56)

"There" is used as an adverb here. It is usually placed at the beginning of a sentence, and used for stress something. Do not mix it up with the use of the word in "there is/are" structure, such as in "There's something worth remembering!" (para. 19).

22. But at that moment they both gave a start, scared by the feeling that somebody had been walking just behind them. (para. 65)

start: a sudden movement of the body that you make when sth. has surprised or frightened you

e.g. He woke with a start.

She gave a start as I entered.

23. Now, as far as I am concerned you are a couple of spies sent out here to keep an eye on me. You were obviously pretending to fish as a cover for your real purposes. (para. 71)

keep an eye on: to look after sb. or sth. and make sure that they are safe

e.g. Mary will keep an eye on the kids this afternoon.

We keep a watchful eye on our elderly neighbors who might need medical assistance.

cover: sth. that conceals or obscures

e.g. under cover of darkness

24. Now, since you've come out here past your own lines, you're bound to have a password so you can get back. (para. 71)

line: (countable) an imaginary limit or border between one place and another or a district/county line

e.g. He was convicted of illegally importing weapons across state lines.

be bound to: to be very likely to do or feel a particular thing

e.g. Don't lie to her. She's bound to find out.

When you are dealing with so many patients, mistakes are bound to happen.

it is bound to be (=used to say that sth. should have been expected)

e.g. "It's hot!"

—"Well, it was bound to be. I just took it out of the oven."

25. If you refuse to cooperate, you'll die—straight away. (para. 73)

The Prussian officer is brutal and likes to give orders that must be immediately executed, and in paragraph 101, he gave another order. "Fry me these little creatures—straight away, while they're still alive!"

straight away: (also straight off) (BrE spoken) immediately or without delay

e.g. He didn't answer straight away.

Please send your gift straight away.

We don't have to leave the room straight away, do we?

26. I'll make it look as though I've taken pity on you both. (para. 80)

"As though" clause could be employed in either real or unreal conditions, here it is used as a real condition.

Compare it with the other two cases in the text in which "as though" is employed as unreal conditions:

a) ... making the two friends look as though they were on fire... (para. 10)

b) ... the restaurant was closed and shuttered and looked as though it had been abandoned for years. (para. 47)

27. Monsieur Sauvage fell like a log onto his face. (para. 92)

"Like a log" is a simile, which means the miserable citizen lost his consciousness straight away.

"Like a log" is a simile, which means the miserable citizen died and fell to the ground right away. The noise of cannons would never wake him up.

See also the phrase "sleep like a log", which means to sleep very well, without being woken by any noises: I slept like a log—I didn't even hear the thunderstorm.

28. Morissot swayed, spun round, then collapsed on top of his friend, with his face staring up at the sky and the blood welling from where his coat had been burst open across his chest. (para. 92)

well: If a liquid wells or wells up, it comes to the surface of sth. and starts to flow out.

e.g. I felt tears well up in my eyes.

29. His men went off and came back with some lengths of rope and a few heavy stones which they fastened to the feet of the two bodies. (para. 93)

The fact that the two friends' bodies were fastened to the heavy stones would foreshadow the Prussian officer's intention to throw their bodies to the bottom of the river to feed the fish.

length: (countable) a piece of sth. long and thin

e.g. a length of rope/pipe/wire

30. All the time Mount Valérien continued to rumble, and now it was capped by a great mountain of smoke. (para. 94)

The thundering and rumbling of the Mount Valérien serves as the melancholy background of this sorrowful war story, and this description implies the peak, or the climax of story, which is the death of two ordinary citizens of Paris.

cap: (often passive) to cover the top or end of sth. with sth.

e.g. You should have your teeth capped (=protected with an artificial covering).

31. "Wilhelm!" (para. 99)

The Prussian officer is addressing to his cook named Wilhelm.

### III Translation of Text A

两个朋友

居伊·德·莫泊桑

巴黎被敌人重重包围，全城在饥饿中挣扎，已经危在旦夕。现在屋顶上麻雀都难觅踪影，连下水道为家的住户有的都不见了。事实是，人们逮到什么活物都用来塞肚子。

正月里一个晴朗的早晨，钟表匠莫里梭先生正阴郁地沿着一条环城大街溜达，路上他忽然遇到了他老朋友索瓦日先生。

在战前，每逢周日，莫里梭总会天一亮就出发，手持竹竿鱼竿，背挎锡铁罐，搭火车到科隆布下车，然后步行到马朗特岛。一到这个美丽的梦之岛，他就迫不及待地开始钓鱼，不到天黑，绝不收竿。

就在这岛上，他遇见一个热情开朗、身材矮胖的索瓦日先生。这位先生是一家男士衣帽店的老板，也是个钓鱼迷。他们时常肩并肩一起坐着，手握钓竿，在水面上荡着双脚，就这样成了莫逆之交。

在风和日丽的早晨，阳光照着河面的轻雾，使之随水缓缓漂动，同时也向两个朋友的背上洒下春天怡人的温暖。莫里梭会对身边的朋友说：

"这里实在太棒了！不是吗？"

索瓦日先生便会说：

"是的，我想不出有比这更好的地方了。"

这简单的一问一答，就足够使他们相互理解、惺惺相惜了。

等到秋天，每当傍晚时分，夕阳将天空染得一片通红，红云倒映在河水里，也染红了整条河。地平线像在熊熊燃烧，他们让二人看起来似乎沐浴在火光之中。树上的叶子，预感到寒冬将至，在簌簌颤动中也披上了金装。索瓦日先生转向莫里梭，露出微笑：

"多美的风景啊！"

莫里梭的心也一样完全沉醉在这醉人的美景之中。

当下他们认出对方后，便紧紧握手，为在当前这悲痛时局中的重逢而激动不已。索瓦日先生叹了口气：

"我们从上一次见面到现在期间所发生的一切可真是一言难尽了！"

莫里梭也忧伤地感慨道：

"这些日子里天气也糟透了，这还真是今年好不容易遇到的第一个好天气。"

确实，今天真是少见的碧空如洗，阳光格外明媚。

他们肩并肩开始继续散步，莫里梭说：

"还记得在岛上钓鱼吗？多美好的回忆啊！"

索瓦日先生陷入了沉思："我们什么时候能再去？"

他们去了一家小咖啡店，各点了一杯酒。

空腹灌了这杯酒，从酒馆出来时，他们都有些醉了，晕晕乎乎的，而且天气正暖，和风拂面。

索瓦日先生停住脚步说：

"怎么样？我们去不去！"

"去哪儿？"

"去钓鱼啊！"

"但是，我们能去哪儿钓？"

"当然是去我们那个岛上。我方战线在科隆布附近。我认识指挥那支部队的上校，我确定我们能得到他放行。"

莫里梭兴奋得都发抖了：

"好！你去哪里，都算我一个。"

于是，他们两人就分头去取各自的钓具。

一个小时，两个朋友肩并肩又大步走在了大路上。他们抵达了那位上校设立指挥所的别墅。上校听到他们强烈要去钓鱼的怪癖要求，不禁笑了起来，但他最后还是批准了。

他们很快就通过了前线地带，穿过已被撤离的哥伦布镇，来到俯瞰塞纳河的梯田上的葡萄园区边上。

他们的对岸，是阿让特伊镇，看去空无一人，一片死寂。一眼望去，远处是一群小山，山前是一直延伸到楠泰尔的大片平地，地上除了光秃秃的樱桃树，便是灰色的泥土，其他空无一物。

索瓦日先生指着这些山冈，嘟囔着说：

"普鲁士人，就在那上面！"

两个朋友面对着这片已被遗弃的农田，不安的感觉渐渐袭来，让他们腿脚发软，几乎难以动弹。

普鲁士人！他们虽然都没有真正亲眼见过他们，但四个月来，他们知道他们就在巴黎郊外，占据着法国的部分领土，抢劫掳掠，犯下种种暴行，制造饥荒……

莫里梭紧张地说："万一撞见他们怎么办？"

一时间，他们犹豫起来，不知道应不应该继续往前走。

最后，还是索瓦日先生打定了主意。

"走吧，我们继续上路！不过我们得加倍警惕。"

于是他们穿越山腰上的葡萄园，手腳并用，弯腰向山下爬去，尽量利用一些葡萄藤掩护自己，竖起耳朵，睁大眼睛。

离河岸只隔着一段裸露的地带了，他们狂奔过去，刚到岸边，立刻就蹲下去钻进干枯的灯芯草丛中。

莫里梭把脸贴在地上，仔细听附近有没有人走动的脚步声。他什么也没听见。

这里除了他们，绝无旁人。

他们这下知道没什么可担忧的，就开始钓起鱼来。

对面无人居住的马朗特岛掩护着他们，挡住了河对岸的视线。岛上的那座小楼里原来有家餐馆，而今小楼的门和窗都紧闭着，仿佛已经被遗弃多年。

钓到第一条鱼的索瓦日先生，莫里梭钓到第二条。接着，他们几乎不停地拉起钓竿，钩上都有一条鲜活跳跃的小鱼。今天的收获真是不小。

他们小心翼翼地放鱼进脚边水里细密的渔网里，心中充满快乐。这种快乐只有当你重新拥有被剥夺已久的心爱之物时才能体会。

这时，和煦的阳光把他们的背脊晒得热乎乎的。他们在钓鱼的时候，什么也听不见，什么也不想，完全不顾周围的一切。

但是，突然一声似乎来自地下的闷响震得他们脚底的大地晃动起来。

远处的加农炮又开始怒吼。

莫里梭转过头，看到河对岸瓦勒良山巨大的山体。山腰处冒出一团羽毛似的白烟，那是火药炸开的地方。

几乎同时，另一道白烟从山顶堡垒处喷出，片刻之后爆炸声再次传来。

炮声接连而至，山上时不时喷射致命气息，吐出的团团白烟缓缓升向沉寂的天空。

"他们又来了！"索瓦日先生耸耸肩说道。

莫里梭原本一直焦急地盯着浮标上的羽毛，一想到这些战争狂，突然发起火来。

"这样热衷于互相残杀，他们真是蠢透了！"他低声吼道。

"简直畜生不如，"索瓦日先生回应说。

刚刚又钓上一条鱼的莫里梭喊道：

"而且只要还有政府，这毛祸就永远改不掉。"

"不，"索瓦日先生反驳说，"要是一个共和国就不会宣战……"

"我认为，"莫里梭打断他的话，说道，"国家如果由国王统治，则会和别国打仗。而换成共和政府又会打内战。"

于是，他们开始争论起来，但是两人都心平气和、态度友善，完全是两个性格温情、通情达理的人试图以普通人的方式来解释世界上重大的政治问题。有一点他们完全同意：人类永远无法获得自由。就在他们谈话的同时，瓦勒良山上炮声始终不断，摧毁着法国人的家园，将生灵炸成齑粉，人体压成肉酱，使人们的美梦化为泡影，欢乐就此终结，撕裂多少妻子、女儿和母亲的心，让她们留下永远无法磨灭的伤痛。

但是就在这时，他们两人突然都吓了一跳，感觉身后有人走来。转头一看，上面四个留着胡子的高大士兵，手上端着枪直指着他们。

几秒钟的功夫两人就被抓住，捆起来押走，扔进一条船带到了小岛上。

在房屋后面他们看到二十来个德国士兵。

一个毛发浓密的魁梧军官分开双腿跨坐在椅子上，抽着烟斗，用地道的法语问他们：

"先生们，钓鱼可还尽兴？"

一个士兵把满满的一兜鱼放在军官脚边。那普鲁士军官笑着说：

"喔！看得出来你们收获不小啊！……但我现在要处理的完全是另外一件事。对我来说，你们俩就是派来专门监视我的探子。显然你们是在假装钓鱼来掩盖真正的目的，不过很不幸，落到了我的手里。可是，战争就是战争……既然你们能跨过你们的阵线来到这里，一定知道回去时需要的暗号。只要告诉我暗号，我就饶了你们俩的命。"

两个朋友，并排站着，面色发白，双手颤抖着。但他们一句话也没说。

军官接着说："此事永远不会有人知道。你们回去不用担心……但如果你拒绝合作，那只有死路一条——而且马上就死。你们自己选吧。"

他们站在那里，一动不动，嘴巴紧闭。

那普鲁士人指着河的方向说道：

"想想吧！五分钟后你们就躺在那条河的河底了。就五分钟！你们都有家人吧。想想他们！"

两个渔夫就站在那里，仍然一言不发。那军官用德语下了几道命令，然后挪开椅子，和两个卫兵拉开了距离。这时十二个人走上前来，在离他们二十步远的地方一字排开，腰间的步枪子弹上了膛。

"我给你们一分钟时间，"军官喊道。

然后他跳起来，来到两个法国人跟前，先抓住莫里梭的手臂把他拉到一边，低声说：

"快点！告诉我暗号！你的朋友不会知道你已经告诉我了。我会装作大发慈悲把你们放了的。"

莫里梭一言不发。

普鲁士人又把索瓦日先生拉到一边，说了同样的话。

索瓦日先生也一样，一言不发。

于是他们被推到一起，并排站着。

就在那时，莫里梭向草地上还在挣扎的那网鱼看了一眼，一刹那间，他心头一酸，忍不住泪水涌上眼眶。

他低声说道："永别了，索瓦日先生。"

"永别了，莫里梭先生。"

索瓦日先生回答。

他们互相握手。

"开枪！"军官大声下令。

十二支枪子儿立刻齐发。

索瓦日先生像一根木头直挺挺向前扑倒。莫里梭晃了几下，转了一圈，仰面朝天横倒在朋友的身边，血从胸口被子弹打穿的土衣窟窿涌出。

德国军官又下了几道命令。他的手下拿了几段绳子和几块大石头来绑在两只尸体的脚上，然后把尸体拖到了河边。

瓦勒良山上的炮声始终未停，现在山顶已堆积如山的浓烟笼罩。

两个士兵分别抓住莫里梭的头和脚，另外两个士兵以同样的方式抬起索瓦日先生，狠狠地来回挥动了几下尸体，然后抛到了河里。

军官仍然若无其事地看着这一切，低声说了一句：

"好了，下面是轮到鱼来做的事情了。"

他往回走向房屋，忽然注意到草地上的那兜鱼，提起来看了一眼，笑了，喊道：

"威廉！"

一个穿着白色围裙的士兵跑过来。普鲁士军官把两个渔夫钓到的鱼扔给他，又命令他说：

"趁这些小东西还活着把它们给我油炸了——马上！"

说完他又点着了烟斗。

## （续）

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

1. noun+verb+er/or (n.)

2. adjective+noun+ed (adj.)

3. verb+adverb (n.)

2. a-: to, at, in, on sth.

4.

1. Verb phrases

come upon, come across, set off, set up, set eyes on, set sth. ablaze, sit astride, go on doing sth., walk on, go off, run into, indulge in, call out, spit out, spin round, ring out, bellow out, tie up, get hold of, be taken up with, sort out problems, put an end to, take pity on, jump to one's feet, get one's hands on, keep an eye on, be bound to

2. Other phrases

under siege, in the grip of, at one's last gasp, at dawn, on fire, on the alert, on the fringe of, in agreement, on an empty stomach, time after time, every now and then, in a matter of, side by side, towards the close of the day, a strip of open ground, a mountain of, a plume of smoke, a jet of, a draft of fishes, some lengths of rope, a shrug of shoulders

5.

confirm mutual appreciation, take one's choice, give a sigh, give permission, give a start, give an order, commit atrocities, loot a village, demolish a city, declare war, spare one's life, form a line, make a good catch, make a proposition, light the pipe, keep a sharp lookout, ease the suffering, fly the fish, caress one's face, shed light

6.

1. And it was here that he met a tubby, jolly little man by the name of Sauvage. (para. 4)

Emphatic sentence pattern: it + be + emphasized parts + that/who-clause.

Other sentences from the text:

It was Monsieur Sauvage who caught the first fish. (para. 48)

It was then that Morissot happened to glance down at the net of fish which was lying in the grass ... (para. 85)

2. On spring mornings when the sun sent floating over the river that light mist which moves along with the current ... (para. 5)

Pay attention to the shift of word order. The normal word order should be "... the sun sent that light mist floating over the river." "floating over the river" is now put before "that light mist" in order to avoid the misunderstanding that the attributive clause "which moves along ..." modifies "the river" instead of "the mist."

3. ... feeling quite emotional over the fact that they had come across each other in such different circumstances. (para. 13)

Appositive clause: a noun clause that sits next to another noun in order to rename or describe it, usually introduced by "that"

Compare:

... feeling quite emotional over the fact that they had come across each other in such different circumstances. (appositive clause)

This is a fact that we take as common sense. (attributive clause)

Another case of appositive clause:

... scared by the feeling that somebody had been walking just behind them. (para. 65)

4. They had never so much as set eyes on them, but for four months now they had been aware of their presence on the outskirts of Paris, occupying part of France, looting, committing atrocities, reducing people to starvation ... (para. 38)

In a matter of seconds they were seized, tied up, hustled along, thrown into a boat and carried across to the island. (para. 66)

Parallelism: Words, phrases, and clauses that are similar in structure and perform the same function in a sentence are said to be parallel. Parallelism makes the reader aware of the similarity in ideas. If the ideas are not similar, the parallel structure may seem awkward.

Other cases of parallelism from the text:

1) Paris was under siege, in the grip of famine, at its last gasp. (para. 1)

2) And they scrambled down the slope of one of the vineyards, bent double, crawling on their hands and knees, taking advantage of the cover afforded by the vines, keeping their eyes wide open and their ears on the alert. (para. 43)

3) And as they talked, Mount Valérien went thundering on, demolishing French homes with its cannonades, pounding lives to dust, crushing human beings to pulp, putting an end to so many dreams, to so many long-awaited joys, tearing into the hearts of all those wives and daughters and mothers with pain and suffering that would never be eased. (para. 64)

5. There they go again. (para. 56)

"There" is used as an adverb here. It is usually placed at the beginning of a sentence, and should be stressed. Do not mix it up with the use of the word in "there is/are" structure, such as in "There's something worth remembering!" (para. 19).

The word "here" can be used in the same way.

# Unit 11

## Text A · Mr. Imagination · George Kent

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

This is a delightful biographical sketch of Jules Verne, a popular 19th-century French writer. Jules Verne is generally regarded as the father of science fiction because, first of all, he was "years ahead of the other visualizers of things to come", secondly, he is the most prolific science fiction writer, having put out more than 100 books in his lifetime. But perhaps more importantly, it is because he wrote about the marvels of tomorrow with such precise, indisputable details that he became a powerful stimulus and inspiration to later scientists.

It is perhaps no mere coincidence why science fiction and modern science and technology experienced their dramatic development more or less at the same time in history. In fact, when we talk about the impact of literature, we must include the powerful effect of science fiction on the imagination of human beings, especially when they are young. Einstein once said that he was no different from other children when he was young. The only difference was that he remained serious about his dreams when he grew up.

An interesting point about Jules Verne, if we can completely believe the author, is the fact that although his books seemed to show that he must have traveled all over the world to know so much about world geography, he must have been experienced in hunting and fishing to be so familiar with the two sports, and he must have been a scientist-turned man of letters to have so much scientific knowledge, he is actually a stay-at-home who has spent less than one of his seventy-seven years en voyage, and has never caught a fish or animal.

So what is his secret for success?

First, we should perhaps mention the fact that he was born on an island at a time when the new railroads and steamers brought back hides, spices, all kinds of curiosities, as well as fascinating stories of far-away places. This must have had a strong influence on Jules Verne as a little boy. Then there was the influence of Alexandre Dumas. But most of his knowledge came from his persistent readings. Without his readings, this stay-at-home could not have written those books.

A word of warning: Like many biographical stories, what the author has written here may not be accurate or even reliable in details.

#### 2 Structure

The introduction (paras. 1–8) provides a contrast between Jules Verne the science fiction writer and Jules Verne the man. The body part (paras. 9–31) deals with his early years, his success as a writer, and his major works. The essay concludes (paras. 32–34) with his last years, and the honor and popularity Jules received at his death.

I. Jules Verne the writer vs. Jules Verne the man (paras. 1–5)

1. One was a great traveler, the other, a stay-at-home. (paras. 1–3)

2. One was skillful in fishing and hunting, the other, a poor hunter and with no fishing experience. (para. 4)

3. One foresaw major 20th-century inventions, the other never used a test tube. (para. 5)

II. Jules Verne, father of science fiction (paras. 6–8)

1. He was years before other major science fiction writers. (para. 6)

2. His description of future marvels is scientifically precise and accurate. (para. 7)

3. He was matter-of-fact when many of his fancies came true. (para. 8)

III. His early years (paras. 9–18)

1. As a child, he loved adventure. (para. 9)

2. As a young man, he was interested in writing. (para. 10)

3. He made up his mind to become a writer after his encounter with Alexandre Dumas. (paras. 11–18)

IV. His life in Paris (paras. 19–22)

1. He lived a few lean years after his father cut off his allowance. (para. 19)

2. He remained a devoted and loving son. (para. 20)

3. He continued to write after he got married. (paras. 21–22)

V. His major works (paras. 23–31)

1. His first book—how he got it published and his second. (paras. 23–26)

2. His best-known book, Around the World in 80 Days—the sensation it created. (paras. 27–29)

3. 20,000 Leagues Under the Sea. (para. 30)

4. A few 20th-century wonders that he visualized in his books. (para. 31)

VI. His last years and his death (paras. 32–34)

1. Sneers and rumors upset him. (para. 32)

2. At his death he was honored and highly praised. (para. 33)

3. He was compared to Santa Claus. (para. 34)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1. Encourage students to read one or two books by Jules Verne.

2. Discuss the value of science fiction or the importance of imagination.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text A

1. Back in the 1880s, a big red-bearded man came to call one day on the French Minister of Education. (para. 1)

Note how the writer begins the essay. Instead of starting with Verne's birth or early days, he begins with an amusing anecdote about Verne when he was 52. This way, the writer succeeds in arousing the reader's interest immediately. By the 1880s Verne had already had his major books published and become a world-famous writer. Here are the years of publication of his major works:

Voyage to the Centre of the World (1864)

From the Earth to the Moon (1865)

20,000 Leagues Under the Sea (1869)

Around the World in 80 Days (1873)

Mysterious Island (1874)

call on sb.: to visit sb. Formally

2. The receptionist looked at the card and his face lighted up. (para. 1)

light up: to become cheerful

3. "Monsieur Verne," he said reverently, "pray be seated." (para. 1)

pray: a word used to add politeness to a command or request (an old-fashioned use)

be seated: (fml) to sit down

4. He had voyaged 60,000 miles under the sea, whizzed around the moon, hitchhiked on comets, explored the center of the earth, chatted with cannibals in Africa and Bushmen in Australia. (para. 2)

voyage: to travel to a place, esp. by ship; to make a long journey

Here the writer is referring to Verne's book 20,000 Leagues Under the Sea. One league is roughly 3 miles, hence, 20,000 leagues is roughly 60,000 miles.

whiz: (AmE, whizz in BrE) to move very quickly, often making a sound like sth. rushing through the air

5. There was very little of the world's geography that Jules Verne, the writer, had not visited. (para. 2)

Jules Verne had written about almost all corners of the world in his books.

6. Jules Verne, the man, was a stay-at-home. (para. 3)

Unlike the heroes in his novels, Jules Verne didn't travel very much. Here, the writer wants to say that he doesn't write from his own experiences, but uses his imagination, hence the title of the essay.

Stay-at-home: a person who prefers to remain quietly at home rather than to go out or to travel

7. For forty years, he sat in a small room of the red brick tower of his home, in Amiens, turning out, year in, year out, one book every six months—more than 100 altogether. (para. 3)

Amiens: a city in northern France. In 1888, Jules Verne was elected town councilor of Amiens, where he served for fifteen years and lived until his death in 1905.

turn out: to produce as the result of work

year in, year out: every year without changing, year after year without a break

Similar expression:

day in, day out / week in, week out: for many days/weeks, continuously over a long period of time

More examples:

He had been wearing the same old suit, day in, day out, for five years.

Ever since I grew up, I've weighed 50 kilos year in, year out.

8. His books are crowded with hunting and fishing expeditions, but Jules went hunting only once. (para. 4)

be crowded with: (of space or time) to be too full of

e.g. The office was crowded with people applying for a visa.

You've crowded your day with too many activities.

My mind was crowded with fragrant memories as I wrote these lines.

9. Then he raised his gun and—poof!—shot the red ribbon off the hat of a game warden. (para. 4)

He raised his gun and fired, but he was such a poor hunter that he missed his target and almost killed the game warden.

poof: interj. an exclamation used to express suddenness as of disappearance or appearance

game warden: a person employed to protect wildlife when hunting is not allowed or out of season

Note the touch of humor in this description of Verne's hunting and fishing expeditions.

10. The only fish he ever caught was on a plate at the end of a fork. (para. 4)

Besides eating from his fork at table, he had never caught any fish.

11. He had TV working before simple radio had been invented. (para. 5)

have sth./sb. doing sth.: to cause sth./sb. to do sth.

e.g. I had them all laughing at my jokes.

In a few years, they had their land producing good crops of corn and wheat.

12. There were, in fact, few twentieth-century wonders that this man did not foresee... (para. 5)

wonder: sth. that causes people to feel astonishment or great admiration; a miracle

foresee: to see or be aware of beforehand

13. ... he was years ahead of H. G. Wells, Conan Doyle, and the other great visualizers of things to come. (para. 6)

... he foresaw many future inventions years before other science fiction writers did.

14. He wrote about the marvels of tomorrow with such precise, indisputable detail that he was taken seriously. (para. 7)

He described the details of things to be invented so accurately and convincingly that people almost forgot that he was writing fiction.

take sb./sth. seriously: to treat sb./sth. as being important and deserving attention or respect

e.g. Don't take him seriously. He's only joking.

The town didn't take the flood warning seriously, and consequently suffered heavy casualties.

I always take your suggestions seriously.

I took the sun too seriously, and didn't take an umbrella with me this morning.

He was an earnest person who took life seriously.

15. Learned societies argued with him. (para. 7)

learned societies: organized groups of scholars

16. Mathematicians spent weeks checking his figures. (para. 7)

checking his figures: examining his figures to see if they were accurate

17. Verne, who lived to see many of his fancies come true, was matter-of-fact about it all. "What one man can imagine," he said, "another man can do." (para. 8)

Many of the things he imagined and described in his novels were invented before he died. But he didn't seem surprised at all. He said since one person can imagine a thing, it was only natural that another person can make it a reality.

matter-of-fact (about sth.): adj. not showing feelings or emotion, esp. in a situation when emotion would be expected

e.g. He sounded matter-of-fact and unemotional.

They spoke in a very matter-of-fact way about their heroic deeds.

18. ... his mother was descended from one of the great families of France. (para. 9)

be descended from sb./sth.: to be related to a person or group who lived a long time ago

e.g. She claims to be descended from Confucius.

Humans are descended from ape-like creatures.

19. ... signed on as a cabin boy. (para. 9)

sign on: to engage oneself for employment, be hired, esp. by a signed agreement

cabin boy: a boy whose work is to serve and run errands for the officers and passengers aboard a ship

20. Fortunately for his later admirers, a friend of the family saw him go on board and told the family. (para. 9)

A friend told his family what he had seen, so his father got him home before the ship left. If the friend hadn't done this, Jules Verne would have had an entirely different life and wouldn't have become a writer to be admired by so many people later.

21. At the bottom he slipped off and landed squarely on a stout gentleman about to ascend the stairs. (para. 11)

At the end of staircase he slipped off the banister and fell right on a stout gentleman who was going to climb the stairs.

land: to come down onto sth.

squarely: directly; exactly

22. You could have knocked Jules over with a breadstick. (para. 17)

You could have knocked Jules down with a feather. It means that Jules was greatly surprised to find who the man was.

knock sb. over: to knock sb. down; to hit or push sb. so that they fall onto the ground

breadstick: a crisp stick-shaped roll, often served with soup

23. Knowing Dumas confirmed young Verne's desire to be an author. (para. 18)

Knowing Dumas, he became firm in his desire to be a writer.

confirm: to make sb. more firm in a belief, an attitude, etc.

24. Jules, urged on by the older man, made up his mind he would do for geography what Dumas had done for history. (para. 18)

Earnestly persuaded by Alexandre, Jules decided to become a writer of travels and accomplish what Dumas had achieved by writing historical novels.

25. His father, impatient with the boy's neglect of his studies, cut off his allowance. (para. 19)

be impatient with: to have had enough, therefore not going to tolerate

neglect: failure to pay enough attention

cut off: to stop abruptly

26. ... but the years that followed were lean ones. (para. 19)

lean year: a year in which people do not have much food

27. "My stockings," he told a friend, "are like a spider-web in which a hippopotamus has been sleeping." (para. 19)

"My stockings are full of big holes," he said to a friend.

28. Though his father had deprived him of his allowance... (para. 20)

deprive sb. of sth.: to stop sb. enjoying or using sth.; to cause sb./sth. to be without (sth. needed)

More examples:

The hot sun deprived the flowers of water.

Many children are deprived of a good education, simply because they live in the wrong place.

The new laws in the country threaten to deprive many people of the most elementary freedom.

29. ... and rarely took a step without first seeking parental advice. (para. 20)

... and generally asked for his parents' advice before he made up his mind to do sth.

30. Cocky, good-looking, and "irresistible" to the ladies... (para. 21)

cocky: conceited; arrogant

irresistible: The ladies found him so attractive that they could not help loving him.

31. Fifteen publishers looked at it, sniffed and sent it back. (para. 23)

Note the words "looked at it" (instead of "read it") and "sniffed".

sniff: to inhale a short audible breath through the nose as in smelling sth. Here it is used to indicate ridicule and contempt

e.g. Such an offer is not to be sniffed at.

32. The publisher read the book through as the fidgeting young author waited. (para. 24)

Unlike the fifteen publishers who merely "looked at it", Pierre Hetzel "read the book through" while the young author, eager to know what the publisher would do with the manuscripts, waited restlessly and nervously.

Pierre Hetzel: one of the most important French publishers of the 19th century, who also published other famous French writers' works, such as those of Victor Hugo. Hetzel helped improve Verne's writings, which until then had been repeatedly rejected by other publishers. The two of them formed an excellent writer-publisher team until Hetzel's death.

33. Hetzel said he would publish it if Jules would rewrite it in the form of a novel. (para. 24)

Originally, the book is a story about a balloon exploration of Africa, which had been rejected by other publishers for being "too scientific". Acting on Hetzel's advice, Verne rewrote the story, by changing the sad ending into a happy one, and toning down various political messages.

34. His next book, Journey to the Centre of the Earth, started his characters off down the crater of a volcano in Iceland. (para. 26)

start sb. off: to cause sb. to go on a journey

crater: the bowl-shaped mouth of a volcano

35. ... and almost missed connections on her account. (para. 28)

... and almost failed to catch the next train or ship because of her...

connection: a train, bus, etc. onto which one changes from another in the course of a journey

e.g. As the local train was late, I missed the connection to London.

on one's account: because of sb. or for sb.'s sake

e.g. Don't give up this opportunity on my account.

36. Verne artfully kept this popular interest alive... (para. 28)

keep sth. alive: to keep sth. in existence or continuing

e.g. The media kept public interest in the scandal alive. Keep your dream alive in spite of the difficulties.

37. ... he was attacked by Red Indians, and arrived in New York to see the ship that was to take him to England only a small speck on the horizon. (para. 28)

Red Indian: Native American, now considered offensive

to see the ship... only a small speck on the horizon: This is an infinitive of result. The phrase means that when Fogg and his party arrived at the wharf, the transatlantic steamer had left and they could only see a small speck on the sea.

speck: a small spot

horizon: the line at which the earth and the sky seem to meet

38. ... and had his hero charter a vessel. (para. 29)

charter: to hire for exclusive use

vessel: a relatively large ship

39. As the world held its breath, Fogg reached London with only minutes to spare, and won his bet. (para. 29)

Note: This is not accurate. For the benefit of those who haven't read Round the World in 80 Days, the following details might be helpful. On Wednesday, the 2nd of October, 1872, Phileas Fogg bet five friends twenty thousand pounds that he would make a tour of the world in eighty days or less. He told them that he would be back to the Reform Club, on Saturday, the 21st of December at a quarter to nine p.m. While traveling eastward, he had gained one day and reached London at ten to nine a day before, on Friday. But Fogg thought it was Saturday and that he lost the bet. When he realized his mistake, he hurried to the Reform Club and reached the room where his five friends were waiting with only a couple of seconds to spare.

time/money to spare: time/money left over

e.g. They arrived at the railway station with 10 minutes to spare.

After paying the rent and utility bills, the family has enough money to spare.

40. Simon Lake, father of the modern submarine, credits Verne with giving him virtually a blueprint for his invention. (para. 30)

credit sb. with: to believe or admit that sb. has done sth. good

e.g. She credits her success to her parents' support.

The patient credits his recovery to herb medicine.

Jim is credited with writing the first draft of the play.

The invention of paper is credited to a man called Cai Lun in the Eastern Han Dynasty.

virtually: almost; very nearly

e.g. He was virtually penniless.

They were virtually cut off from the town by snow.

Local production virtually eliminates transport costs.

41. The people of his fancy made diamonds synthetically, developed a convertible automobile-ship-helicopter-plane, and fired glass bullets containing an electric spark instantly fatal. (para. 31)

the people of his fancy: people of his imagination, the characters he created in his books

instantly fatal: causing death immediately

42. Intellectual circles sneered at him. (para. 32)

sneer at: to express scorn or contempt in speech or writing

43. Gossips said there was no such man as Jules Verne. (para. 32)

Note the structure "no such... as"

e.g. There's no such thing as ghosts.

There's no such thing as a job for life.

Americans believe there's no such thing as friendship between countries. It is the national interests that combine or divide nations.

44. The Russians claimed him as a Slav, a Pole and former expionage agent who had taken to letters. (para. 32)

claim sb./sth. as sth.: to state that sth. is true even though it has not been proved

e.g. They claim their company as the largest in the country.

Both countries claim the islands as theirs.

who had taken to letters: who had begun to devote himself to literature

Similarly:

take to doing sth.: to start doing sth. regularly

e.g. The man took to drinking after he lost his job.

45. To his colleagues he wired: "There is no Jules Verne and Company—there is only Jules." (para. 32)

He sent a telegram to his colleagues, saying that the person named Jules Verne did exist and he did not work with any other people as a group.

company: other people working with him as partners

46. The old story teller is dead. It is like the passing of Santa Claus. (para. 34)

Jules Verne has entertained the world with his wonderful stories in the same way as Santa Claus entertains children with gifts, and his death is a loss, which is as great as the passing of Santa Claus would be.

the passing of sth.: the fact that sth. ends

### III Translation of Text A

幻想大师

乔治·肯特

回溯到19世纪80年代，一位身材高大、留有红胡须的男士前去拜访法国教育部长。接待员看了他的名片，顿时高兴起来。"凡尔纳先生，"他毕恭毕敬地说，"请坐，您这样到处遨游，肯定累了吧！"

按说儒勒·凡尔纳是该筋疲力尽了，他环球旅行100次，或更多——其中一次是用了80天，他在海底遨游了六万英里，闪电式环绕月球一周，搭乘过彗星，探索过地心，还与非洲的食人族以及澳洲的丛林人聊过天。地球上几乎没有作家儒勒·凡尔纳未造访过的地方。

儒勒·凡尔纳本人却是位不爱出门的人。他要是累了，也只可能是因为写

## （续）

作时间长了手腕僵直了。在40年的时光里，他坐在他家位于亚眠市的红砖塔顶的小屋里，年复一年地出书，每六个月一本，共一百多部。凡尔纳本人到过欧洲和北非，在纽约观光过六周，仅此而已。这位世界上最非凡的旅行家，在他77年的生涯中用于游历的时间还不到一年。

他的作品描写了无数狩猎和垂钓旅行，可是儒勒仅有过一次狩猎经历：他举起了猎枪——啪的一下，却击中了野生动物监察员帽子上的红丝带。他一生中唯一捕获的鱼是他用叉子叉在盘子里拿到的。

尽管儒勒·凡尔纳的手从未拿过试管，他却成为实验室里科学家灵感的来源。在简易收音机发明之前，他就让电视机出现了；他比莱特兄弟早半个世纪就想出了直升机。事实上，20世纪那些奇妙的发明，没有几件是他没有预见到的：霓虹灯、空调、摩天大楼、导弹、坦克、潜水艇、飞机等等。

毫无疑问，儒勒是科幻文学之父；他比 H.G. 威尔士、柯南道尔以及其他伟大的预见家出道都要早。

他不仅是娱乐读者，他对未来发明的细节描述如此精确、无可争议，以至于人们都很认真对待他。学术机构与他辩论；数学家们花好几个星期来核实他书中的数字。他那部去月球旅行的书出版时，竟有500人志愿参加下一次的月球之旅。法国著名的马歇尔·利奥泰元帅有一次说，现代科学简直就是在现实中逐个实现儒勒·凡尔纳的文字中所预见的种种发明。

凡尔纳有生之年目睹了自己许多幻想成为现实，但他对此事却反应淡然。"一个人能想象的事情，"他说，"另一个人就能做到。"

儒勒的父亲是位律师，母亲出身法国名门。他们的儿子于1828年诞生在南特附近的菲多岛。那时拿破仑已经去世，威灵顿是英国首相。第一条铁路才建成五年，汽船已经横越大西洋，不过仍然要用船帆来补充蒸汽机的动力。少年儒勒在自家的窗口能看到帆船的桅杆，观察渔民晾晒的渔网，闻到从商船飘来的皮革和香料的气味。11岁的时候，有一天他和一个伙伴在码头玩耍，小姑娘说她想要一串红珊瑚珠链，而且是船员们从海外带回来的那种。儒勒郑重地答应给她弄一串。就在当天下午，他上了一艘即将驶往印度的船，签约当一名船舶服务员。他家的一个朋友看到他上了船，并且告诉了他的家人。这对他后来的崇拜者来说，真是一件幸运的事情。他父亲把他带回了家，狠狠地揍了他一顿，然后叫他上床睡觉。

18岁那年，儒勒到巴黎去学法律，可是他对诗歌和剧本创作更感兴趣。

一天晚上，他出席了一场时尚的聚会。他感到十分无聊，悄然离开，兴高采烈地从楼梯的栏杆滑了下去，和一位正要上楼的壮实绅士撞了个满怀。他不假思索地问道，"先生，您吃过晚饭了吗？"

那人回答说吃过了，他吃了一份南特风味的煎鸡蛋。

对此凡尔纳反驳说，"呸，在巴黎没有人会做正宗的南特煎蛋。"

"你会吗？"那人问道。

"当然，我是南特人。"儒勒说。

"很好，下周三来吃晚饭，做正宗的煎鸡蛋。"

说着，他递给那年轻人他的名片，接着上了楼梯。原来儒勒邂逅的是亚历山大·仲马，《三个火枪手》的作者。此刻你真能用根面包棍把儒勒打翻在地。

与大仲马的结识坚定了青年凡尔纳成为作家的理想。在忘年交的激励下，他决心在地域和空间上发展大仲马在岁月时空方面所取得的成就，于是他开始忘情地读书、写作，而忘却了他来巴黎的初衷。

儒勒的父亲对儿子忽视法律学习感到不满，不再给他提供生活费。儒勒在一家剧院找了份无足轻重的工作。此后他过了几年缺衣少食的日子。他给母亲写信说，"我吃的牛排前几天还在巴黎大街上拉车呢！"一次由于他用买食物的钱买了一套莎士比亚的作品，他一连三天只能用干吞充饥。他的衣衫破旧，不能御寒，他对一位朋友说，"我的袜子像是蜘蛛网，里面睡了一只河马！"

尽管那段时间父亲断了儒勒的生活费，但他一直是个忠诚、孝顺的儿子，即使中年后，他仍然经常去信，与父母探讨他的书、他的计划、他的理想，极少有不征求父母意见就行事的情况。正是这强烈的家庭观念使他能够在寻欢作乐、追求享受的巴黎仍然去教堂，坚持自己的宗教、甚至清教主义的信仰。

自负、帅气、魅力让女人难以"抵御"的儒勒迅速坠入爱河，但凡尔纳先生冷静了下来。后来他又坠入爱河，这次他娶了所爱的人——一位有两个孩子的漂亮寡妇。

在父亲的帮助下，他成了股票经纪人，境况得以改善，不过他仍然住在阁楼里，在那里写作。

儒勒的第一本书是《气球上的五星期》。书稿前后送到了15个出版商手上，他们翻了翻，不感兴趣，都退了回去。愤怒之下儒勒将书稿扔进了火炉。他妻子把它抢救了出来，说服他再试一次。儒勒腋下夹着轻微烧焦的书稿去见皮埃尔·埃泽尔。

在年轻作者焦急不安的等待下，埃泽尔快速翻阅了书稿。他说如果儒勒将此书改写成小说，他就会出版。

两周后，儒勒拿来了修改后的书稿。《气球上的五星期》成了畅销书，并译成了各种语言。1862年，年仅34岁的作者一举成名。

在下一部小说《地心游记》里，他让书里的人物沿着冰岛的一个火山口进入地球内，经历了上千次的冒险活动，最终顺着意大利火山岩浆流，滑出地心。

或许，凡尔纳最为著名的作品是《八十天环游地球记》。此书最初在巴黎的《时代报》连载，书中主人公的行程引起世人极大的兴趣，纽约和伦敦各报社驻巴黎的记者，每天都给各自的报社发电，报告这位虚构的斐利亚·福克的行程。

在欧洲各国，人们纷纷就福克是否能及时回到伦敦、赢得他的赌注而打赌。凡尔纳巧妙地将这种广泛的关注保持到底：他的主人公将一位印度寡妇从死神手中营救出来，爱上了她，几次因她几乎错过了行程中的火车或轮船；在横穿美国大平原时，他遭到印第安人的袭击，等他赶到纽约时，却看到他预定去英国的轮船已经远去，成了地平线上的一个小点儿。

所有的跨洋轮船公司都表示如果凡尔纳让斐利亚上他们的船，他会得到一大笔钱。作者对此全部谢绝，而让他的主人公租用了一艘船。正当全世界都屏住呼吸时，斐利亚仅提前几分钟到达伦敦，赢得了赌注。

在《海底两万里》中凡尔纳构想出了一种潜水艇，此艇不仅有双船体和电力推进器，还能利用海水发电。现代潜水艇之父西蒙·莱克说，凡尔纳几乎为他的发明勾画出了蓝图。

阅读凡尔纳的科幻小说，人们难以相信这些书写于一百多年之前，他想象中的人物生产出了人造钻石，开发出汽车、轮船、直升机和飞机可相互转换的四合一的交通工具，发射能产生电火花并能立即致命的玻璃子弹。

儒勒·凡尔纳的最后几年并不愉快，知识界蔑视他，他没有当选为法国科学院的院士。流言蜚语盛行，说根本没有儒勒·凡尔纳其人：俄国人声称他是个斯拉夫人，是一个波兰人，曾经是个间谍，后来才从事文学创作。意大利的知识界说，儒勒·凡尔纳是一帮法国科学家共用的笔名，还派遣了一位作家到法国去寻找证据。此人查阅了儒勒的手稿，完全信服地离开了法国。这位作家发电报给同事说，"不存在什么儒勒·凡尔纳一帮人，只有儒勒一人。"

儒勒·凡尔纳于1905年离世。来自世界各地的人出席了他的葬礼，其中包括所有那些曾经蔑视他以及传播过流言蜚语的人，还有法国科学院那 30 名院士、外交使团以及一些国王和总统的特使。

在他逝世时所发表的无数赞扬之词中，假如儒勒·凡尔纳在天有灵，他会最喜爱一家巴黎报纸上的两句话："讲故事的老人已经去世，他的仙逝宛如圣诞老人离开了我们。"

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1.

Some words are formed by adding affixes, and sometimes more than one:

prefixes: in-, ir-, fore-, re-, sub-, trans-

suffixes: -able, -ible, -ing

Some words are derivatives from the same word, for example, "ascend" and "descend" both come from the Latin verb scandere (to climb), but their prefixes make the difference: "a-" means "up," while "de-" means "down." Similarly, "convert," "avert," "revert," "divert," "pervert" share the same Latin root "vert," which means "turn." Some words are compound words made by joining a phrase or a group of words together with hyphens.

2.

1. Verb phrases

call on sb., argue with sb., arrive in, be bored with, be crowded with, be descended from, be impatient with, be irresistible to, be worn out, credit sb. with sth., cut off, deprive sb. of sth., fling sth. into the fire, knock sb. over, light up, make bets on, rescue sb. from, retort to sb., sail for, sign on as, slide down, slip off, sneer at sb., take to sth., tuck sth. under one's arm, urge sb. on, work out, turn out, blurt out

2. Prepositional phrases

ahead of, at the age of, beyond any doubt, in a rage, in the form of, on board a boat, on sb.'s account, on the horizon, on the wharf, on voyage, in the style of

3. Other phrases

a set of Shakespeare; year in, year out; a string of

3.

arouse much interest, ascend the stairs, carry sail, charter a vessel, confirm sb.'s desire, cross the Atlantic, develop a submarine, do much traveling, fire bullets, make a bet, make visits, manufacture electricity, invent the radio, foresee/visualize wonders, miss connections, neglect one's studies, obtain a job, publish a book, report sb.'s whereabouts, rewrite the story, seek sb.'s advice, supplement the engines, win one's bet, hold a test tube, hold one's breath, explore the center of the earth, raise one's gun, utter the words

4.

1. With all the traveling you do, you must be tired. (para. 1)

"with" phrase used as adverbial of reason, and modal verb "must" used to express possibility

2. Knowing Dumas confirmed young Verne's desire to be an author. (para. 18)

Gerund used as subject and infinitive used as attributive modifier

3. Jules ... made up his mind he would do for geography what Dumas had done for history. (para. 18)

Analogy; what clause used as object

4. ... His hero rescued an Indian widow from death ... and arrived in New York to see the ship that was to take him to England only a small speck on the horizon. (para. 28)

Infinitive used as adverbial of result, suggesting an unexpected or unfavourable result

5. He was not elected a member of the French Academy. (para. 32)

Passive form of verb + object + compliment pattern. More examples of the same pattern from the text can be found in the grammar exercise.

6. Of all the thousands of words of praise uttered at his death, Jules Verne would have liked best these two sentences ... (para. 34)

Subjunctive mood expressing possibility

#### Reading Comprehension

1.

(1) great traveller

(2) stay-at-home

(3) major 20th-century inventions/wonders

(4) railroads and steamers

(5) confirmed his desire to be a writer

(6) lean years

(7) a best seller

(8) more than 100 novels

(9) held its breath

(10) make it back to London in time to win the bet

(11) sneers and gossips

2.

1. Many of the things he imagined and described in his novels were invented before he died. But he was not surprised at all. He simply said that since one person can imagine a thing, it is only natural that another person can make it a reality. He said this in a matter-of-fact way.

2. You could have knocked Jules down/over with a breadstick or feather. It is a humorous way of saying that Jules was so surprised to find who the man was that he almost fainted.

3. Encouraged by Alexandre, Jules decided to turn geography into novels the way Dumas turned history into novels.

4. "My stockings are full of huge holes," he said to a friend.

5. He sent a telegram to his colleagues, saying that the person named Jules Verne did exist and he did not work with other people as a group.

#### Language Exercises

1.

1.

1. re- (meaning: back): react; recover; recall; rebound; recycle; repay; return; reject; remind; replace; renew; rewind; retort; reverberate; respond

2. re- (meaning: again): revisit; rearm; rearrange; readjust; rebuild; reassure; reawaken; reconsider; reform; reconstruct; review; reproduce; reorganize; rethink; reprint; remarry; retell

2.

1) ... a very learned (博学的) man. (-ed should be pronounced as "id")

... have learned (学习，完成式) the language. (-ed is pronounced as "-d")

2) ... that blessed (幸运的) morning ... (-ed should be pronounced as "id")

... her father finally blessed (为之祈求神的保佑，完成式) her marriage ... (-ed is pronounced as "-d")

3) ... an aged (年迈的) farmer. (-ed should be pronounced as "id")

... he seemed to have aged (变老，完成式) beyond recognition. (-ed is pronounced as "-d")

4) ... their beloved (受人爱戴的) leader... (-ed should be pronounced as "id")

Beloved (被爱，完成式) by young people ... (-ed is pronounced as "-d")

5) ... a queer ragged (衣衫褴褛的) millionaire. (-ed should be pronounced as "id")

3.

1) publish a book

2) cross the Atlantic

3) supplement his income

4) ascend/mount the stairs

5) confirm his desire

6) neglect/ignore one's studies

7) obtain/get a job

8) seek sb.'s advice

9) rewrite the story

10) arouse much interest

11) report sb.'s whereabouts

12) miss connections

13) charter/rent a boat

14) win a bet

15) develop/invent a submarine

16) fling/toss/throw/pitch a stone

4.

1) respectfully

2) wonders

3) exact/accurate

4) unquestionable

5) examine

6) numbers/statistics

7) foresee/envision

8) dreams

9) beloved/lover

10) luckily

11) bring back/retrieve

12) suddenly

13) encourage

14) seldom

15) look for

16) at once/immediately

17) good-looking

18) restless/uneasy

19) skillfully/cleverly

20) put

21) drive forward

22) practically

23) artificially

24) deadly

2.

1. She had a son who was handicapped. So she often had to ask to leave early on his account. But gradually she urged him to learn to take care of himself.

2. Jack London was a sailor for quite a few years before he quit the job and began to take to letters.

3. The affordable prices and fine quality made the products we turn out popular in Africa.

4. Although she had retired, many people still came to seek her advice when they came across difficulties in their work.

5. Before the bridge was built the kids living in that area were practically deprived of the right to have a good education.

6. He slipped onto a ship with only five minutes to spare, without a ticket and without a single penny in his pocket.

7. I'm tired of working at that factory manufacturing year in, year out things that do such serious damage to people's health.

8. His father was a famous space scientist. But his dream is to explore the secrets of deep sea.

9. Three years ago, she was made general manager of the factory, and she is now having the factory making profits.

10. The opportunity was so inviting that it was almost irresistible. Many people therefore offered to go on the voyage.

11. I think China is about to put a man on the moon soon. Our dream will soon come true.

3.

1. on

2. With; up

3. Beyond/Without; with

4. from

5. with

6. According; to; in; into; on; from

7. In; on; with; on; out; but

4.

Example 1:

Pattern of complex object: verb + object + noun

Note: Apart from the verbs in bold type above, the verbs listed below can also be used this way:

choose, call, name, nickname, nominate, crown, designate, etc.

1. 我们觉得她是个启发能力强的教授。

2. 第二次大陆会议任命乔治·华盛顿为大陆军总司令。

3. 他在路边捡到了这个小乞丐，最后让小乞丐成为了他事业的继承人。

Example 2:

Pattern of complex object: verb + object + to-infinitive

Note: Apart from the verbs in bold type above, the verbs listed below can also be used this way:

instruct, cause, advise, allow (permit), ask, beg, call, challenge, choose, encourage, expect, force, get, hate, help, intend, invite, lead, leave, like, order, persuade, prefer, press (urge), remind, teach, tell, tempt, warn, etc.

4. 凡尔纳的父亲想让他学习法律。

5. 全球舆论强烈希望冲突双方停火。

6. 在我认识的所有人中，她是性格最温和的。我从未听过她发脾气。

Example 3:

Pattern of complex object: verb + object + (to) infinitive

Note: Apart from the verbs in bold type above, the verbs listed below can also be used this way:

make, hear, feel, help, listen to, look at, notice, observe, perceive, etc.

7. 我们不能让任何一家公司主宰市场。

8. 看到自己种的树一棵棵死去，他颇为伤心，但是他拒绝放弃。

9. 我们的学生未来会成为翻译。我们应该让学生学会如何应对跨文化沟通。

Example 4:

Pattern of complex object: verb + object + present participle

Note: Apart from the verbs in bold type above, the verbs listed below can also be used this way:

watch, catch, feel, hear, leave, listen to, mind, notice, overhear, set, etc.

10. 用户发现那家公司在广告中欺诈。

11. 他看到一个老头儿坐在渭河边。老头儿手里拿着钓鱼竿，鱼线离水3尺，也没有鱼钩。

12. 我们知道必须保持火堆燃烧，以保证群狼不会靠近。

5.

1. We hope that there will not be any misunderstanding between us.

2. I'd like you to meet a good friend of mine. You'll find that he is an extremely interesting person.

3. I consider that it is possible to recite all the texts. It's merely a matter of time. But I think that it is not a good idea to do that.

4. The boy at first did not understand why his father found that it was necessary to punish him by sending him to school.

6.

1. The staff were instructed by the sales manager to respond to customer complaints promptly.

2. People have recently been caught pushing drugs among school kids in the neighborhood by the police.

3. A year later, the patient was discovered by the doctor to have completely recovered without taking any medicine.

4. It is proven true by their study that many students feel it stressful to have more examinations.

5. Our students are meant to solve these problems on their own.

6. More people worldwide may be caused by the extremely bad weather conditions to suffer hunger.

7. I have been asked to take care of the dog during her absence, and to feed and walk it every day.

8. A group of youngsters were noticed regularly hanging around the bank by one of the guards. He reported it to the manager.

7.

1. He could have overslept. He didn't go to bed till 2 this morning.

2. What a pity. The money should have been used to build two or three primary schools in the countryside.

3. I guess he must have witnessed the accident. The clash of the two cars occurred right in front of his shop.

4. Yes, I think they must have dropped to the bottom of the sea because of terrible weather conditions.

If that was the case, people would have found some traces by now.

There are people who think they may have been seized by the aliens.

5. The enemy must have decided to abandon this city.

But why should they have abandoned the city? After all, it is the last defense line for them.

Perhaps they might have tried to lay a trap for us?

No, I don't think so. They wouldn't have had time to plan a trap.

1. She claimed to be descended from one of the oldest families in the United States.

2. This experiment aroused great interest among the people. They all came to watch as Galileo ascended the stairs calmly, and when he got to the top of the tower, dropped the two shots to the ground.

3. Until 1920, women in America were still deprived of the right to vote.

4. Historians usually credit the First Emperor of the Qin Dynasty with unifying the whole country for the first time in Chinese history.

6. With all my social activities, I am really exhausted. What's more, they are making me neglect my studies.
